# Changelog

Each release's section here becomes its GitHub release notes (`.github/workflows/release.yml`; `release-notes.yml` re-syncs the notes of an existing release). Benchmark figures are tokens per finished task (code written plus errors read, retries scored as patches), from [bench/LEADERBOARD.md](bench/LEADERBOARD.md). An entry for work by an outside contributor ends with its PR and `thanks @handle`; the release notes then list everyone thanked, and `python3 tools/credits.py` checks before a release that no one is missing.

## Unreleased

## v0.28.3 (19 Nov 2026)

- Docs: Brevi is now pitched as the verified native runtime for agent-written Python. New [Quickstart for agents](https://brevi-lang.org/docs/quickstart) (install, write `main.py`, read the one-line JSON error, fix it, `brevi run --verify`), a runnable end-to-end demo in `examples/agent-loop/` (check, verify, a visible refusal, MCP, timing against CPython), and the install page now names the C linker `rustc` needs and the CPython 3.13 `--verify` compares against.
- CI: `install-test` workflow (by hand and on each release) runs the documented install on a bare Ubuntu container, macOS, Windows x64 and Windows ARM64, then the agent-loop demo.
- Security (v0.28.3): a sandboxed program can no longer plant code in Brevi's build cache for a later run to execute. `--allow-write` (and MCP `allow_write`) now refuses a folder that holds or lies inside the build cache, Brevi's temp folder or `BREVI_PY_CACHE`, since later runs replay binaries and link runtime libraries from there with no check. A cache folder another user owns or can write is no longer used (Brevi says so and uses a private one). The sandbox image no longer has a world-writable shared cache: each user has their own, and the runtime libraries come prebuilt from a read-only, root-owned seed (`BREVI_CACHE_SEED=/opt/brevi-seed`).
- Security (v0.28.3): crate builds no longer use a cargo home another user can change. cargo runs what its home holds (`config.toml` can name a rustc wrapper; `registry/src` keeps crate sources it compiles without checking them again), and the sandbox image set `CARGO_HOME` to a folder every user could write, so one user could run code in another's native build. Brevi now uses `CARGO_HOME` (else `~/.cargo`) only if root or the user owns it and everything directly in it and no other user can write them, and otherwise says so and uses a private one. cargo also runs in Brevi's private build folder rather than the caller's, so a `.cargo/config.toml` left in a shared folder such as `/workspace` or `/tmp` is never read. The image no longer sets `CARGO_HOME`: each user's crates live in their own `~/.cargo`.
- Dynamic builds take class keyword arguments (`class C(Base, flavor="x")`): they go to the base's `__init_subclass__`, to a metaclass's `__prepare__`, `__new__` and `__init__`, and `boundary=` sets an Enum Flag's `_boundary_`, with CONFORM and EJECT now handled as CPython's `Flag._missing_` handles them. Keywords that no `__init_subclass__` takes raise CPython's TypeError. Fixed along the way: an IntFlag with KEEP wrapped some negative values wrong (`A(-8)` gave 0 where CPython gives 8), and `from enum import STRICT` before the first use of `Flag` made `Flag._boundary_ is STRICT` false. Still refused: an operator between members of two different IntFlag classes when a bit of the result is not a single-bit member of both classes (CPython keeps the other class's member in the result), and a Flag whose `__new__` makes its values with `<<` or `**` (values past 2**62).
- The conformance scorer now cuts out 9 more CPython tests it used to skip as not extractable: `Enum._convert_` tests get the module constants their filter reads, an `assertRaises` block written in a class body no longer adds a name to the class, test_binop may pass the `unittest` module as a plain value, and one dataclasses pickle test compares with `==` where CPython itself cannot print the repr. test_binop's constructor test now matches; the other 8 are refused by both builds, which shows their real blocker. Two test_patma tests are excluded with their reasons (one reads CPython bytecode with `dis`, one hunts reference leaks in CPython's compiler).
- Conformance: the three threaded `functools.lru_cache` tests (`TestLRUC.test_lru_cache_threaded`, `threaded2`, `threaded3`) are always classed unstable, because CPython's threads print at the same time and the OS scheduler orders (and can split) their lines. `threaded3` kept flipping between matched and unstable and failed the stdlib floor by one. Both builds still run them, so a crash, a wrong exit code or a refusal still counts; the Linux stdlib floor drops by the 2 tests that had matched (1989/1991 to 1987/1989 native), the Windows stdlib floor by 1 (only `threaded2` had matched there), and `threaded` (refused in both builds) now counts as not matched instead of being left out.
- Crate lines are checked before anything reaches Cargo.toml: `use NAME "VERSION" "FEATURES"` needs a Cargo version requirement (`1`, `0.10`, `=1.2.3`, `>=1, <2`, `1.0.0-rc.1`) and Cargo feature names (`std`, `serde/derive`), and under `--sandbox` a crate with no version (which would build whatever crates.io serves that day) is refused, including one the rust code only names (`sha2::Sha256` with no `use sha2 "VERSION"` line).
- `brevi mcp --http`: after five wrong tokens in a row, an address (an IPv6 /64 counts as one) gets 429 for 1 s, then 2, 4, up to 60 s, without its token being checked; a right token clears the count. A server without a token (127.0.0.1 only) answers only local Host names (localhost, 127.0.0.1, [::1]) unless `--allow-host HOST` names another, so a page that rebinds its own name to 127.0.0.1 cannot reach it.
- `tools/ds_contrib.py` holds its spend cap as a hard stop. `--budget` is at most $2 a run. Each call is priced at its worst case (all prompt tokens uncached, a prompt bound from the request's size, every allowed output token, peak prices if peak could begin during the call) before it is sent, and its output allowance shrinks to fit, or the run stops. Replies are charged from the API's usage at the peak or off-peak price in force; a reply whose usage can't be read stops the run, and an attempt that may have been billed without a reply is charged its worst case. A lane gets its first run and one resume; a third run is refused, counted in a ledger on shared storage, and the run refuses to start when that ledger can't be read or written. `tests/ds_contrib_test.py` checks all of it against a fake API.
- Dynamic builds run enum's pickle helpers: `enum.pickle_by_global_name` (also `_reduce_ex_by_global_name`) and `enum.pickle_by_enum_name` set as an Enum's `__reduce_ex__`, and `enum._make_class_unpicklable`.
- Fixed in dynamic builds: the class of an exception a Python library raised (`type(e)` of a `pickle.PicklingError`) is now the library's class for `is`, `==`, `hash`, `issubclass` (both ways), `__mro__` and `__bases__`, and calling it makes that exception. Before, `issubclass(type(e), pickle.PicklingError)` and `type(e) is pickle.PicklingError` answered False, and `issubclass(pickle.PicklingError, Exception)` raised TypeError.
- Dynamic builds: `sys._getframe(n).f_globals.get('__warningregistry__')` (test.support's `check_warnings`) is built. The frame is never made: n must be a literal, the frame may only be read that way, the depth is checked against the program's own frames, and the key is answered only while no module has a warning registry; anything else is still refused. `sys.modules['name']` reads a module the one-file program imports at its top, whatever CPython loaded at startup. Core conformance: +22 (test_set mixed str/bytes tests).
- Dynamic builds store module globals through `globals()` by a key known only at run time (`g = globals(); g['NAME_%s' % s] = v`), read them back by key, and run `Enum._convert_` (with a filter lambda that only reads the name) and `enum.global_enum`. A store that would rebind a def, class or import of the file, or add a name the program reads by name, stops the program instead of giving a different answer.
- Dynamic builds: `inspect.signature` now builds for the program's own defs, lambdas, wrappers and bound methods, the `__init__` a dataclass writes, and `dataclasses.dataclass` itself, as `Signature.from_callable` already did. A Signature now has `parameters` (a mappingproxy over an OrderedDict of `inspect.Parameter`, the same one on every read) and `return_annotation`; a Parameter has `name`, `default`, `annotation` and `kind` (an `inspect._ParameterKind` IntEnum), and `inspect.Parameter.empty` and its five kinds are there. A dataclass's `__init__` now has CPython's `__annotations__`. Constructing a Signature or a Parameter builds with CPython's inspect as before, and everything else a Signature or Parameter does not have stops the program. Fixed: a dataclass with a field named `self` stored the instance in that field (its `__init__` now names its first parameter `__dataclass_self__`, as CPython does). Three more CPython dataclasses tests match.
- Dynamic builds: hex(), oct(), bin(), `%x`/`%o`, range() and round() accept int subclasses and objects with `__index__` as CPython does (they raised TypeError).
- Dynamic builds: fixed silent wrong answers: auto() inside a namedtuple (or other tuple subclass) Enum value now gets its value; a pickled Enum member with a namedtuple mixin loads as the member itself; `getattr is getattr` (and every other builtin function) is True; a class attribute named `__name__` (such as a property for the instances) no longer hides the class's name, and assigning `C.__name__` / `C.__qualname__` works; `@builtins.property` makes a working property.
- Dynamic builds: builtin functions such as getattr pickle as CPython pickles them and load back as the program's own.
- Dynamic builds: a `unittest.mock` Mock or MagicMock stored on a class of the program (`D.__set__ = Mock()`, `D.__set_name__ = Mock()`, or under a plain name) now builds. The program's objects go into the mock sealed and come back as themselves (`side_effect`, `return_value`); `__set__`, `__delete__` and `__set_name__` mocks are called without the descriptor, as CPython's slots call them. Reading `call_args` and the other attributes that show what a mock was called with is still refused in such a program, a mock stored under another special name stops the program, and Python using a sealed object any other way stops it. Five more CPython dataclasses tests match.
- **Speed figures say how few programs they cover.** Every page, package README, agent skill and MCP text that quotes the typed speed median now says that only a small share of programs take the typed build, and `tools/published_numbers.py --check` refuses one that doesn't, as well as a core share rounded to a whole percent without its scope. Conformance reports now print the coverage with "of the scored suite, with N string-as-code tests excluded".
- Fixed: the result cache did not key on `OPENBLAS_CORETYPE` or the CPU's numpy BLAS kernels, so a numpy program built natively and then run again with `OPENBLAS_CORETYPE` naming other kernels replayed the native build (printing the modelled kernels' last digits instead of numpy's under that setting) rather than going to CPython. Both now choose the cache entry.
- Fixed a wrong answer: on ARM64 (Linux, macOS, Windows) and on x86-64 CPUs without AVX2 and FMA, Brevi's own numpy printed other last digits than numpy for float `@`, `dot`, `matmul`, `inner`, `vdot`, `cov`, `corrcoef`, `polyfit` and `numpy.linalg` (seen in `lstsq`, #612). Those programs now run in CPython there, as they do when `OPENBLAS_CORETYPE` names other kernels than the CPU's own, and a binary moved to such a CPU raises NotImplementedError for them.
- Fixed a wrong answer: `np.linalg.norm` of a float32 array could differ from numpy in the last digit; it now follows numpy's `sqrt(x.dot(x))` (OpenBLAS sdot).
- Fixed: `NPY_DISABLE_CPU_FEATURES` and `NPY_ENABLE_CPU_FEATURES` change which SIMD loops numpy runs (and so the last digits of its float32 `exp`, `tanh`, `log1p`, `sin` and `cos`), but a build ignored them and used Brevi's numpy, modelled on the CPU's own dispatch. With either set, or any other `NPY_*` / `NUMPY_*` setting, numpy programs now run with CPython's numpy, and the result cache keys on them.
- Docs: a case study of speed by build path on 587 agent-written Python programs at v0.28.2 (typed 62.4%, dynamic 37.6%, no CPython bridge, no wrong answers), with the start-up and compute caveats spelled out ([docs/PATH_SPLIT.md](docs/PATH_SPLIT.md), `bench/pathsplit.py`).
- Dynamic builds pickle the program's own int and tuple subclasses and namedtuples with CPython's bytes at every protocol (namedtuples used to pickle under module `_brevi_dyn` and load as another class), and run a class's own `__reduce__`, `__reduce_ex__`, `__getnewargs__` and `__getnewargs_ex__` in pickle and copy. `pickle.loads` runs the program's `__new__` and `__init__` at the point CPython runs them; a load whose objects would see a list, dict or set the load still adds to stops the program.
- An enum whose data type is a class of the program with its own `__new__` now finds `Enum.__new__`, as in CPython. `globals()['Name'] = value` inside a function stores a module global when nothing else in the file binds that name.
- Dynamic builds: `pickle.loads` of the program's own objects now runs a class's own `__setstate__` (and a `__getattr__` or `__getattribute__` the `__setstate__` lookup goes through, or a `__setattr__` the slots go through) as CPython 3.13 does, in the order CPython's Unpickler runs them. It was refused at run time, which made `test_dataclasses` `test_frozen_slots_pickle_custom_state` a wrong exit on main. Where Brevi cannot keep CPython's order (program code the load itself runs after such a BUILD, a BUILD the result never reaches, or a load that fails after one), the program stops with a refusal that a `try` cannot catch.
- Dynamic builds pickle instances of the program's own classes on object, set or frozenset (dataclasses included) with CPython's exact bytes, and load them back; `copy.copy`/`copy.deepcopy` now honour a class's `__getstate__`, `__setstate__`, `__reduce__`, `__reduce_ex__`, `__getnewargs__` and `__new__` (they were silently skipped), and frozen `slots=True` dataclasses get `_dataclass_getstate`/`_dataclass_setstate` as in CPython.
- Dynamic builds give a class or def made under `global NAME` in a function CPython's module-level `__qualname__`, and load a pickle of a nested class of the program below protocol 4 (`getattr(Outer, 'Inner')`).
- Dynamic builds: every refusal on the pickle and copy paths (a class's own `__reduce__`, a bound method, a deque or iterator Brevi cannot pickle as CPython does, and the rest) now stops the program instead of raising an exception a `try` could catch, so a program can no longer go on with an answer CPython would not give. `copy.copy`/`copy.deepcopy` of an object whose `__reduce__` gives a tuple of the wrong length now raises CPython's own `TypeError` instead of a refusal.
- No Rust install needed on Linux ARM64 and Windows (x86-64 and ARM64) either: with no `rustc` on the PATH, brevi fetches its pinned Rust there too and links with zig (on Windows the GNU-ABI Rust, so neither Visual Studio nor its C++ build tools are needed), each checked by `pinned-toolchain.yml` on a real machine of that kind. Refused, with the reason, on an ARM64 Linux machine with a Cortex-A53 core and no C compiler (zig cannot apply the linker fix for its erratum 843419).
- A program that fails to link now says what the linker printed, instead of only its exit code.
- Security: under `--sandbox`, a program that runs CPython (`--allow-py` or `--allow native`) is now refused where the kernel cannot lock CPython to the grants, instead of running it unlocked. That is macOS and Windows at build time (no file lock, and no network lock without `--allow net`), and at start a Linux kernel without Landlock, or Linux other than x86-64 and arm64 without a network grant. Before, with no folders named, CPython there kept full file access, and it kept the network on macOS and Windows. The new `--allow-unlocked-python` flag (also `brevi mcp --allow-unlocked-python` for a server's calls) builds such a program anyway, and it then prints a `brevi: warning:` line naming the missing lock every time it starts. On Linux with the locks nothing changes. Real locks for macOS and Windows are on the roadmap.
- Security: under `--sandbox` (and the MCP tools), every import the parser finds is now checked, not only imports at the start of a line: an import after `;`, after a one-line `if`/`def`, or split with `\` is refused unless it is on a line of its own (or the caller grants `--allow native`). Brevi's child-process module (`asyncio.create_subprocess_exec`/`_shell`) now also checks the native grant itself on every call, and programs can no longer import Brevi's internal `_brevi_*` modules.
- Docs: published scores, `docs/NUMBERS.md` and the package READMEs now quote v0.28.2 (unchanged from v0.28.1: core 1,043 of 1,072, 97.3% of the scored suite, with 83 string-as-code tests excluded; stdlib 1,989 of 2,053; none wrong). `docs/NUMBERS.md` says the Windows release gate now holds the standard-library slice too, read from the tag's release.yml. The README and install pages say the Rust-free install is in v0.28.2 rather than "from the next release", and release notes from v0.28.2 on no longer say brevi needs rustc on the PATH on Linux x86-64 and macOS.
- docs/SECURITY_TESTING.md: the two findings fixed in v0.28.2 (shared temp folders on unix, unbounded `brevi mcp --http` reads before the token check) are now listed with their details, affected versions and the fix.
- Brevi's `anthropic` and `openai` copies now answer as anthropic 2.8 and openai 3.51: structured outputs send a `const` or a non-empty enum of plain values without its `type`, as anthropic 2.8 does (a `Literal[...]` field no longer fails with "Schema must have a 'type'"), `anthropic.SandboxEgressRestrictedError` is new, and `__version__` reports the new releases. numpy 2.5.5 and onnxruntime 1.32.0 print the same as the versions before, so only their pins moved.
- Security: under `--sandbox`, CPython (`--allow-py`, `--allow native`) on Linux can no longer reach other processes. Whenever a hold is taken, the seccomp filter now also makes `ptrace`, `process_vm_readv`, `process_vm_writev`, `pidfd_open`, `pidfd_getfd` and `userfaultfd` fail with `PermissionError`, so held CPython cannot read or write another same-user process's memory, take its files, or use userfaultfd in a kernel exploit. Before, the filter held only `socket` and `io_uring_setup`, and only without a network grant. A kernel that refuses the filter now refuses such a program even with the network granted.
- Security testing in the open: docs/SECURITY_TESTING.md lists Brevi's attack surface, its fuzz targets, checks and red-team attempts with their results, and docs/THREAT_MODEL.md now has a table of every place outside input reaches Brevi. Ten coverage-guided fuzz targets (`cargo +nightly fuzz`, in fuzz/) cover the parsers, the sandbox's capability check, MCP messages and grants, `brevi mcp --http` request framing, `--limit` values, folder grants and the standalone CPython bundle reader. CodeQL scans the code weekly.
- `brevi mcp`'s JSON reader refuses a number too large for a float (`1e999`), which it used to write back as `inf`, which is not JSON. Found by the new fuzzing.
- **Release and install supply chain.** `install.sh` now takes everything from one release tag: it finds the latest release once (or uses `BREVI_VERSION`), and its source-build fallback builds that tag with `--locked` instead of `main` with fresh dependency versions. When `gh` is installed and signed in, it also checks the archive's build-provenance attestation and installs nothing if the check fails. The sandbox image and the dev container feature fetch `install.sh` and the SDK from the release they install, not from `main`. Both Docker images now run as an unprivileged `brevi` user and pin their base images by digest (checked in CI: the Rust image must really be the pinned Rust). The release workflow grants no permissions by default, and the MCP registry publisher it runs with an OIDC token is pinned by version and SHA-256.
- `sys.setswitchinterval` and `sys.getswitchinterval` now work in dynamic builds. Brevi's green threads switch only at blocking calls, so the interval schedules nothing, but it is kept as CPython keeps it (whole microseconds, so `set` truncates and `get` gives `1e-6 *` that), with CPython's argument conversion and errors. An interval of 4294.967296 seconds or more, `nan` or `inf` is refused: CPython casts it to a C `unsigned long`, whose result differs by platform.
- Wrong-answer fix: a dict filled with `d.setdefault(k, [..])` was read by the sharing proof as holding no lists, so two names for one of its items could print different values on the typed build (`x = d[k]; y = d.setdefault(k, []); y.append(9); print(x)` printed the old list). Such files now build over dynamic values.
- The sharing proof now follows `m = d[k]` / `m = d.setdefault(k, ...)` then `m[0] += v` while d is still read: the typed build writes m back right after each change, and the proof checks that it did (else the file builds over dynamic values). Programs like the FIFO stock and league solutions build typed again.
- `BREVI_DYN=0` no longer skips the wrong-answer guard: a file the guard sends to the dynamic build is refused instead of built typed (only the compiler's own typed tests skip the guard, with `BREVI_DYN=0 BREVI_GUARD=off`, never under `--strict`).
- Typed build: a loop over a JSON number, bool or None stops with Python's TypeError (`'int' object is not iterable`) instead of looping zero times.
- Fix (wrong answer): the typed build read a JSON int past 2**53 with `json.loads` as a nearby float (`9007199254740993` printed as `9007199254740992`), and `json.dumps` of a dict or list holding such an int rounded it the same way. It now stops there, and `brevi run` re-runs the program on the dynamic build, which keeps every digit (a standalone `brevi build` binary stops with an error that says so).
- Fix: JSON ints from 1e15 up to 2**53 stay ints in the typed build (`d["n"] + 1` used to stop with "expected an int here"), and an error inside such a conversion names the statement's line instead of line 0.
- Typed build: 7 of the 29 agent-written programs whose typed Rust failed to compile (path-split case study) now build typed and match CPython: `s * n` with a local str and a computed int, `print(a if c else b)` with branches of different plain types, `d.get(k, 0) + x` where the values are Decimal or float, a dict of Decimals read by a computed key (`RATES.get(tier, ZERO)`), and `s.split(None, n)`.
- Typed build: `split()`, `strip()`, `lstrip()`, `rstrip()`, `isspace()` and `Decimal(...)` now treat U+001C..U+001F as whitespace, as CPython's `str.isspace()` does (they printed different results before).
- Security: `brevi build`, `check`, `emit`, `test` and `fix` now refuse an option they do not have, with a clear error and exit code 2, as `brevi run` already did after the file. Before, a misspelt flag was dropped silently: `brevi build fs.py --sandbx` built an unsandboxed binary and exited 0. `brevi run` now also refuses an unknown option before the file, and `--timeout`, `--input`, `--once` and `--mcp` without `--verify`; the error names the nearest real option (`did you mean --sandbox?`). An unknown command (`brevi biuld`) is refused before anything is built, and `brevi toolchain install` takes no further words.
- Fix: words after `--` are the program's own: `brevi run main.py -- --fast --sandbox` passes both to the program instead of taking them as Brevi options.
- Docs: the quickstart, README, both sites, the PyPI README and the agent skills now lead with one command, `uvx --python 3.13 brevi run --verify main.py`, which fetches brevi, CPython 3.13 and the pinned Rust. No Rust install on Linux x86-64 and macOS from v0.28.2; Linux ARM64 and Windows need Rust from rustup until the next release.
- CI: `install-test` now runs that command as documented (uv, then `uvx`, then `uv tool install` for the demo) on a bare Ubuntu container with no C compiler, macOS with the runner's Rust removed, and Windows x64 and ARM64 (with rustup until the next release).
- `--limit cpu` now works on Windows. A Job Object holds the program and every process it starts: the kernel ends the job when its user-mode time reaches the limit, and a watch thread in `brevi`, which the program cannot stop or starve, ends it once user and kernel time together reach it (checked every 10 ms). `--limit file`, `fds` and `threads` stay refused on Windows, with messages that say why: Windows has no limit on one file's size or on a process's open handles, and a job cannot cap threads.

## v0.28.2 (16 Nov 2026)

- Security: `brevi mcp --http` now bounds what a client can make it hold before the token is checked. Chunk sizes that would add up past the 4 MiB body limit are refused (413) instead of wrapping, a body is stored only as its bytes arrive, a connection silent for 30 s or a request not whole within 120 s is closed, and at most 64 connections are served at once (503 past that).
- Security (Linux and other unix): Brevi's temporary folders (build folders, cargo projects, MCP work folders, the background build slots, and the folder a standalone binary unpacks its CPython into when there is no home folder) now live in a folder private to the user, `$TMPDIR/brevi-UID` (else `~/.cache/brevi/tmp`), used only when it is a real folder owned by the user with no access for anyone else, under folders no one else can change. Before, they were shared names in the temp folder that another local user could make first. With no private folder to use, brevi stops and says why. With no home folder, the cache moves into the private folder too.
- Security posture stated plainly in SECURITY.md, the README, the docs, both sites, the package READMEs and the skill: no external security audit yet (we are seeking funding for one; until then, do not run hostile code under the sandbox), the sandbox is a policy layer and not an isolation boundary, run untrusted code in a container or VM, and `--allow-py` / `--allow native` are full trust. SECURITY.md now says who should use Brevi today (design partners with trusted code in their own container) and what a public beta and a hostile-code claim need first; the audit is on the roadmap. `tools/security_claims.py` keeps these statements on every page and fails CI on a page that calls the sandbox an isolation boundary.
- **Windows: the 23 stdlib wrong answers found by the first Windows stdlib run are fixed** (#587 source paths, #588 CRLF source, #589 cp1252 and `re.LOCALE`), so the Windows stdlib known-wrong list is empty and this release is the first gated on the Windows stdlib slice. KNOWN_ISSUES.md's Windows section says so.
- Source files with Windows line ends (CRLF, or a lone CR) now read as CPython reads them: every line end is a newline before lexing, so a triple-quoted string no longer keeps the `\r`, and a backslash before a CRLF inside a string is a line continuation again. This fixes 13 stdlib conformance tests (textwrap, `re` error positions) that gave wrong answers on Windows only (#588).
- Dynamic builds now give CPython's answer for `re.LOCALE` in Windows' default cp1252 locale (#589). The cp1252 codec (also `windows-1252` and `1252`) works for `bytes.decode`, `str.encode`, `bytes(s, "cp1252")` and `str(b, "cp1252")`, with every error handler and CPython's `charmap` errors. Bytes patterns under `re.LOCALE` take case and word characters from the C library in the environment's LC_CTYPE locale, as CPython's sre does, on every platform. Under `--sandbox`, which reads no environment, a `re.LOCALE` match stops the program instead of guessing the locale.
- With `PYTHONUTF8=1`, the CPython that a built program embeds for bridged modules runs in UTF-8 mode, as python3 does. `locale.getpreferredencoding()` now returns `utf-8` there, where before it returned the locale encoding: ASCII under the C locale, and the ANSI code page (cp1252) on Windows.
- **Windows stdlib conformance gates a release.** The stdlib CPython conformance slice now has its own Windows floor (`compat/conformance_windows_stdlib_floor.json`) and known-wrong list (`compat/conformance_windows_stdlib_known_wrong/`), like the core slice. A release runs `tools/conformance.py --slice stdlib --check --release-gate` on the `brevi.exe` it ships, on x86-64 and ARM64, so a Windows stdlib wrong result or a score below the floor or 80% publishes nothing. The weekly Windows job runs both slices and raises both Windows floors on main.
- Windows: warnings, `functools.partial` methods, `sys.path[0]` and `os.path.realpath` give paths as CPython does (`C:\...`), never with the `\\?\` prefix (#587).
- **One table for every published number.** `docs/NUMBERS.md` is written by `tools/published_numbers.py` from the release tag's own conformance reports and `bench/compare/speed.json`: core 1,043 of 1,072 (97.3% of the scored suite, with 83 string-as-code tests excluded), stdlib 1,989 of 2,053 (6 excluded), assertion-bearing 88.2% and 94.3%, typed share 2.6% and 1.2%, and speed as typed median 9.3x against dynamic median 0.85x. CI (`--check`) refuses a stale table, any page, package README, agent skill, MCP text or release note that quotes the core headline share without that scope, and any that quotes a typed speed median without the dynamic one.
- The package READMEs (PyPI, npm, crates.io) quoted v0.26's 58% conformance; they now quote v0.28.1's.
- **Release notes say which commit the numbers measured.** A release's reports measure the release PR's merge and the tag is the commit that adds them, so the two hashes differ (v0.28.1: measured `aa554040`, tagged `96a51afc`). The notes now name both and check that only reports and floors differ between them.
- Release notes now open with the conformance numbers in full, for both the core and stdlib slices: matches, wrong answers, tests refused by design (they run a string as code and are left out of the count), the share of matches that print something to compare, the matches that print nothing, and the matches that took the typed build. The notes quote every figure or none: `tools/release_notes.py` stops if a report lacks one. When the notes of an existing release are rewritten (release-notes.yml), the numbers come from the reports the tag holds, not from main's, and the release's "Tested" line is kept.
- The conformance reports' percentages are rounded once, from the counts: they rounded the share to four places and then to one decimal, so core assertion-bearing coverage, 945 of 1072 (88.153%), read 88.1% instead of 88.2%. The release gate now compares the counts with the gate too, rather than the four-place rounded share.
- `tools/release_notes.py --check` (release preflight) now refuses a report whose printed share is not the counts beside it, rounded once, or whose counts differ from report.json, for coverage, assertion-bearing coverage, typed-path share and the forced dynamic build. report.json now stores the shares unrounded. Every published share, the "Paths taken" line included, is printed by one helper (`tools/share.py`), which rounds half up: 1 of 16 is 6.3%, where Python's `.1f` gave 6.2%. The conformance and release-notes unit tests now run in CI.
- `brevi run` on a file it hands to CPython now prints its `{"path":"cpython",...}` note before CPython starts, so the note always comes before the program's output (it was printed after the spawn and could land after lines the program had already written). If CPython then fails to start, a `note: not run with CPython` line says so.
- No Rust install needed: with no `rustc` on the PATH, brevi fetches the Rust release it is tested with on its first build, and zig to link with when the machine has no working C linker, each checked against a SHA-256 compiled into brevi and kept in Brevi's own folder. On by default on Linux x86-64 and macOS (Apple silicon and Intel); Linux ARM64 and Windows (the GNU-ABI Rust with zig, no Visual Studio) are refused with the reason until each passes `pinned-toolchain.yml`, and `BREVI_TOOLCHAIN=pinned` tries them. `brevi toolchain` fetches now and prints what builds use; `BREVI_TOOLCHAIN=auto|system|pinned`, `BREVI_LINKER=cc|zig` and `BREVI_TOOLCHAIN_DIR` choose. `uvx --python 3.13 brevi run --verify main.py` is a whole install. install.sh no longer installs rustup where the pinned toolchain works.
- Docs: what a `--standalone` binary cannot carry.

## v0.28.1 (16 Nov 2026)

- **Conformance reports name the commit they measured, and a release refuses reports from another tree.** v0.28.0's reports and release notes said "Brevi 0.27.1": main measured the v0.28.0 tree before the version bump, and its run after the release merge timed out, so the reports were never redone. Each report now gives the Brevi version and the commit it measured. `tools/release_notes.py --check`, which runs in release.yml's preflight (so in dry runs as well), fails unless both reports measured the version being released on the release's own tree. Main's conformance run gets 50 minutes instead of 30; its last three runs were cancelled at 30.
- **The conformance floors count native matches only.** The stdlib floor said 1990 while the report's coverage said 1988, because the floor counted the 2 tests that match only through the CPython bridge. Those do not count towards coverage, so a test moving from a native match to the bridge lowered coverage but still passed the floor. The floor now counts what coverage counts, and the stdlib floor reads 1988.
- README, the home page and `llms.txt` now show the current conformance numbers. They used to show v0.27's 80% core and 45% stdlib. The README also gives the tests refused by design (83 core, 6 stdlib), the shares of matches that print something to compare (88.2% and 94.3%) and the shares that take the typed fast path (2.6% and 1.2%).
- `collections.namedtuple` checks type and field names with `str.isidentifier()` on CPython 3.13's tables (it took any alphabetic or numeric character, so `'a²'` was accepted and `'a·b'` refused), splits a string of field names on CPython's whitespace (`\x1c`-`\x1f` included), and stops the program on a field name NFKC normalization may change (CPython compiles the normalized name), rather than build it differently.
- `re.IGNORECASE` now works with any Rust toolchain: it folds case with CPython 3.13's own tables (generated into `src/unicode.rs` and checked against every code point) instead of rustc's, whose Unicode version follows the toolchain. On Rust 1.100 (Unicode 18) and newer, Unicode `re.IGNORECASE` patterns were refused at build time; on Rust older than Unicode 15 they were refused too. CI's pinned Rust moves to 1.100.0.
- The typed build's `re.IGNORECASE` (`re.I`, a leading `(?i)`) now matches as CPython 3.13's `re` does. Before, it lowercased the text and the pattern with rustc's Unicode tables, which gave wrong answers: `ſ` did not match `s`, `İ` matched nothing, `\x41` did not match `a`, and `re.I | re.A` still folded non-ASCII characters (the Kelvin sign matched `k`). It now compiles each character and set as CPython does, with `_sre`'s case tables and `re._casefix`'s extra cases (generated into `src/unicode_sre.rs` by `tools/gen_unicode.py --sre`, shared with the dynamic build), and folds only ASCII letters under `re.ASCII`. It is also faster: the text is no longer lowercased again for every search position.
- Typed build of a .py file: a regex pattern built at run time (`p = "(a)" + "\\1"; re.search(p, s)`) is now checked with the same rule as a literal one. Syntax the typed matcher lacks (back-references, named groups, look-arounds, `\ud800` escapes, CPython compile errors) stops the typed run, and `brevi run` runs the program again on the dynamic build, which gives CPython's answer. Before, such a pattern matched silently wrong. A program that cannot be run twice (it writes files) stops with an error that says why. Brevi (.bv) programs keep their own regex rules.
- `str.upper()`, `lower()`, `title()`, `capitalize()`, `swapcase()`, `isupper()`, `islower()`, `istitle()`, `isalpha()`, `isalnum()` and `isspace()` follow CPython 3.13's Unicode 15.1 tables in both builds, generated into `src/unicode_case.rs` by `tools/gen_unicode_case.py`. Before, they used rustc's tables, which are Unicode 16 or newer, so characters added since 15.1 gave wrong answers (`"ɤ".upper()` gave U+A7CB). CPython's own rules now hold too: `title()` and `capitalize()` use the title case mapping (`"ǆ".title()` is `"ǅ"`), `title()` starts a new word after any uncased character (`"中a".title()` is `"中A"`), `lower()`, `swapcase()` and `title()` apply the final sigma rule, `isupper()` and `islower()` are False when there is a title case character, and `isspace()`, `split()` and `strip()` treat `\x1c` to `\x1f` as whitespace.
- Windows ARM64: releases ship `brevi-aarch64-pc-windows-msvc.zip`, a `win_arm64` PyPI wheel and npm support for `win32-arm64`, and the setup action installs it on ARM64 Windows runners. It is built and checked on a real ARM64 Windows machine (the same archive, platform and parity checks as x86-64), never cross-built untested.
- Windows: HTTPS in Brevi's own requests and httpx now trusts the Windows certificate store when no CA file is given, read as CPython 3.13's `SSLContext.load_default_certs()` reads it: the "CA" and "ROOT" stores of the local machine, the current user, group policy, enterprise, services and users, keeping the certificates trusted for server authentication (an enhanced key usage that leaves server authentication out is not trusted). Certifi's bundle (or `SSL_CERT_FILE`, `REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE`, `CURL_CA_BUNDLE`) is still loaded beside it, so a root Windows has not downloaded yet still verifies. Before, a certificate trusted only through the Windows store, such as a company's own root, failed to verify. New parity checks in `tests/windows/parity.ps1` add self-signed certificates to the current user's "CA" store for one run, and verify a public site through the ROOT store alone, each compared with CPython's default context.
- **Windows conformance gates a release.** The core CPython conformance slice now has its own Windows floor (`compat/conformance_windows_floor.json`) and known-wrong list (`compat/conformance_windows_known_wrong/`, one file per entry). A release runs `tools/conformance.py --check --release-gate` on the `brevi.exe` it ships, so a Windows wrong result or a Windows score below the floor or 90% publishes nothing. The weekly Windows job now runs the same ratchet and raises the floor on main. The stdlib slice has no Windows floor yet, so on Windows it only measures and refuses to gate. Four `test_set.TestBasicOpsTriple` tests are now classed unstable on every platform: CPython prints a set holding `operator.add`, which it orders by memory address, and Windows CPython prints another order than Linux CPython.
- `brevi build --standalone` works on Windows: one .exe carrying CPython 3.13, the standard library, the imported packages and the DLLs they need, which runs on a machine with no Python. It is refused at build time with Microsoft Store Python or a Python for another CPU.
- A `--standalone` binary now uses only the Python it carries. If its bundle is missing (the file was cut short, or code signing appended to it), it refuses to run rather than quietly using an installed Python.
- Dynamic builds with `--standalone` now carry CPython too. Before, they were built without it and needed Python installed.
- With `--standalone`, duckdb, onnxruntime, the network modules and (on Linux and macOS) sqlite3 run in the bundled CPython, not in Brevi's own copies. Those copies load a library from outside the program (the installed package's engine, OpenSSL, the system's libsqlite3), so the binary would not have been standalone. Under `--sandbox` without the module granted to CPython, the build is refused.

## v0.28.0 (15 Nov 2026)

- Dynamic build: abstract methods are now enforced. A class built on `abc.ABC` or made with `metaclass=abc.ABCMeta` gets `__abstractmethods__`, and instantiating it while an abstract method is left raises CPython's TypeError. Before, such a class could be instantiated, which was a silent wrong answer. The check follows CPython's `getattr(value, "__isabstractmethod__", False)`, so abstract properties, classmethods, partialmethods and `singledispatchmethod` over `@abstractmethod` count as well (the last was refused before). `property.__isabstractmethod__` and the attribute on a staticmethod or classmethod read as in CPython.
- Dynamic build: `re.Scanner` is supported. It follows CPython's construction: `.scanner.pattern` is None, the flags are raw, and without `re.UNICODE`, `\w`, `\d`, `\s` and IGNORECASE are ASCII-only. A phrase with its own groups, backreferences or inline flags is refused, because CPython numbers its groups differently inside the combined pattern.
- Contributor tooling: `CLAUDE.md` and `tools/code_map.py` (one line per source file) so agent sessions find their way without reading the tree; `tools/session_tokens.py` measures an agent session's tokens; `tools/agent_hooks/context_ceiling.py` makes long agent sessions hand off instead of growing; `tools/pr_guard.py` holds the changelog and published-score rules in CI; `tools/ds_contrib.py` runs DeepSeek only in its off-peak hours.
- Dynamic build: a def keeps its `__annotations__` when the program reads them by name (`getattr(f, "__annotations__")`) or through `functools.WRAPPER_ASSIGNMENTS`, not only as `f.__annotations__`; before, those reads were refused at run time.
- **Fixed: values shared through a library call stay shared (dynamic builds).** `pickle.loads(pickle.dumps([a, a]))` used to give back two separate lists, and a dict and the list it held came back unlinked. Every list, dict and set reached twice in one library call is now one object on the other side, in both directions, cycles included. That is what CPython does, and what pickle, copy and other libraries expect.
- **Fixed: `reversed(some_list)` is live (dynamic builds).** It used to iterate a copy, so a write made after `reversed()` was missed. It now reads the list as it goes and stops early if the list shrinks, like CPython's `list_reverseiterator`. Typed builds and dict views still iterate a copy (#459).
- **Fixed: list and dict iterators pickle as CPython's do.** An iterator and its list pickled together come back sharing the list, at the same position. A list's reverse iterator pickles as `reversed(list)` at its index, and a dict's reverse iterators pickle as their remaining items. The typed build gives a file that pickles an iterator to the dynamic build, which handles this. With these fixes the core conformance slice has no known wrong results and reads 91.7%, so the 90% release gate passes.
- Dynamic builds: a Python library CPython runs inside the program now sees the program's defs, lambdas and bound methods as CPython's functions and methods: `__name__`, `__qualname__`, `__doc__`, `__module__`, `__defaults__`, `__kwdefaults__`, `__annotations__`, assigned attributes, `co_flags`, `inspect.isfunction`/`ismethod`/`isgeneratorfunction`, binding when put in a class, equality and hashing, `functools.wraps`. They used to arrive as an anonymous builtin, so `inspect.getdoc` printed `None` and a library read the name `brevi_function`. What compiled code cannot answer truly (its bytecode and the other `co_*` fields, `__globals__`, `__closure__`) is refused with `NotImplementedError` when it is read, so `dis` loaded through `importlib` and `inspect.signature` no longer raise a `TypeError` or `ValueError` CPython would not raise. A code object's `co_*` fields other than `co_name`, `co_flags` and `co_firstlineno` are refused in the program itself too, instead of raising `AttributeError`. `type(f)` of such a function answers as `types.FunctionType` (`types.MethodType` for a method) does to `==`, hashing (a dict keyed by `FunctionType` finds it), attributes, `repr`, `isinstance`, `copy` and calling it; only `type(f) is types.FunctionType`, `id(type(f))`, `issubclass(type(f), types.FunctionType)` and `type(type(f))` still tell it apart, since CPython checks those on its own C type and no stand-in can pass them.
- Dynamic build: a builtin, or a library module's function, stored as a class attribute no longer binds as a method. `class T: reduce = functools.reduce` and then `T().reduce(f, xs)` works as in CPython, where these are not descriptors; before, `self` was passed as the first argument (#447). `functools._CacheInfo` is the class every `cache_info()` returns, and it can be called (#448).
`functools.cmp_to_key(mycmp=f)` and `key(obj=x)` take their argument by name too, with CPython's errors.
- Dynamic build: a builtin type answers only the attributes CPython gives it, so `hasattr(int, "zzz")` is False and `int.zzz` raises AttributeError (any name used to return a method stub) (#470).
- `reversed()` of a dict or one of its views is live in the dynamic build, as in CPython: it sees later value changes and raises RuntimeError when the dict's size changes; a same-size key change while it runs is refused, not guessed. The typed build sends a file to the dynamic build when a list changes while a `reversed()` of it is still read (#459).
- **`bytearray` and `memoryview` in the dynamic build**: both were refused at build time and now run natively. `bytearray` has CPython's mutators, slice assignment and deletion, in-place `+=`/`*=`, and bytes' methods returning bytearray; `memoryview` covers one-dimensional views of bytes and bytearray (slicing, item and slice assignment, `release()`, `with`, `tobytes`, `tolist`, `toreadonly`, `hex`, hashing of read-only views). A view or an `re` iterator over a bytearray holds its buffer, so resizing it raises CPython's BufferError, and `re` group slices of a bytearray are read live. Still refused at build time: `memoryview.cast` and multi-dimensional views, subclasses of bytes, bytearray and memoryview, and either type in a program that also uses a CPython-run library; passing one to such a library raises NotImplementedError.
- Dynamic build: subclasses of bytearray (`class BA(bytearray)`): methods, mutation, `+=`/`*=` in place, repr naming the subclass, int()/float() parsing, memoryview over the instance, copy, `fromhex` building the subclass; unhashable as in CPython.
- Dynamic build fixes (silent wrong answers): a str or tuple subclass instance no longer loses its hash after its first lookup (`S('a') in {S('a')}`); copy.copy/deepcopy of a bytearray make a new one; bytearray's repr escapes `'` as CPython's does; bytes, bytearray and memoryview no longer report attributes CPython's lack (`hasattr(b'', 'append')`); float() of a bytearray shows its repr in the error; int(x, base) takes a str subclass instance.
- **A bytearray passed to array, struct or zlib in the dynamic build**: `array.frombytes(bytearray(...))`, `array.array('B', ba)`, `a.extend(ba)`, `struct.pack_into`/`unpack`/`unpack_from` (and `struct.Struct`'s), `zlib.crc32`/`compress` and the other C functions of these modules take a bytearray, or an instance of a bytearray subclass that defines no methods, and their writes reach the program's bytearray, as in CPython. Each call gets a copy that is copied back afterwards, so it is allowed only when the call's other arguments are plain values (no program code can run during it); a library that keeps the bytearray after the call (`struct.iter_unpack`) stops with NotImplementedError, and a bytearray passed to any other CPython-run library is still refused.
- **Subclasses of `bytes` in the dynamic build**: `class B(bytes)` was refused at build time and now runs. Instances keep their class (`type`, `isinstance`, `__mro__`, slots and mixins), use bytes' methods (which return plain bytes, as in CPython), and work as `re` patterns and subjects (`Pattern.pattern` is the instance), with `memoryview`, `int()`/`float()`, `B.fromhex` (which returns a `B`) and `B.maketrans`. `re.sub` also takes a str-subclass replacement. Subclasses of `bytearray` and `memoryview` stay refused at build time.
- Dynamic build: `typing.ByteString` is refused at build time. `collections.abc.ByteString` issues CPython 3.13's DeprecationWarning on `isinstance()` and when a subclass is made, at the program's line.
- Dynamic build: `warnings.catch_warnings(record=True)` is no longer refused. The block gets CPython's own list, kept live, and each recorded warning names the program's file, line and module (with the line tracking from the warnings change). A warning at a line the build cannot place is still refused rather than recorded at a wrong line. It stays refused in a program that uses itertools and pickles or copies, because itertools iterators there do not pickle yet and their copies miss CPython 3.13's DeprecationWarning.
- **CI no longer rebuilds every old solution when only the conformance lists change.** The pull-request check rebuilt all ~2,000 old solutions (Brevi, Python and Rust) and reran the grammar check whenever a file in `compat/` changed, but only `compat/baseline.txt` feeds those checks; the conformance ratchet's lists in `compat/` are checked by their own step. PRs from the conformance work now skip the Python and Rust rebuild (and the grammar check unless they touch what it parses).
- CI: pull requests no longer raise the conformance floors or rewrite the published conformance reports (`bench/conformance/`). After each merge, main runs both slices on the merged tree and commits the floors it measures and the reports (`tools/conformance.py --check --raise-floor` in `conformance-main.yml`). `tools/floor_guard.py` fails a PR that raises one, and `--write-baseline` now only lowers them. Every conformance PR used to raise the same two numbers, so each merge put every other open PR into conflict. Each PR then had to merge main, re-measure and re-run CI (#483 ran 9 times).
- The conformance known-wrong lists are now folders with one file per entry (`compat/conformance_known_wrong/`, `compat/conformance_stdlib_known_wrong/`; each file is named `test-id.mode` and holds `issue=#N owner=LOGIN expires=YYYY-MM-DD`). PRs that add or remove different entries no longer conflict, as they did when neighbouring lines of one list changed.
- `tools/ci_local.sh` runs the MCP checks against the same pinned mcp stack as GitHub CI (a cached venv over the local Python, made once per pin set), so a container with another pydantic no longer reports `brevi verify` "differs" on correct servers.
- `tools/ci_local.sh` puts its pinned mcp venv on PATH only for the MCP checks (new `CHECK_SKIP` in `tests/check_parallel.py`), so the CPython sandbox checks, which cannot see `$HOME`, pass locally again.
- **CI: pull requests now check the MCP servers against the real `mcp`.** The pins come from tests/requirements-native.txt, without duckdb and onnxruntime, and the step takes about a minute. Until now these checks ran only in the daily run and at release, so a change to Brevi's pydantic copy (2.14.3 in #457) made `brevi verify` on tests/mcp_sdk report "differs" on machines where CPython still had pydantic 2.13, and no pull request showed it.
- CI, releases and the Docker image build with Rust 1.99.1 (pinned in `.github/rust-version`) instead of the latest stable. Rust 1.100 ships Unicode 18 tables, and Brevi refuses `re.IGNORECASE` on Unicode tables it has not checked against CPython, so every PR's checks failed the day it reached GitHub's runners. The daily toolchain run still tests the latest stable.
- Pull requests that change only docs, the site or the conformance lists no longer start a GitHub CI run; they merge on `tools/ci_local.sh`, which now also runs the workflow, credential, built-file and `--limit` checks, and both conformance slices whenever a conformance list changed.
- CI: the stdlib conformance slice now runs on main after every merge that can change it (`.github/workflows/conformance-main.yml`; a newer merge cancels the run in progress, and a failure opens a "conformance" issue), so two PRs that pass alone but leave a wrong result together are caught within one run. `tools/ci_local.sh` runs the stdlib slice too when a branch changes the compiler.
- **Class `__annotations__` in the dynamic build.** A program that reads `C.__annotations__` gets the class's own annotations, evaluated as the class body runs, as in CPython; an instance finds them through its class's bases, and a class without any raises AttributeError from an instance. A program whose class annotations are type expressions (`int | None`, `list[int]`) is refused rather than run, and programs that never read `__annotations__` build as before.
- **Comprehensions in a class body (dynamic build).** `class D: q = [i for i in range(2)]` failed to build in the dynamic build, and once built would have let the comprehension see the class's own names. Its variables are now plain locals, and past the first iterable it resolves names as CPython does: enclosing functions, globals and builtins, never the class namespace (also in Enum classes and classes that call `locals()`). A walrus inside such a comprehension is refused at build time, where CPython raises SyntaxError ([#442](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/442)).
- **`locals()` in a class body (dynamic build).** A class body that calls `locals()` now runs over its real namespace dict, as CPython's does: `locals()["x"] = v` is seen by later name reads, `del name` deletes from it, and the class is made from the dict. Classes that do not call `locals()` build exactly as before.
- Dynamic build: `vars(C)` and `C.__dict__` now hold exactly what CPython 3.13's type dict holds, in its order: `__firstlineno__`, `__static_attributes__`, the `__dict__` and `__weakref__` descriptors, `__slots__` member descriptors, ABCs' `_abc_impl`; no more Brevi-internal `__flags__`, `__abc_tpflags__` or `register` entries, and a namedtuple class has no extra `__eq__`/`__hash__`/`__lt__`/`__gt__`. Builtin types answer from CPython 3.13.13's own tables (`tools/gen_type_dicts.py`). A class Brevi cannot give exactly (an Enum, a dataclass, a typing class, a base of unknown layout, a class whose annotations Brevi did not keep) refuses instead of answering wrongly.
- Dynamic build: private names are mangled as CPython mangles them (`self.__x` in class `A` is `_A__x`, also in `vars()` of instances and in `__slots__`); before, they were stored unmangled.
- Dynamic build: `dir(x)` is supported, answered from those exact dicts (a module, or `dir()` without an argument, is refused). `C.q` for a `__slots__` name gives its member descriptor instead of raising.
- Dynamic build: docstrings are cleaned as CPython 3.13 cleans them (leading spaces and common indentation removed); `classmethod(GenericAlias)` works as a class's `__class_getitem__`; `locals()` in a class body starts with `__module__`, `__qualname__` and `__firstlineno__`.
- Dynamic build: `classmethod()` and `staticmethod()` of a callable instance (a `functools.partial`, an object with `__call__`) bind as CPython's do. A classmethod used to drop the class argument.
- **Functions have `__closure__`** in the dynamic build: `None` without free variables, otherwise a tuple of cell objects (`cell_contents`, comparison and repr as in CPython), in CPython's order. Cells are recorded only in programs that name `__closure__`, so making a closure costs nothing extra elsewhere; reading it where the cells were not recorded (a computed attribute name, or a method using zero-argument `super()`) is refused with an error, never answered `None`. Written by DeepSeek, reviewed and reworked by Claude.
- **`collections.abc` is native in the dynamic build**: the ABCs from CPython 3.13's `_collections_abc.py` with ABCMeta's rules (`isinstance`/`issubclass` through `__subclasshook__`, `register()`, the caches, `__abstractmethods__`, instantiation errors), subclassing with the mixin methods, `Mapping[str, int]` as a `types.GenericAlias`, and `type(Mapping)` being `abc.ABCMeta`. Before, a program using an ABC as a value ran CPython's ABCs through the bridge (about 27 ms to start; now about 4 ms). Builtin types answer the ABC hooks from an exact table of CPython's methods; an ABC check of a library type the table does not know is refused. `collections.abc.Callable[...]` is refused. Fixed with it: a class that defines `__eq__` without `__hash__` gets `__hash__ = None` (unhashable, and its subclasses too, as in CPython; before, it inherited a base class's `__hash__`); `object`'s `__eq__`/`__ne__`/`__lt__`/... read off an instance return CPython's answers (`NotImplemented` included); `type()` of dict views, `slice`, `...`, `NotImplemented`, builtin functions and bound methods names CPython's classes. Written by DeepSeek, reviewed and reworked by Claude.
- Dynamic build: `collections.ChainMap`, `UserDict`, `UserList` and `UserString` are CPython's own classes on the native `collections.abc` ABCs (a program that names one builds them; the typed build sends such a file to the dynamic build). `x.__class__` of a generator or other builtin value is its type, not a bound method.
- **`complex` in the dynamic build**: complex literals (`1j`, `3.25-4j`) and `complex()` were refused at build time and now run natively, computed as CPython 3.13 computes them: `complex()` from numbers, strings (`complex('(1+2j)')`), `__complex__`, `__float__` and `__index__`, with CPython's errors; `+ - * / **` with ints, floats and complexes (Smith's division, the small-integer power path, `ZeroDivisionError`/`OverflowError` where CPython raises them); `abs`, `hash` (equal to the int or float hash when the imaginary part is 0), `==`, `repr`/`str`, `.real`, `.imag`, `.conjugate()`, `format()` with every complex format spec, `%`-formatting errors, `pow(z, n, m)` raising "complex modulo", and a negative float to a fractional power (`(-8) ** 0.5`) now giving a complex instead of being refused. Complex values cross to and from libraries CPython runs. The typed build still refuses complex and hands the program to the dynamic build; a subclass of `complex` is refused at build time.
- Dynamic build fixes found on the way: the `z` format option (negative zero coercion) was ignored, so `format(-0.0, 'z.1f')` printed `-0.0`; it now prints `0.0`, and `z` on an int or str raises CPython's ValueError. `iter(it)` of an iterator class of the program's returns `it` itself (`iter(it) is it`), and an `__iter__` returning a non-iterator raises CPython's TypeError. `type(iter(bytearray()))` and `type(iter(memoryview(b)))` are `bytearray_iterator` and `memory_iterator` instead of a refusal. `itertools.repeat(x, times=n)` takes its keyword arguments as CPython does instead of crashing. `range()` with a start, stop or step past 64 bits (`range(sys.maxsize - 5, sys.maxsize + 5)`) was refused and now runs: len, indexing, `in`, `index`, `count`, `reversed`, ==, repr and attributes all match CPython, `len()` of a range too long for an ssize_t raises its OverflowError, and slicing such a range is refused. `hash(range(...))` now follows CPython's rule (length, start, step) and equals CPython's value, so `hash(range(0)) == hash(range(5, 5))`. Iterating a range near the 64-bit limits no longer overflows: `range(sys.maxsize - 2, sys.maxsize, 5)` never ended and `reversed(range(-sys.maxsize - 1, -sys.maxsize + 2))` was empty; both now give CPython's items.
- **Conformance: object addresses no longer make a test "unstable".** A default repr such as `<function f at 0x7f...>` shows where the object sits in memory, so CPython printed something different on every run, and the scorer set those tests aside without counting them. Addresses are now blanked before outputs are compared, and the rest of the repr is still compared exactly. This scores 30 more stdlib tests: 6 match, and 20 tests were wrong without anyone seeing it. Those 20 are now tracked under #446, #447, #451 and #452. Stdlib coverage moves from 66.6% to 64.5%, which is the honest number.
- **Conformance: tests that run a string as code count as "refused by design".** A Brevi binary is compiled ahead of time and sandboxed by its caller, so it refuses `compile()`, `exec()` and `eval()` of a string on purpose. The tests that need them (25 core, 2 stdlib) are now named in the reviewed list `compat/conformance_by_design.txt` and left out of the coverage denominator. Their count is printed beside every score. `--check` fails on such a refusal that is not in the list, and on a listed test that is no longer refused that way, so the list changes only through a reviewed edit. Core reads 89.2% (257 of 288) against the 90% release gate, and stdlib reads 65.3%.
- `tools/conformance.py --candidates` measures 81 more CPython test files (`tools/conformance_candidates.json`) for planning which files to add next. It only writes a report: it refuses `--check`, `--raise-floor`, `--release-gate` and `--write-baseline`, and it never writes into `bench/conformance`. The published slices, floors and scores are unchanged.
- **Conformance: the scorer now runs every test it used to skip as not extractable.** `tools/conformance.py` now handles tests that use `threading`, `locale`, `io.StringIO`/`BytesIO` and `unittest.mock`. It also handles a `with` statement that holds `subTest`/`assertRaises`/`assertWarns` together, `self.skipTest` and `self.addCleanup`, tests with default arguments, `exec` strings that call `self.assert*`, `from test.re_tests import ...` inside a test, `test.support.warnings_helper`, and `test_grammar`'s `typinganndata` modules, which are copied as pinned companion files. Core and stdlib now have no test left as not extractable (0 of 35, down from 35). The newly measured tests turned up 4 wrong answers in stdlib (#477 to #480), and they are tracked on the known-wrong list. A test CPython itself skips on the reference build (a missing locale, or no `_testcapi`) is excluded with that reason. This moves tests out of the denominator: excluded goes from 37 to 40 in core and from 142 to 150 in stdlib, and by design goes from 25 to 32 in core and from 3 to 4 in stdlib. Part of the score rise comes from that, not from fixes. Tests of CPython's own machinery are excluded with a stated reason: subprocess crash checks, `multiprocessing`, the deprecated `sre_*` modules, and `_testinternalcapi` recursion depth. Tests that call CPython-private helpers (`re._compile`, `functools._c3_mro`, and 4 others) are listed in `compat/conformance_cpython_private.txt` (#481). They stay in the denominator as not matched, and their count is printed beside every score. `--check` fails on a stale line there, or on a line without an issue.
- Conformance tool: more CPython tests are extracted instead of reported not extractable. `self.enterContext(cm)` enters the manager and exits it after tearDown, as unittest does (22 test_set tests). An assertion method passed as a value (`helper(self.assertIs, x)`) prints what the inline rewrite prints. `type(self) in (SomeTestCase, ...)` is compared by class name. A decorator the test file defines at top level (test_enum's `reraise_if_not_enum`) is applied as written; `@unittest.skip` is a CPython skip; `threading_helper.reap_threads` is the identity on stdout; `python_version` skip conditions are decided when the file binds it as `sys.version_info[:2]`. test_dataclasses: its companion modules (dataclass_module_1/2 and _str, dataclass_textanno) are pinned and shipped with an empty stand-in package `__init__.py`, and a test whose strings name ClassVar, InitVar or KW_ONLY gets the file's own bindings of those names, which dataclasses reads to resolve string annotations.
- Conformance: the extractor now reads decorators the way CPython applies them. It decides `skipIf`/`skipUnless` with the reference interpreter, and it handles `assertWarns` blocks, `eq = self.assertEqual` aliases and helper assertions. The stdlib slice has 75 fewer not-extractable tests. It surfaced 29 tests Brevi gets wrong that were hidden before: itertools pickling, warnings re and int() don't raise, re.escape, namedtuple field docs. They are tracked in #396, #400, #418 and #419.
- **Conformance extractor: two more kinds of functools test are scored.** A test class that sets several members inside one `if c_functools:` block used to keep only the last of them, so the generated test failed under CPython. Every member is now picked. A nested method decorated with `@self.module.lru_cache(...)` now keeps the test's `self`. This scores 5 more functools tests: 4 match and 1 is wrong (`partial` pickling, #446).
- **Conformance: functools's pure-Python copies are scored.** `import_helper.import_fresh_module('functools', ...)` in CPython's tests now becomes `import functools as ...` in the extracted script, so the 11 `cached_property` tests are built and scored instead of failing to extract.
- **Conformance gates.** Every known-wrong line in `compat/conformance_known_wrong.txt` and `compat/conformance_stdlib_known_wrong.txt` now names a tracking issue, an owner and an expiry date, and `tools/conformance.py --check` fails on a line without them or past its date. New `--release-gate` fails below an absolute release bar (core 90%, stdlib 80%, zero known-wrong). Reports add assertion-bearing coverage and the typed-path share.
- **Conformance: mixin test classes are scored.** CPython runs many functools tests through mixins (`class TestPartialC(TestPartial, unittest.TestCase)`), and the extractor used to skip their inherited tests without counting them. They are now extracted against the module's C copy. The pure-Python twins are excluded, since Brevi has one copy of each module and the C twin already scores it. This scores 91 more functools tests and shows 51 wrong results that had been hidden, now tracked as #446, #447 and #448.
- **Conformance: comparing modules.** An `assertEqual` with a module on either side is now extracted as an equality check, not as two printed reprs: a module's repr names the file it was loaded from, which differs between machines. `test_collections.TestNamedTuple.test_module_parameter` now matches.
- Conformance fixes in the dynamic build, each matching CPython 3.13: `itertools.count(start=, step=)` takes its arguments by keyword (#477; before, a keyword step was silently ignored); codec names follow CPython's lookup and alias table for ascii, utf-8 and latin-1 (`ANSI_X3.4-1968`, `us`, `cp367`, `iso_8859_1:1987`, ...) in `bytes.decode`, `str.encode` and `open()` (#478); a program may assign `sys.stdout` (`sys.stdout = io.StringIO()`, `setattr(sys, "stdout", ...)`) and `print()` and `input()` write to it, with `sys.__stdout__` for the real stream (#479; assigning `sys.stderr` or `sys.stdin`, or `sys.stdout` in a program that runs a library in CPython, is refused); `print(file=obj)` calls `obj.write()` once per piece and `obj.flush()` for `flush=True`, as CPython does; `functools.lru_cache` hashes each call's key exactly once (#480); `operator.length_hint` is supported (refused for an iterator whose remaining count Brevi cannot show); `int(b"...", base)` works and a byte past 0x7f is never read as part of a number.
- Typed build: an undefined name inside `__del__` is no longer reported as a sure mistake (CPython ignores errors raised there), and `.dispatch()` / `.registry` of a `functools.singledispatch` function are known methods, so such programs build over dynamic values instead of being refused.
- **Conformance: a test that needs the string model is named, not hidden.** `test_re`'s `test_bigcharset` needs a str holding a lone surrogate, which Brevi's strings cannot hold yet (#450), so it stops with a `NotImplementedError` instead of giving a wrong answer. It is listed in `compat/conformance_needs_string_model.txt` and counted as not matched, never as matched. Its count is printed beside every score. Only that exact stop, seen in both builds for a listed test, counts this way, and `--check` fails on a stale line or a line without an issue.
- Conformance tool: one disturbed CPython reference run no longer moves the score. A reference run that timed out, hit the memory limit or died on a signal is tried again and, if it fails twice, reported as not extractable ("CPython reference run failed at PYTHONHASHSEED=N"), never as unstable. Before a test is classed unstable, each seed's output must be reproduced by a second run at that seed: rule 1 (seeds 1 and 2 differ) and rule 2 (seeds 3 to 5, tried for a wrong result) both say which rule and seeds fired, and report.md lists every unstable test by name. On 11-14 a single disturbed run had dropped two deterministic tests (test_graphlib, test_re) from the stdlib score.
- The CPython conformance slices grow by five whole CPython v3.13.13 test files that already pass with no wrong result: core adds `test_contains`, `test_binop` and `test_richcmp`, and stdlib adds `test_graphlib` and `test_colorsys` (pinned by SHA-256 like the others, and moved out of `tools/conformance_candidates.json`). The scored totals change: core grows from 276 to 302 scored tests and stdlib from 634 to 655. The two tests in `test_binop` and `test_richcmp` that the extractor cannot run yet, and one `test_graphlib` test that matches only through `pybridge`, stay in the denominator as not matched. `test_keyword` stays a candidate: its normal build matches natively in only 2 of 11 tests.
- Coroutines in dynamic builds follow CPython's object protocol, measured with CPython's `test_coroutines` (62 wrong answers there before, 0 now; 63 of 89 tests match): `types.CoroutineType`, `GeneratorType`, `AsyncGeneratorType` and `FunctionType` are the program's own classes, so `type(c) is types.CoroutineType` holds; coroutines have `__class__`, `cr_suspended`, a dotted `__qualname__` and `__await__()` (the `coroutine_wrapper` iterator); sending to or throwing into a finished coroutine is `RuntimeError`, re-entering a running one is `ValueError`, a `StopIteration` raised inside one becomes `RuntimeError` (PEP 479), and a coroutine that was never awaited issues CPython's `RuntimeWarning`. `async with` and `async for` raise CPython's errors for a bad context manager or `__anext__` result, and `copy.copy` of a coroutine is `TypeError`.
- An exception the runtime raises inside an `except` block (`1/0`, `[][1]`) now has the handled exception as its `__context__`, as in CPython.
- Refused when the program is built (dynamic builds): a coroutine's or async generator's `cr_frame`, `cr_code`, `cr_await`, `cr_origin`, `ag_frame`, `ag_code`, `ag_await`, and `types.coroutine` in any use but one.
- `@types.coroutine` as the innermost decorator of a generator function works natively, as in CPython: the function comes back itself with `CO_ITERABLE_COROUTINE` set, its generators can be awaited and can `yield from` a coroutine, and they are not `Awaitable` or `Coroutine` instances. Awaiting a coroutine that is suspended part-way raises CPython's `RuntimeError` ("coroutine is being awaited already").
- **Fixed: Brevi uses only CPython 3.13.** Brevi answers as CPython 3.13 does, but a program that bridges to a Python library loaded whatever `python3` was on PATH, so with a 3.14 or 3.15 there some library calls answered as that version does (bare `pickle.dumps` wrote protocol 5). Now every CPython Brevi asks or runs (the bridge's libpython, the build's questions about modules and packages, the fallback, `brevi verify`, brevi-python and `py` functions) is a CPython 3.13: `$BREVI_PYTHON` when set, else the first 3.13 among `python3`, `python3.13` and `python` on PATH, the `py` launcher (Windows) or `uv python find 3.13`. Any other version is refused with an error that says how to install 3.13, never used. A built program checks the libpython it loads too, and refuses one that is not 3.13.
- Dynamic builds have CPython's private helpers that CPython's own tests call: `collections._count_elements`, `functools._c3_mro`, `_compose_mro` and `_find_impl`, `re._compile` and `re._compiler._generate_overlap_table` (#481). The six stdlib conformance tests that used them now match CPython 3.13.
- A compiled `re` pattern can go through `pickle` (it pickles as CPython's `re._compile(pattern, flags)` and comes back as the program's own pattern).
- A `Counter` subclass's `__init__`, `update` and `subtract` call the subclass's own `get`, `__setitem__` and `update`, as CPython's Counter does.
- `functools.singledispatch` follows CPython's dispatch cache: it is made by `weakref.WeakKeyDictionary` when singledispatch runs (which a program may assign), and an ABC registration clears it only once the function has an ABC registered. `functools._c3_mro` no longer lets one base's ABC list changes reach its sibling bases.
- Fixed a crash at exit when a library value CPython runs was dropped after the program's output buffer.
- Dynamic builds hash `str` and `bytes` exactly as CPython 3.13 does (SipHash-1-3 over the PEP 393 form), keyed from `PYTHONHASHSEED` as CPython reads it (unset, empty or `random`: a random key; `0`: the all-zero key; an invalid value stops at startup with CPython's fatal error). At a fixed seed, `hash()` of a string and the order of a set of strings match `python3`. Under `--sandbox` the key is CPython's for `PYTHONHASHSEED=0`.
- The typed build no longer walks a set of strings in its own order: such programs take the dynamic build, whose order is CPython's.
- Conformance runs CPython and both builds at `PYTHONHASHSEED=0`, so tests whose output depends on string hashes are scored.
- Dynamic builds lay out a set literal of constants as CPython 3.13 does: the frozenset constant is rebuilt once more when one of its strings is already interned (a name in the module, or one of the strings CPython interns at startup, `tools/gen_interned.py`), and `for`, comprehensions and `in` use that frozenset itself. Bytes and complex literals (`2j`, `1+2j`) are constants too; a set of folded constant expressions (`{1+1, 2, 3}`) is refused at build time, and so is a set literal of 2 or more constants in an imported module whose order the program walks when that order depends on the strings CPython interned before importing it (some seed always reorders it); in a library module Brevi copies (jinja2), the program stops with "not supported" instead, only at a fixed `PYTHONHASHSEED` where the two possible orders differ.
- Dynamic builds: `d.fromkeys(...)` on an instance of a dict subclass builds that subclass. `hash()` of a compiled `re` pattern is refused at build time when the build can see it (CPython hashes the pattern over its sre code), and stops with "not supported" otherwise.
- Dataclasses in dynamic builds: a bare `ClassVar` annotation no longer becomes a field; `ClassVar[C]` and `Final[C]` over a class print, compare and hash as CPython's (`typing.ClassVar[int]`), so class annotations with them can be read and `make_dataclass` takes them; every dataclass has `__replace__`, as in Python 3.13; `class C(*bases)` builds; `copy.copy` and `copy.deepcopy` of a class with `__slots__` and its own `__setattr__` set the slots through it (a frozen dataclass with its own `__slots__` raises FrozenInstanceError, as in CPython).
- A Python file that calls a method named only in a string (`make_dataclass(..., namespace={'add_one': ...})`) falls back to the dynamic build instead of stopping with "no Python type has a method".
- Dynamic builds: `@dataclass` now follows CPython 3.13's dataclasses.py step by step. Fields read the class's evaluated annotations (Field.type, a real Field repr, the generated `__doc__` such as `C(x: int, y: List[int] = <factory>)`), ClassVar pseudo-fields are kept in `__dataclass_fields__`, `InitVar[T]` has its `.type` and repr, `__dataclass_params__` and `match_args=False` work, generated methods carry the class's qualified name, a recursive repr prints `...`, class dicts stay exact, and the frozen, mutable-default, unannotated-field, KW_ONLY and ClassVar checks raise CPython's errors. `asdict`/`astuple` take `dict_factory`/`tuple_factory` and keep defaultdict, OrderedDict and namedtuple types. `slots=True` makes a new class through type(), as CPython does. Programs that call `inspect.signature` (CPython's inspect cannot read a dynamic build's functions), or read a dataclass `__doc__` that only CPython can write, are refused at build time instead of failing at run time.
- Dynamic builds: `dataclasses.make_dataclass` works natively; `inspect.isabstract` reads a class's abstract methods; a dataclass on an ABC keeps the right `__abstractmethods__`.
- Dynamic builds: `slots=True` dataclasses treat an inherited slot as no default, honour `weakref_slot=True` (`weakref.ref`, `obj.__weakref__`), and refuse weak references to slotted instances without `__weakref__`, as CPython does.
- Dynamic builds: `super().__delattr__`, `C.__hash__ is object.__hash__`, `field(default=MISSING, default_factory=...)`, "cannot unpack non-iterable X object", and CPython 3.13's "Did you mean" for `dataclass()`, `field()` and `make_dataclass()` keywords match CPython.
- Dynamic builds refuse at build time (instead of stopping at run time): pickling the program's dataclasses, a `unittest.mock` object stored on the program's class, `field(metadata=...)` with the program's own mapping class, and subscripting a class built on a subscripted generic class.
- **Fixed two silent wrong answers in the dynamic build** (#421): `datetime.datetime` is now a subclass of `datetime.date` (`isinstance`, `issubclass`, `__mro__`, as in CPython), and library types named by a dotted CPython type name report CPython's `__name__`, `__qualname__` and `__module__` (`type(date(2020, 1, 1)).__name__` is `date` and its `__module__` is `datetime`; before, `datetime.date` and `builtins`). Error messages keep the dotted name, as CPython's do.
- Dynamic build: a function's `__annotations__` now holds its evaluated annotations (as CPython 3.13 evaluates them when the `def` runs), and lambdas give `{}`. `functools.wraps`/`update_wrapper` copy every attribute CPython copies (`WRAPPER_ASSIGNMENTS` and `WRAPPER_UPDATES` are exposed), builtins carry CPython's docstrings, `classmethod`/`staticmethod` objects expose `__func__`, and `contextlib.contextmanager` keeps the wrapped function's metadata. A module's `__annotations__` is refused rather than guessed.
- Dynamic builds: `collections.deque` follows CPython's rules: iterators, `in`, `count`, `index` and `remove` raise when the deque changes under them; `+`, `*` and `*=` keep `maxlen`; `<`/`<=`/`>`/`>=` compare deques; `insert` into a full deque raises `IndexError`; `index` takes start and stop; `__init__`, `__getitem__`, `__copy__` and the other dunders exist; argument errors match CPython's.
- Dynamic builds: a deque holding itself prints `[...]` instead of overflowing the stack; a deque subclass prints its own class name; `deque` reports module `collections`; `id()` of a deque, set, iterator or function is its own address (it was 1 for all of them); deques take weak references.
- Typed builds: `insert` on a queue clamps an index past either end, as `list.insert` does.
- Dynamic builds: `pickle.dumps` and `pickle.dump` take deques, their iterators and instances of deque subclasses (with their `__dict__` and `__slots__` values), in CPython's own `deque.__reduce__` form, and `pickle.loads` gives them back as the program's own (a deque holding itself still holds itself). Any other library still refuses a deque, since it would get a copy.
