Pickling a subclass that defines its own `__reduce__`, `__getstate__`, `extend` or `append`, or an iterator over a subclass instance, stops with a clear "not supported" message.
- Dynamic builds: `subclass * n`, `n * subclass` and `subclass + d` of a deque subclass give an instance of the subclass, `maxlen` of a subclass instance works, and `str()` of one is its repr, as in CPython.
- Typed builds: `list.insert` in a `.py` file clamps an index past either end, as CPython does (it stopped with "index out of range").
- Conformance: CPython's `test_deque` joins the stdlib slice (every scored test matches, no wrong result; `test_repr` runs `eval` and is listed as refused by design).
- Dynamic builds follow the descriptor protocol for the program's own classes, as CPython does: an object whose class defines `__get__` and is stored on a class is asked for the attribute (`obj.x` calls `__get__(obj, cls)`, `Cls.x` calls `__get__(None, cls)`), one whose class also defines `__set__` or `__delete__` handles assignment and `del` and wins over the instance's own attribute, and `__set_name__(owner, name)` is called when the class is made. Before, the descriptor object itself was returned and `__set_name__` was never called: a silent wrong answer.
- Dynamic builds: an enum class's `__dict__` and `dir()` of a mixed-in enum member match CPython 3.13 (also inside `__init_subclass__`); `Enum.__format__` and `Enum.__reduce_ex__` exist; IntFlag operators accept another IntFlag's members, `~` returns existing members, a Flag member whose value is None raises CPython's TypeErrors, and `enum.IntFlag('X', [])` works through the module.
- Dynamic builds: `staticmethod` and `classmethod` are types (`type(C.__dict__['s']) is staticmethod`, `isinstance`), and `C.s` gives the wrapped function; subclassing either is refused at build time.
- Dynamic builds: an Enum may take `datetime.date` as its data type (`class Holiday(date, Enum)`, `Enum('E', ..., type=date)`); any other instance of a date subclass, and date arithmetic or `replace()` on such a member, is refused. Enum also gains `_add_alias_` and `_add_value_alias_`, CPython 3.13's checks on inheritance order and on extending an enum with members, `_missing_` errors chained to the ValueError, iteration over a class while its members are made, and members whose name a base's property or DynamicClassAttribute uses. `vars()` with no argument is refused at build time.
- Dynamic builds follow enum.py 3.13's class-body rules: a member name bound twice, or bound over a method or property, raises TypeError; `_ignore_` names leave the class; a `functools.partial` in the body warns FutureWarning and is not a member. Namedtuple values are unpacked into `__new__`, a dataclass data type gives members its field repr, and a data type's own `__repr__`/`__str__`/`__format__` gives way to the Enum's. Fixed for every class: `hasattr()` on a builtin value only finds the dunders CPython's type has (`hasattr(frozenset(), '__delete__')` was True), `super().__new__` in an int subclass reaches `int.__new__`, and a body's `__qualname__ = ...` names the class. Refused at build time: a metaclass built on `type(SomeEnum)`, and `X.__new__(...)` in an Enum method other than `__new__`; `type()` of an Enum class is refused at run time.
- Dynamic builds: the enum functional API (`Enum('Color', 'RED GREEN')`, lists, pairs, dicts, `start=`, `type=`, `module=`, `qualname=`), `enum.property`, `ReprEnum` and `_member_type_` now build natively; a class's own `_missing_` and `_generate_next_value_` are used; `Enum.name`/`Enum.value` are read-only descriptors as in CPython, and "is not a valid" errors name the class's `__qualname__`.
- Fixed a wrong answer: `locals().update(...)` (or any write through `locals()`) in an Enum's class body skipped the duplicate-name check, so a member set twice made the class where CPython raises `TypeError: 'A' already defined as 1`. In an Enum's body `locals()` is `enum.EnumDict`; Brevi's namespace is a plain dict, so the dynamic build now refuses `locals()` in the body of a class that may be an Enum (any base or metaclass other than a builtin the program does not rebind). Such a program runs in CPython instead (or, with `--strict`, does not build).
- Dynamic build Enums make their members as CPython does: a class's own `__new__` (with `object.__new__(cls)` or `int.__new__(cls, x)`), `_value_ = data_type(*args)` for int, str and float mixins, `__init__` on aliases too, and `super().__new__` in `__new__` raising CPython's TypeError. Reassigning or deleting a member, `mro` or an unknown `_sunder_` name as a member, and a mismatched `_order_` raise CPython's errors; `reversed()`, `copy.copy`/`deepcopy`, `dir()` of an enum class or member, and `format()`/`str.format` of a mixin member with its own `__str__` match CPython.
- `object.__str__(x)` calls `type(x).__repr__`, as in CPython (it gave the default object repr), and `int.__new__(cls, x)` (also float, str, tuple, bytes, frozenset) makes an instance of the subclass.
- A class built on `datetime.date`, `datetime`, `time`, `timedelta` or `timezone`, or `Enum(..., type=date)`, is refused at build time: the dynamic build's instances of such a class held no date and gave wrong answers.
- Dynamic builds: `enum.EnumType` (and `EnumMeta`) is `type()` of every Enum class; `member()` and `nonmember()` in class bodies; `verify()` with `UNIQUE`, `CONTINUOUS` and `NAMED_FLAGS`; `STRICT`, `CONFORM`, `EJECT`, `KEEP` and `FlagBoundary` as CPython's members; `show_flag_values`, `_iter_bits_lsb` and the `_is_*` helpers. A memberless `class S(metaclass=EnumMeta): pass` works as an Enum base; anything else it would do, and subclassing EnumType, is refused.
- Dynamic builds: an Enum member pickles and unpickles as CPython's does (its `__reduce_ex__`, so `pickle.loads(pickle.dumps(Color.RED)) is Color.RED` at every protocol), `float.hex()` and `float.fromhex()` work (correctly rounded, as CPython's), and `int.from_bytes` / `float.fromhex` called on a subclass (an `IntEnum`, a float-mixin Enum, any `int` or `float` subclass) return an instance of that subclass, as CPython does (they returned a plain `int`, or failed).
- Dynamic build: module, class and def annotations are evaluated as CPython 3.13 evaluates them, so `x: undefined_name` raises NameError instead of passing silently; subscripts of classes and typing forms (`int[str]`, `Dict[str]`, `Callable[int]`, `Union[()]`, `G[int, str]` on a one-parameter Generic) raise CPython's TypeError, and those Brevi cannot check exactly stop as not supported.
- Typed build: a file where an annotation CPython evaluates could raise (`def f(a: Later)` above `class Later`, `o: Vec` inside class Vec's own body, a name bound on only some paths, `Callable[int]`, `"x" | int`) now goes to the dynamic build, which raises as CPython does, instead of printing a result CPython never reaches. The check is in src/ann_guard.rs, with CPython 3.13's subscript rules in src/pynames/ann_types.txt (tools/gen_ann_types.py).
- Brevi's pydantic: a model's annotations are evaluated through its field descriptors (as before), and an annotation outside a model that subscripts or combines a pydantic type or a descriptor alias stops as not supported rather than being evaluated on values that are not CPython's typing objects.
- The conformance floor files now say who raises them: main, after each merge.
- Dynamic builds have `functools.cached_property` (CPython's semantics: cached in the instance's `__dict__` under the name from `__set_name__`, subclassable, CPython's errors for a missing `__dict__`, a reused property and a missing `__set_name__`) and `functools.RLock`. test_functools: 10 more TestCachedProperty tests match.
- Dynamic builds have `functools.partialmethod`, with CPython 3.13's semantics: it binds through the wrapped object's `__get__` when that makes something new (a classmethod, staticmethod or nested partialmethod), otherwise it binds as an instance method. Its repr, `__partialmethod__` and `__isabstractmethod__` match, and so does the TypeError for something that is neither callable nor a descriptor. Functions and static/class methods have `__get__` as in CPython. Written by DeepSeek and reviewed. test_functools: 4 more TestPartialMethod tests match.
- Dynamic build: PEP 479 is enforced for generators. A StopIteration that escapes a generator's body or a generator expression (raised, from `next()`, or thrown in) now raises `RuntimeError("generator raised StopIteration")` chained from it, as CPython does. Before, the generator ended silently, a wrong answer. Async generators already raised the RuntimeError; it now carries the original as `__cause__` and `__context__` too, so `contextlib` sees the exception it threw.
- The endless-generator rewrite (a seeded generator read only with `next()`, built typed as a class) is skipped when its loop could raise StopIteration: a call other than a few pure builtins, an attribute or item read, a class with dunder methods, or an import of the program's own modules. Those programs keep CPython's answer.
- **The grammar check parses only programs that changed.** `tools/grammar_check.py` remembers every program that parsed, keyed by the grammar, Lark's version and the program's text, in `$BREVI_CACHE/grammar-ok`. A rerun, or a pull request reusing the daily run's cache, parses only new or changed programs: a full check took about 60 s on 4 cores and 1.5 to 2 min on GitHub, and a repeat takes under a second. `BREVI_GRAMMAR_CACHE=0` parses everything.
- `id()` of a float is no longer always 1 (`id(1.5) == id(2.5)` was True): each float value has its own id, and `id()` agrees with `is` for every kind of value in the dynamic build (ints of any size, floats, the empty str and 1-char ASCII strs, the empty tuple). `id()` of a NaN that has no identity of its own refuses instead of answering.
- `is` holds between a value and itself for every int (`x = 1000; x is x` was False), big ints, bound methods, ranges, slices, dict views and properties.
- The typed build no longer answers `is` by comparing values (`[1] is [1]` was True): a file that uses `is` with anything other than None, True, False or Ellipsis goes to the dynamic build.
- Dynamic build: `int()` of an object whose `__int__` or `__index__` returns a strict subclass of int (such as `bool`) gives a plain int with CPython 3.13's DeprecationWarning, where it used to return the subclass instance silently. `int()` delegating to `__trunc__` issues its DeprecationWarning too, and a `__trunc__` result that is not an integer is CPython's TypeError.
- Dynamic build: `copy.copy()` and `copy.deepcopy()` of a list, tuple, str, range or reversed-list iterator give a new iterator at the same place, as CPython's do. They used to return the same iterator, so advancing the copy advanced the original. Copying a `map`, `zip`, itertools, dict or set iterator is refused for now rather than shared.
- Dynamic build: a class built on a builtin iterator type (`zip`, `map`, `filter`, `enumerate`, `reversed`, or an itertools type such as `chain` or `repeat`) is refused at build time. This includes a base chosen at run time in a program that uses those types as values. It used to stop at run time with "bases must be types" (#398).
- **Pickling the program's iterators** (lists, tuples, strings, ranges, dicts, dict views, sets) now works when they are passed to `pickle`, with the same bytes CPython writes for list, tuple, str and dict iterators. Iterators whose CPython pickle form Brevi cannot reproduce (`reversed`, `map`, `zip`, generators) raise `TypeError: cannot pickle` instead of being saved as a plain list iterator. `next()` on a library value that is not an iterator raises CPython's TypeError. Written by DeepSeek, reviewed by Claude.
- **`type()` of an iterator names CPython's class** in the dynamic build: `list_iterator`, `str_ascii_iterator`, `dict_keyiterator`, `list_reverseiterator`, `map`, `zip`, `enumerate`, `generator`, `itertools.chain` and the other itertools types, a user iterator's own class, and, for a value from CPython, the type CPython reports. `type(a) is type(b)` holds for two iterators of one kind. An iterator whose class Brevi cannot name raises an error from `type()` instead of guessing, and `isinstance` of it against any other class is `False`, as in CPython. Written by DeepSeek, reviewed and fixed by Claude.
- Dynamic build: itertools iterators (`chain`, `cycle`, `count`, `repeat`, `islice`, `accumulate`, `starmap`, `takewhile`, `dropwhile`, `compress`, `filterfalse`, `zip_longest`, `groupby` and its groups, `product`, `combinations`, `combinations_with_replacement`, `permutations`), and `map`, `filter`, `zip` and `enumerate`, now pickle, copy and deep-copy as in CPython 3.13. `__reduce__()` returns CPython's tuple, `__setstate__()` restores state, and a pickle loads back as Brevi's own iterator of the same type, including pickles CPython made (`itertools.cycle` pickles from older Pythons). Each itertools `__reduce__` and `__setstate__` issues CPython 3.13's DeprecationWarning at the line CPython names: the program's line for pickle, and `copy.py`'s line for `copy.copy` and `copy.deepcopy`. `copy.copy` and `copy.deepcopy` of a list, tuple, str, range, dict or set iterator now make an independent copy instead of returning the same iterator (a silent wrong answer before). Copying a generator raises CPython's `TypeError`, and other iterators are refused. `warnings.catch_warnings(record=True)` is no longer refused in programs that pickle or copy itertools iterators. Module functions such as `operator.pow` pickle by reference. `SystemError` is a builtin, and generators take weak references.
- Dynamic build: itertools constructors check their arguments as CPython's argument clinic does, so too many arguments, an unknown keyword or one given twice raise `TypeError` (`groupby('abc', key, 10)` and `permutations('abc', 2, 1)` returned an iterator before). `combinations` and `combinations_with_replacement` take `iterable=` and `r=` by keyword.
- **`itertools.tee` and `itertools.batched`** in the dynamic build, as in CPython 3.13: independent tee iterators over one source that keep only the values some live member has not read yet (lockstep reading and deleting a tee free memory), `RuntimeError` on re-entry, copies at the same position, a tee of a tee making new members of its group, an iterator with `__copy__` copied rather than buffered, and `type()` naming `itertools._tee`; `batched(iterable, n, *, strict=False)` lazily, with CPython's argument errors and its `incomplete batch` error. Written by DeepSeek, reviewed and fixed by Claude.
- Dynamic build: `itertools.tee` follows CPython's linked 57-value blocks, so a tee can be pickled, deep-copied and restored with `__setstate__`, with CPython's results and DeprecationWarnings (`itertools._tee_dataobject` included).
- Dynamic build: `zip`, `map`, `filter`, `enumerate`, `reversed` and the itertools iterator types are classes, as in CPython: `type(chain([])) is chain` is now True (it was False), and classes can be built on them (`__new__`, `__init__` with extra keywords, `chain.from_iterable` on a subclass). Copying or pickling an instance of such a subclass is refused rather than guessed, and non-subclassable iterator types are refused as bases with CPython's message.
- Dynamic build: `operator.is_` and `operator.is_not`; `zip()` now raises TypeError for an unknown keyword argument.
- The MCP `limits` descriptions and the docs now agree on where each `--limit` is enforced (`mem` is Linux and Windows, not macOS; `cpu`, `file` and `fds` are Linux and macOS; `threads` is Linux), a table in `docs/SUPPORT_MATRIX.md` lists them, and CI (`tools/limits_check.py`) keeps them in step with the code.
- Dynamic builds: a str with lone surrogates can be encoded, printed and handed to CPython, with CPython's answers (#450, step 4). `str.encode`, `bytes(s, ...)` and `bytearray(s, ...)` take the `surrogatepass`, `surrogateescape`, `backslashreplace` and `xmlcharrefreplace` error handlers for utf-8, ascii and latin-1, and raise `UnicodeEncodeError` with CPython's message, range and `encoding`/`object`/`start`/`end`/`reason` attributes. `bytes.decode` takes `surrogateescape` and `surrogatepass`. `print` and `sys.stdout.write` raise `UnicodeEncodeError` for a surrogate stdout cannot encode (stdout uses `surrogateescape` under the C locale and `strict` under another UTF-8 locale, as python3's does; under `--sandbox`, which reads no environment, such a print stops instead), and `sys.stderr` writes one as `\udXXX`. `json.dumps(ensure_ascii=False)` and lone dict keys, `re.escape` and `re.Scanner` work with lone strs. A lone str passes to and from a library CPython runs.
- `print` writes the pieces before an argument whose `str()` raises, as CPython does.
- Dynamic builds: `re` works over code points, lone surrogates included, as CPython's sre does: subjects, patterns and replacement templates may hold U+D800..U+DFFF, `\ud800` escapes and ranges over the surrogate block work, and a surrogate is never a word, digit or space character and folds to itself under IGNORECASE. `json.loads` keeps a lone `\ud800` escape as a lone surrogate and `json.dumps` (ensure_ascii) writes one back as an escape. This makes `test_re`'s `test_bigcharset` match CPython (#450, step 3).
- `re` patterns with a `\ud800`..`\udfff` escape compile, as in CPython, instead of raising `bad escape`.
- Dynamic builds: a str can now hold a lone surrogate (U+D800..U+DFFF), as in CPython, from a literal such as `'123\ud800'` or from `chr(0xD800)`. `repr()`, `ascii()`, `%r`, `!r`, `==`, `<`, `sorted()`, `hash()` (dict and set keys), `len()`, `in`, `+`, `ord()`, `bool()` and `int()`'s ValueError handle such strs. Every other operation on one stops the program with a `NotImplementedError` that no `except` can catch, never a different answer; the next steps are in handoffs/string-model-design.md (#450). This makes `test_int`'s `test_error_message` match CPython.
- A docstring holding a lone surrogate is refused at build time in dynamic builds.
- Dynamic builds: text operations on a str holding a lone surrogate now match CPython: indexing, slicing, iteration, `*`, `str.join`, `%` formatting (`%s`, `%c` with a surrogate code point), `str.format`/`format_map`, `format()` and f-strings, and the str methods `split`, `strip`, `replace`, `find`/`index`/`count`, `startswith`/`endswith`, `partition`, `splitlines`, `center`/`ljust`/`rjust`/`zfill`, `expandtabs`, `removeprefix`/`removesuffix`, the case mappings and every `isX()` predicate. `encode`, `translate`, `re` and printing such a str still stop the program (#450, step 2).
- `'%c'` of an int outside range(0x110000) raises OverflowError, as in CPython, instead of giving `'?'`; `'%c'` of a str longer than one character raises TypeError.
- `str.replace` returns the str itself when there is nothing to replace, as CPython does.
- Dynamic build: a hit in a bounded `functools.lru_cache` no longer changes the cache's dict (it only moves the result to most recently used), so a key whose `__eq__` calls the cached function again works as in CPython instead of raising `RuntimeError: dict mutated during lookup` (#451).
- Python `match` statements (PEP 634) now build natively, over dynamic values: literal, capture, wildcard, value, sequence (with `*rest`), mapping (with `**rest`), class (with `__match_args__`), or-patterns, `as` and guards. Checks follow CPython's order (length checks, subscripts or unpacking, `get()`, attribute lookups), names are bound only when the whole pattern matches, and CPython's TypeErrors and ValueErrors for bad `__match_args__`, repeated attributes and duplicate keys are raised. A pattern CPython's compiler rejects (an unreachable case, a name bound twice, alternatives binding different names) refuses the build. CPython's test_patma goes from 0 to 266 of 323 tests matched with no wrong answers; the rest call compile() or sys.settrace.
- Fixed: a class built on dict or list and a collections.abc ABC (`class M(dict, Sequence)`) no longer fails to instantiate as abstract or raises "cannot be both Py_TPFLAGS_SEQUENCE and Py_TPFLAGS_MAPPING", and `isinstance(x, P)` for a Protocol class made inside a function is refused at build time instead of answering False.
- Dynamic build: metaclasses. `class M(type)` and `class C(metaclass=M)` work with CPython's class creation order (`__prepare__`, `__new__`, `__init__`), the most derived metaclass and its conflict error, metaclass methods, properties and descriptors on classes, and `==`, `hash`, `len`, `bool`, `repr` and `str` of classes through their metaclass. Hooks not built yet (`__call__`, `__getattr__`, `__instancecheck__` and other dunders) are refused at build time. `abc.ABCMeta` can be subclassed and read; making a class with such a subclass is refused.
- Dynamic build: PEP 695 `def f[T]`, with `TypeVar` objects in `__type_params__`. Generic classes, the `type` statement, bounds and defaults are refused at build time.
- Dynamic build: typing forms as values. `int | str` (`types.UnionType`), `typing.List[int]` and the other subscripted aliases, and `typing.Union`/`Optional` have CPython's reprs, equality and errors, and `functools.singledispatch.register` takes class unions and rejects subscripted forms with CPython's message.
- Fixed a wrong answer: `super().__new__(cls)` in a class's `__new__` passed `cls` twice.
- Dynamic build: annotated assignments follow CPython's compiler: `(x): int` with no value binds nothing (before, it made `x` a local), and `o.a: int` / `o[i]: int` with no value evaluate `o` and `i` (before, nothing was evaluated).
- Dynamic builds: a program module's repr is CPython's (`<module 'helper' from '/abs/helper.py'>`), and its `__file__`, `__name__`, `__doc__` and `__package__` read as CPython's, where they raised AttributeError before; a module's own `__doc__` and `__package__` names work too. Its `__spec__`, `__loader__`, `__cached__`, `__dict__`, `__builtins__` and a package's `__path__` are refused. A library module's repr, which printed `<module 'math'>`, is now the one the build machine's CPython prints (`<module 'math' (built-in)>`, `<module 'json' from '...'>`). The reprs of `sys.stdout` and `decimal.getcontext()` are refused when the build sees them and stop the program otherwise. On Windows `__file__` no longer starts with `\\?\`.
- A Python program that imports a module with top-level statements (a `print`, a loop, `__annotations__[1] = 2`) now builds natively over dynamic values, which run the module once on import as CPython does. Before, the normal build refused it with "a used file holds only fn, type, enum and test items" even though the dynamic build matched CPython. A `.bv` file's `use` keeps that rule.
- **Operators on objects run their dunders in CPython's order** in the dynamic build. For `a + b` (and every binary operator), the right operand's reflected method runs first when its class is a strict subclass of the left operand's class and gives that method a different value (its own or an inherited one), and it is not called a second time after `NotImplemented`; two operands of one class never try the reflected method. For `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`, a strict subclass on the right is asked first, each method runs once, and when every method answers `NotImplemented` the result is CPython's default (identity for `==`/`!=`, `TypeError` for ordering). `!=` without `__ne__` inverts `__eq__` per side, as `object.__ne__` does, and a `__ne__` returning `NotImplemented` is no longer taken as true. Before, these gave wrong answers or ran a user method twice.
- Dynamic build: `collections.OrderedDict` now behaves like CPython's in the cases that gave wrong answers. Iterating an OrderedDict that changes size or keys raises "OrderedDict mutated during iteration"; `==` between two OrderedDicts also compares the order; `reversed(od)` is live; `repr` gives `Name()` when empty, `...` for a recursive one, and raises KeyError for a key whose hash changed; views are `odict_keys`/`odict_values`/`odict_items`. `update()`, the constructor and `copy()` follow CPython's argument checks and look each key up again. A subclass's `copy()` builds `type(od)()` through its own `__getitem__`/`__setitem__`, and `str`/`print` of a subclass name the subclass. `|`, `|=`, `fromkeys(value=...)`, `__reduce__` and instance `__dict__` work. Pickling an OrderedDict now gives back an OrderedDict in its order (before, a plain dict).
- Dynamic build: calling dict's own methods on an OrderedDict (`dict.pop(od, k)`, `dict.__setitem__(od, k, v)` and the like) is refused with a clear error. In CPython those calls leave the OrderedDict's order out of step with its contents, so Brevi does not copy that behavior.
- Dynamic build: a `functools.partial` (or an instance of a subclass of it) pickles through its own `__reduce__` and loads back as the program's own partial, with its arguments, keywords and `__dict__` (#446). It used to fail with "cannot pickle 'PyCapsule' object".
- Dynamic build: `functools.partial` is a type, as in CPython 3.13 (#446). `isinstance(p, partial)` and `class S(partial)` work; `func`, `args` and `keywords` are read-only; a partial of a partial is flattened while its `__dict__` was never made; `repr`, `__reduce__`, `__setstate__` (with CPython's errors), `partial[int]` and `del p.__dict__` (refused with TypeError) match CPython. Before, partial was a plain function, so subclassing it failed and its attributes could not be read.
`repr(object())` reads `<object object at 0x...>` and a cmp_to_key key shows its address, as in CPython.
- Tests: the fallback tests no longer depend on how rustc words its first error, and the DeepSeek end-to-end test skips with a reason, instead of failing, where the model box cannot run (any non-root run). Local CI on a non-root machine is now clean.
- Dynamic builds take PEP 695 type parameters in full outside class bodies: `def f[T]`, `class C[T]` and `type X[T] = ...` with bounds, constraints, defaults, `*Ts` and `**P`, each evaluated lazily in its own annotation scope as CPython does; `typing.NoDefault`, and `isinstance(x, TypeVar)` (and `TypeVarTuple`, `ParamSpec`) on a PEP 695 parameter.
- A program with a class on `Generic` or `Protocol` (or `class C[T]`) is now refused, rather than answered without them, when it reads a class's `__bases__`, `__mro__`, `mro()`, `__orig_bases__` or `__dict__`, or calls `dir()` or `vars()`: the dynamic build leaves `Generic` and `Protocol` out of a class's bases.
- Dynamic build: `pickle.dumps` of a function or class the program defines (a def, a class, a method, a nested class, or one from a module of the program) now gives CPython's exact bytes at every protocol: a global (`__main__` and the qualified name, STACK_GLOBAL from protocol 4, `getattr` for a dotted name below it), the module name memoized once and the same object memoized when it comes again. Before, it was saved through a private `_brevi_dyn` helper, which printed different bytes, a silent wrong answer. A builtin class such as `int` or `list` now crosses into a library as CPython's own class, so `pickle.dumps(int)` is `builtins.int` too, and it comes back as the program's class. Loading a pickle that names a missing attribute of a module of the program raises CPython's AttributeError, naming the module with its path.
- Dynamic build: `pickle.dumps`, `pickle.dump` and `pickle.Pickler` default to CPython 3.13's protocol 4 (and `pickle.DEFAULT_PROTOCOL` is 4) even when the CPython on PATH is 3.14 or newer, whose default is 5.
- Dynamic build: `pickle.dumps` writes a repeated str, bytes, tuple or complex once and then refers to it, exactly as CPython does, at every protocol (before, `[s, s]` wrote `s` twice: different bytes). Object identity of these values now follows CPython 3.13: equal constants of a module are one object (with CPython's constant folding: `"a" + "b"`, `(1, 2) * 2`, `1 + 1` inside a tuple), and operations CPython answers with the same object (`s[:]`, `s + ""`, `"".join([s])`, `f"{s}"`, `format(s)`, `"%s" % s`, `s.split(sep)` with no match, the bytes equivalents, `t + ()`, `copy.deepcopy` of an atomic tuple) keep it, so `is` gives CPython's answer there too. A tuple of constants whose folding Brevi does not compute (`(2 ** 0.5, 1)`) is refused at build time in a program that pickles.
- Dynamic build: a `collections.defaultdict` or `Counter` crosses into a library as CPython's own type, so `pickle.dumps` gives CPython's bytes and `pickle.loads` gives back a defaultdict with its `default_factory` (before, it pickled as a plain dict and loaded as one: a KeyError on a missing key).
- Dynamic build: an instance of a `str`, `bytes` or `tuple` subclass no longer hands back the value it was made from or the value it wraps (`S(s)[:] is s`, `x[:] is x[:]`, `x.strip()`, `"" + x` were `True`; CPython makes a new object, and `x.partition(sep)` gives the instance itself). `sys.intern` keeps one str per text (a name-like constant of the program when there is one) and raises CPython's TypeErrors.
- Dynamic build: `functools.partial` (and subclasses) pickles with CPython's bytes, memoized by identity, and loads back as the program's partial; a module's function pickles under CPython's own module for it (`operator.add` as `_operator.add`) and loads back as the program's function.
- Dynamic build: `pickle.dumps`, `pickle.dump` and `pickle.Pickler` default to CPython 3.13's protocol 4 (and `pickle.DEFAULT_PROTOCOL` is 4) even when the CPython on PATH is 3.14 or newer, whose default is 5.
- Dynamic builds pickle the program's own functions and classes by reference, as CPython does: the pickle names the function's module and qualified name, dumping checks that the name finds the same object (a `PicklingError` or, for a def local to a function, an `AttributeError` otherwise), and loading gives back that very object. It used to fail with `TypeError: cannot pickle 'PyCapsule' object`. A builtin function or bound method of the program is refused with a clear error.
- `functools.total_ordering` fills in CPython's shared helpers (`_gt_from_lt` and the others, module `functools`), so `A.__gt__ is B.__gt__` holds as in CPython, and each helper looks the root comparison up on `type(self)` when called, so a subclass that overrides `__lt__` is used. Before, `__module__` read `__main__` and the root of the decorated class was always called.
- **Native engine libraries are pinned by SHA-256.** DuckDB's and ONNX Runtime's libraries (and, on Windows, Python's OpenSSL DLLs) are hashed when a program is built, and the program loads one only if its bytes still match, checked on the very bytes that get loaded (a sealed memory file on Linux, a private read-only copy on macOS, files and folders locked against change on Windows). A library swapped after the build never runs; after upgrading duckdb or onnxruntime, `brevi run` rebuilds by itself and a binary from `brevi build` must be built again.
- **No planted DLLs on Windows.** Every DLL Brevi or a program loads (CPython's, DuckDB's, ONNX Runtime's, OpenSSL's, a `BREVI_*_LIB` override) is loaded by an absolute path with its dependencies searched only in its own folder and System32, never the program's folder, the working folder or `PATH`.
- Dynamic builds: `isinstance()` and `case P():` class patterns with `typing.Protocol` classes follow CPython's `_ProtocolMeta` (the `@runtime_checkable` TypeError, structural member checks, "Protocols cannot be instantiated", protocols that inherit from non-protocols); `issubclass()` with a protocol is refused.
- Dynamic builds: `str(bytes, encoding[, errors])`, `sys.getdefaultencoding()`, and StrEnum or `(str, Enum)` members made from several arguments, with CPython's errors.
- Conformance: the `support.no_tracing` and `support.infinite_recursion(N)` test decorators are extracted.
- **pydantic 2.14 and openai 3.49.** Brevi's pydantic now answers as pydantic 2.14.3 (pydantic-core 2.50.4) does: validation errors link to `errors.pydantic.dev/2.14`, and `pydantic.VERSION` is `2.14.3`. Brevi's openai answers as openai 3.49.0: `openai.__version__` and the response types are regenerated from that SDK (one new model name in the Responses API's `model` literal). Pins, expected files and docs follow ([#243](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/243), [#293](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/293)).
- **`re` in the dynamic build matches bytes patterns** (they used to fall back to CPython). It follows CPython's rules: ASCII-only `\d\w\s\b` and case folding, `re.LOCALE` as the C locale (IGNORECASE sets test both cases of each byte), and bytes results from matches, `findall`, `split`, `sub`, `expand` and `escape`. Mixing str and bytes raises CPython's TypeErrors, and bad flag combinations raise its ValueErrors. `sub` with a template is faster on str as well. A program that uses `re` together with `array`, `mmap`, `ctypes` or `weakref` runs on CPython, which matches buffers and weak-references patterns.
- **`re.error` / `re.PatternError` is a real exception in the dynamic build**, with CPython 3.13's exact messages and its `msg`, `pattern`, `pos`, `lineno` and `colno` (the regex parser was rewritten for it), and `re.error(msg, pattern, pos)` can be raised by programs. Checked with a differential fuzz against CPython (5,000 random patterns, valid and invalid). Written by DeepSeek, reviewed by Sonnet and Claude.
- **Fixed silent wrong answers in `re`** (dynamic build) that the fuzz found: `re.IGNORECASE` on non-ASCII letters now follows CPython (`K` and the Kelvin sign, `ß`/`ẞ`, `ı`/`İ`, `ſ`, `ς`/`σ`, `re.A|re.I` no longer folds `é`), scoped `(?s:.)`, `(?m:^)`/`(?m:$)` and `(?a:\b)` keep their own flags, `\B` never matches in an empty string, `lastindex`/`lastgroup` name the group that closed last, verbose mode skips only ASCII whitespace (so `re.escape` of `\x85` and `\xa0` matches, #419), variable-width look-behind raises CPython's error instead of being searched, `(?a)` with `re.U` raises `ValueError`, `\)` and `\>` do not end a `(?#...)` comment or a group name. Fixed-width look-behind now tries one start instead of every earlier position, and a `sub` template is parsed once per call.
- **`str.isidentifier()` uses CPython 3.13's identifier tables** in the dynamic build (it took any letter or number, so `'a²'.isidentifier()` was `True`), and `re` group names are checked with it.
- **`re` in the dynamic build accepts `str` subclasses** as patterns and subjects (they raised `TypeError`), and a look-behind wider than 2**32-1 characters raises CPython's "looks too much behind".
- Dynamic build: `re._compiler.MAXREPEAT` and `re._compiler.MAXGROUPS` are supported, and `re._constants.MAXREPEAT` now matches CPython. It is a `_NamedIntConstant` with the value 4294967295 whose `str` and `repr` are `MAXREPEAT`, and it is the same object in both modules. Before, `re._constants.MAXREPEAT` was the plain int 4294967294, a silent wrong answer.
- **`re.Pattern`, `re.Match` and `\N{NAME}` in the dynamic build**: `re.Pattern` and `re.Match` are real classes, so `isinstance(p, re.Pattern)` and `type(m) is re.Match` hold, and calling them raises CPython's TypeError. `\N{NAME}` escapes in patterns work, with CPython's error messages and positions, including computed CJK and Hangul syllable names. Only programs whose source has a `\N{` carry the name table.
- **`re` in the dynamic build issues CPython's warnings**: a DeprecationWarning for a positional `count` or `maxsplit`, and a FutureWarning for possible set operations (`[a--b]`, `[[`, `&&`, `||`, `~~`). Each names the caller's line. With the `warnings` module they go through CPython's filters (`"error"` raises them). Without it they are printed to stderr as the default filters do. `count`/`maxsplit` are now read as an index (`re.I` is 2), with CPython's TypeErrors and OverflowError, and a positional `count` given again as a keyword raises CPython's TypeError (#418).
- **Bridged libraries:** a context manager's `__enter__` value stays live rather than being copied, and Python's builtin exception classes come back as the program's own, so `is`, `issubclass` and `except` agree with CPython (#438).
- **Fixed a silent wrong answer in the typed build's regex engine:** a repeated group whose last iteration matched empty (`^((x|y)*)*` on `"xyyzy"`) lost that iteration's captures, so `m.group(1)` printed `None` where CPython prints `""`. Written by DeepSeek, reviewed by Claude.
- **Releases are held to the conformance gates.** The release suite now runs `tools/conformance.py --release-gate` on both slices: nothing is released or published unless core matches CPython on at least 90% of its scored tests, stdlib on at least 80%, and neither has a known-wrong result.
- **Releases carry an SBOM and signed provenance, and are checked for reproducibility.** Each release attaches `brevi-sbom.cdx.json` (CycloneDX: Brevi's crates, the Rust standard library, and every archive with its SHA-256) and GitHub build-provenance attestations for every archive, the SBOM and SHA256SUMS; check one with `gh attestation verify FILE -R ets-public/brevi`. The release workflow also builds the Linux x86-64 binary again from a fresh checkout in another folder and refuses to release unless the two are byte-identical.
- Dynamic builds: `itertools.repeat` objects repr as CPython's (`repeat('a', 2)`, the count going down as items are taken, negative counts shown as 0), and `repeat(x, n)` no longer builds a list of n items up front. Each namedtuple field (`Point.x`) has its own `__doc__`, which can be set and deleted as in CPython.
- **Sandboxed CPython gets no network without a grant (Linux).** Under `--sandbox`, a program that loads CPython (`--allow-py`, `--allow native`) and has no network grant now holds itself, before CPython starts, to no network with a seccomp filter: `socket()` fails with `PermissionError` for CPython, its C extensions and every process they start, Unix sockets by path included (`socketpair()` still works). A sandboxed program that starts CPython with hosts named by `--allow-net` is refused on every OS, since CPython cannot be held to those hosts: grant `--allow net` or leave CPython out. See docs/THREAT_MODEL.md.
- **Security and support docs.** SECURITY.md now says which releases get fixes, how fast we reply to reports, how a compromise is handled and how to check a download; new pages cover [support](docs/SUPPORT.md), [upgrading](docs/UPGRADING.md) and [known issues](docs/KNOWN_ISSUES.md).
- Sets no longer crash with "RefCell already borrowed" when a key's `__eq__` changes the set during a lookup, update or in-place operator: the lookup lets go of the set while user code runs and starts over if the set changed, as CPython's does.
- An exception raised by a key's `__eq__` inside a set operation is now passed on instead of being read as "not equal" (which gave wrong results such as a KeyError from `remove`).
- `x in s`, `s.remove(x)` and `s.discard(x)` accept a set `x` and look it up as a frozenset; `set()`/`frozenset()` refuse keywords and a second argument; `set.__init__` works.
- `id()` gives every heap value (sets, functions, classes, iterators, ...) its own identity instead of 1.
- frozenset subclasses work; int, float, str, bytes, tuple and frozenset subclasses with their own `__init__` keep the value they were built from; set and frozenset subclasses repr with their class name, and a set inside its own repr prints `set(...)`.
- frozenset hashes are cached (nested frozensets no longer rehash exponentially); `set(dict)`, `set.update(dict)` and `dict.fromkeys(set)` reuse stored hashes; a class attribute holding `dict.fromkeys` no longer binds; set iterators have `__length_hint__`; `copy.deepcopy` of a frozenset copies its items.
- CPython's test_set joins the scored core conformance slice (0 wrong; 94.2% of its scored tests match). Its 126 `*Mutating*` tests print output that depends on unseeded random numbers, so the conformance tool always classes them unstable (they flipped between matched and unstable from run to run); both builds still run them, so a crash, wrong exit code or refusal still fails.
- Dynamic builds support `sys.settrace` and `sys.gettrace` with CPython 3.13's call, line, return and exception events, for programs whose traced code uses a checked set of statements (assignments, expressions, pass, return, raise, assert, if/elif/else, match/case, try/finally, defs and classes, each on one line). Anything else in a program that uses `sys.settrace` is refused when it is built, never traced differently. `tools/trace_fuzz.py` compares random programs from that set against CPython 3.13.
- `import dis` is refused in dynamic builds: a compiled function has no CPython bytecode to disassemble (it used to raise `TypeError` when run). `brevi run` runs such a program with python3.
- The typed build's shared-list check now follows functions imported from a sibling file (`from util import f`) instead of taking every such call as one that may change its arguments, so multi-file programs that pass a list or dict to a helper in another file take the typed path again (the latency benchmark had fallen to the dynamic build in v0.27.0: `check --fast` after an edit 160 ms instead of 16 ms). It follows only what it can follow exactly (no `as`, and no module-level name of one file that the other reads or binds); anything else still refuses. `[0] * n` no longer refuses when `n` is a parameter the check knows nothing about. What the check requires of a module's code (the typed build sharing a list in place) is now checked at that module's own lines in the joined program, where it could before land on the main file's line of the same number.
- `brevi check --fast` on a multi-file program is faster again (the latency fixture about 21 to 18 ms here): each file is parsed once for the guard and the sharing proof, and a module whose every statement was already proved inside the file that imports it is not proved a second time.
- Dynamic build: `inspect.Signature.from_callable` works for the program's own defs and lambdas, for functions that wrap them (`functools.wraps`, `lru_cache`, `singledispatch`, `singledispatchmethod`) and for bound methods, and its `str()` and `repr()` match CPython 3.13 exactly (positional-only `/`, `*`, defaults, annotations, return annotation). Any other callable (a builtin, a class, a `functools.partial`), a typing annotation, and the rest of the Signature API (`.parameters`, `==`, `hash`, `Signature(...)`) are refused instead of guessed. Three more CPython stdlib conformance tests match.
- Dynamic build: `lru_cache` wrappers' `cache_info` and `cache_clear` report CPython's `__qualname__` (`_lru_cache_wrapper.cache_info`).
- Dynamic build: `Signature.from_callable(functools.cmp_to_key)` and of the key function it returns match CPython (`(mycmp)`, `(obj)`). `copy.deepcopy` in a program that uses `itertools.tee` is now refused when the program is built, not when it runs.
- Dynamic build: `functools.singledispatch`'s `register` reads a string annotation (`def _(arg: "collections.abc.Sequence")`) as `typing.get_type_hints` does, from the module's globals. It used to stop at run time with a refusal (#452).
- Dynamic build: `functools.singledispatch` and `functools.singledispatchmethod` follow CPython 3.13's algorithm: MRO dispatch, cache invalidation on `register`, a `registry` view, `dispatch()`, annotation-based `@f.register` including `int | str`, and classmethod/staticmethod. Dispatch is cached per concrete class in a hash map. With 1,000 registered classes it takes 0.08 s against CPython's 0.39 s. Forms Brevi cannot do faithfully are refused: ABC registration, string annotations, and `singledispatchmethod` with `abstractmethod`.
- `--strict` (or `BREVI_STRICT=1`) fails closed on any fallback, for CI and production jobs: only the typed build may answer (pybridge included), a file only the dynamic build takes is refused with one `{"e":"strict","path":"dynamic","line":...,"fn":...}` error, nothing runs with `python3` (`brevi-python` refuses every CPython handoff too), a typed run past 64-bit ints stops instead of re-running on the dynamic build, and every build prints its path line, typed too. It works with `run`, `build`, `check` and `verify`; `run --verify --strict` keeps its meaning (a checked run, so its dynamic build is allowed), while `BREVI_STRICT=1` holds that too. The release notes now give the share of conformance programs on each path, and `docs/SUPPORT_MATRIX.md` lists which constructs take which path on which platform.
- Dynamic build: `string.Template` and `string.Formatter` are now available, compiled from CPython 3.13's `string` module. Programs that use only the `string` constants keep the native module and build as fast as before. The stdlib conformance slice rises from 45.1% to 50.4%.
- Dynamic builds: `import pkg.sub` now loads the program's `pkg/sub.py` and sets it on `pkg` as CPython does (it raised AttributeError on `pkg.sub`). `from pkg import sub` sets it on `pkg` too, and `import a.b.c` loads every level.
- Dynamic builds: `sys.modules['__main__']` is the script's module (it raised KeyError). Its names read and assign through it.
- Dynamic builds: a missing attribute of a program file raises CPython 3.13's AttributeError wording, including a circular import ("partially initialized module", "cannot access submodule") and a file that shadows a library or a standard library module. It raised NameError for a name the file had not bound yet.
- Dynamic builds: `from FILE import *` of a program file, and a new attribute set through a program module object, are refused (the names read as unbound before). The program runs in CPython instead.
- Dynamic build: `sys.flags` gives the options of a plain `python3 prog.py` run (`optimize`, `debug`, `dev_mode`, ... read 0 or False, since a built program takes no interpreter options). The fields that depend on the environment or on CPython's startup (`hash_randomization`, `utf8_mode`, `int_max_str_digits`, `gil`) and the tuple forms are refused rather than guessed.
- Dynamic builds: `k in sys.modules`, `sys.modules[k]` and `sys.modules.get(k)` answer as CPython 3.13 does, counting the modules it loads before a script runs (`"os" in sys.modules` is True). A read whose answer CPython does not fix is refused when the program builds: a module only some environments load at startup (encodings, sitecustomize, underscore modules), any module the program does not import before its other statements when it imports a library whose import loads others (json loads re), or after a codec lookup by name that may import more. A key that is not a string literal is refused when the program builds too, except a parameter of a def that the program only calls by name, with a string literal for it every time (each read is then decided as that literal is). `len(sys.modules)`, its keys, iteration and other methods, `from sys import modules`, and sys.modules reached through getattr or an alias are refused.
- Conformance: CPython's `test_dataclasses` joins the stdlib slice (212 of 246 tests match in both builds, no wrong result; the rest are refused with a clear message).
- Conformance: CPython's `test_enum` joins the stdlib slice (no wrong result in either build; the rest are refused with a clear message). `datetime` joins the imports a scored test may use, and `test_enum.TestSpecial.test_empty_globals` (it runs a string through `compile()`) is refused by design.
- Conformance: CPython's `test_patma` (match statements) joins the core slice (266 tests match in both builds, no wrong result). Its 44 tests that run a string through `compile()` are refused by design and listed in `compat/conformance_by_design.txt`; the rest are refused with a clear message.
- Fixed: `brevi run` could fail with "Text file busy (os error 26)" when the build folder and the cache were on different file systems or mounts (a /tmp mount, for example), about one run in sixty with several at once. A cached program is then copied rather than linked, and the copy was written by a brevi process whose start-up thread could be starting a child that briefly held it open. Programs are now copied by a separate single-threaded helper process.
- Fixed random "Text file busy (os error 26)" failures when many builds of the same program ran at once (32 parallel test jobs hit it). A build that took a program from the binary cache marked it as recently used by opening it for writing; cached programs are hard linked into place, so for that moment every other process starting a copy of it was refused by the kernel. The mark is now set through a read-only handle (Windows: attributes only), and it now also lands while the program is running, which the old write handle could not do. A build through cargo also writes a fresh output file instead of writing into one that may be linked to the cache.
- `textwrap` with `fix_sentence_endings=True` now works in native builds, exactly like CPython (it used to stop with NotImplementedError at run time). Fixes two known-wrong conformance results (stdlib slice). Written by DeepSeek, reviewed by Claude.
- Dynamic builds run each `threading.Thread` on a stack of its own, and threads take turns where one waits: `threading.Event`, a `Lock` or `RLock` another thread holds, `join()` and `time.sleep()` now wait as in CPython (before, `start()` ran the whole thread at once and locks were never held). A thread that never waits prints what it printed before. CPython's `test_tee_concurrent` now matches.
- `bytes()` of an `array.array` (or another library object with a buffer) in a dynamic build gives its buffer, as CPython does: `bytes(array('h', [1, 2]))` is `b'\x01\x00\x02\x00'`, not `b'\x01\x02'`.
- `threading.Condition`, `Semaphore`, `BoundedSemaphore`, `Barrier` and `BrokenBarrierError` in dynamic builds, with CPython's wake order, messages and Barrier states (reset and abort while threads wait).
- `re` matches an `array.array` as the bytes of its buffer, and `bytearray`, `memoryview`, `int()`, `float()` and `collections.abc` take an `array.array` as CPython does: `bytearray(b'ab') == array('b', b'ab')` is True, `int(array('B', b'42'))` is 42 and `issubclass(array.array, MutableSequence)` is True. A `memoryview` of a library object is refused at run time (a copy could not write through). `deque` is a `MutableSequence`, as in CPython.
- Dynamic build: a warning names the program's line, file and module as CPython's does. That covers `warnings.warn` in the program, a library's warning whose stacklevel points back into the program, and the warnings Brevi's runtime issues (re). It holds in functions, generators and coroutines across yields, lambdas, class bodies, functions a library calls back, and loop headers, so filters, the once-per-line `default` action and `showwarning` see what CPython sees (they saw `<sys>:0` before). A line the dynamic build cannot know is refused, never guessed: `warnings.warn(..., stacklevel=2)` in the program does not build, and a warning from a call that opens on a later line of its statement raises the dynamic build's "not supported" error. Only programs that import `warnings` keep lines.
A warning from a call that opens on a later line of a multi-line statement, whose line the dynamic build does not know, is raised when an "error" filter that names no line catches it (the line cannot change that answer) and refused otherwise.
`functools.partial` read from an instance (`class A: m = partial(f)`, then `A().m`) issues CPython 3.13's FutureWarning at that line. A program that uses `functools.partial` keeps line tracking on for this, the same cost as importing `warnings`.
- Dynamic build: `weakref.proxy` works. Every operation goes to the referent while it lives (attributes, calls, `str`, truth, `len`, iteration, items, arithmetic, comparisons) and raises `ReferenceError` once it is gone; the proxy keeps its own type, repr and identity and is unhashable, as in CPython. A proxy with a callback, or of an object with `__index__`, `__int__`, `__float__` or `__complex__`, is refused. `re.Pattern` objects now take weak references. Four more CPython stdlib conformance tests match.
- Dynamic build: `iter(x)` of an object whose `__iter__` returns an iterator object now returns that object itself (`iter(it) is it`), as CPython does; before, it was wrapped, so its attributes were unreachable.
- **CPython conformance on Windows.** `tools/conformance.py` now runs on Windows (wall-clock timeout and memory watch per program, the process tree killed on either, crashes reported by their NTSTATUS code), and a weekly `conformance` job in `.github/workflows/windows.yml` measures the core slice there and keeps the report. It reports and does not gate yet: a Windows known-wrong list and floor will come from its first results.
- Windows: `os.path` is now `ntpath`, as in CPython. It handles drive letters, both slashes and UNC shares. `os.name`, `os.sep`, `os.altsep` and `os.linesep` are now the Windows values. Before this change, a program on Windows used POSIX path rules and gave silently wrong answers: `os.path.basename("C:\\a\\b.txt")` returned the whole path. `import ntpath` works natively on every platform, and posixpath gains `splitroot`/`splitdrive`.
- `sys.platform` is now `win32` on Windows and `darwin` on macOS in dynamic builds. It used to say `linux` everywhere.
- Windows: `pathlib` is refused in dynamic builds. Brevi's copy is POSIX-shaped and CPython's `Path` on Windows is a `WindowsPath`, so such a program now runs in CPython instead (or, with `--strict`, does not build).
- Windows: a generator's fiber now reserves its 16 MB stack and commits only 64 KB up front, as on Linux and macOS. Before, each live generator committed its whole stack.
- New Windows parity checks (`tests/windows/parity.ps1`, part of the Windows workflow) cover command-line arguments, CRLF text mode, 5,000 live generators, paths, DLLs planted beside a program, and TLS against public roots.

## v0.27.1 (11 Nov 2026)

- `brevi-python` works on macOS: it ran the program's `run FILE` arguments through CPython there, because macOS reports the link's path as the running binary. A copy of brevi named `python3` (not a link) no longer loops either. The new release archive check found it.
- Releases test what they ship: the release runs the whole Linux suite (both CPython conformance slices included) on the commit it releases, runs the Windows platform checks on the brevi.exe unpacked from the zip it ships, unpacks and runs every archive, checks SHA256SUMS against the files as published, links the run from the release notes, and releases nothing unless every platform passed. A `dry_run` input runs all of it without releasing.
- The duckdb, onnxruntime and MCP-client checks no longer skip silently: releases, the daily run and Windows CI install the pinned packages (tests/requirements-native.txt) and fail without them.
- Windows CI also runs nightly on main (skipped when nothing it depends on changed) with a canary that runs the last release's Windows zip; a nightly failure opens a `windows` issue.

## v0.27.0 (11 Nov 2026)

- **Windows is beta.** The Windows build is x86-64 only (`x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`). It builds, runs and verifies programs natively, including library calls through CPython, but `--standalone` builds of programs that use Python libraries need Linux for now. Of the sandbox limits, `--limit mem` is held by the OS (a Job Object, from the program's first instruction), and `time` and `output` are held as on Linux; `cpu`, `file`, `fds` and `threads` are refused on Windows rather than half enforced.
- **`del f.attr` on a function works in dynamic builds.** Deleting an attribute set on a def (`f.x = 1; del f.x`) raised AttributeError, so the native build of `functools.update_wrapper`'s own CPython test gave a different answer; it now removes the attribute, before and after `f.__dict__` is read, and deleting a missing one raises AttributeError as in CPython.
- **`sys.stdin.buffer` and `sys.stdout.buffer` in dynamic builds.** The fast-I/O idiom `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()`, `sys.stdin.buffer.readline()`, `for line in sys.stdin.buffer` and `sys.stdout.buffer.write(b"...")` now run natively, reading and writing bytes on the same streams as the text calls, in order. Before, a program using them built and then stopped with `NotImplementedError: binary streams` (found by the DeepSeek release test).
- **`int()` of a text local is a big integer when it needs to be.** In the typed build, `a, b = parts[0], parts[1]` then `str(int(a) + int(b))`, or `s = input()` then `n = int(s)`, now handle numbers past 64 bits as Python does, instead of failing with "invalid literal for int()". `int(x)` of a float next to big integers truncates as Python's does.
- **Running the same file twice at once no longer clashes.** Every build of a file used one shared folder (`$TMPDIR/brevi-HASH`), so two `brevi run`, `build` or `verify` processes on the same file at the same time (parallel MCP sessions, parallel verify runs) could overwrite each other's generated code or binary, and one then failed its build and quietly ran with CPython (or, with `--native`, failed). Each process now builds in its own folder inside that one, removed when it exits; built binaries are still shared through the build cache, so the second process reuses the first one's build.
- **Faster runs after an edit.** `brevi run` and `brevi test` (and the MCP `run` and `test` tools) no longer wait for the optimised build. After an edit they build the program unoptimised, run it at once, and build the optimised binary in the background, so the next run of the unchanged program gets it. On bench/latency (a 200-line, 3-file program, 4 cores), a run after an edit takes 0.32 s instead of 0.82 s on the typed path and 0.50 s instead of 1.50 s on the dynamic path. The unoptimised binary runs slower (often 2x to 5x on loops), so use `--release` for a long computation, or set `BREVI_TIERED=0` to always wait for the optimised build. `brevi build` is unchanged, and `bench/latency.py` gains a `run_edit` mode. Only one background build runs at a time on the machine (two with 8 or more cores). None starts when less than 2 GB of memory is free, and one still running after five minutes is stopped, so many runs at once (a test suite, parallel agents) fall back to the quick binary instead of piling up rustc processes.
- **DeepSeek in the release test.** `bench/e2e.py --model deepseek` (DeepSeek V4.1 Flash, `deepseek-flash`) and `--model deepseek-pro` (`deepseek-v4-pro`) run the same tasks, tools and checks as Claude through DeepSeek's OpenAI-compatible API (`bench/deepseek.py`, standard library only, key in `DEEPSEEK_API_KEY`); `replay.py` and the weekly table take them too. DeepSeek is reported by `--verdict` but does not count toward the release bar until it has two weekly runs.
- **Programs that make many objects run faster, and Brevi's dynamic build now beats CPython on a scalar-autograd GPT.** Three changes to how a built program keeps its objects in memory. Small blocks now come from 16 KB pages of one size each, and a freed block goes back to its own page, as in CPython's pymalloc. Before, one list per size handed blocks back in the order they were freed, so a program that makes and drops objects in rounds (a training loop that builds a new graph each step) ended up with its objects scattered over the whole heap. Instances whose attributes were set in the same order share one record of the names, so each instance keeps only the values (an autograd node with four attributes went from 256 to 128 bytes). And every value is now two words instead of three: tuples, module names and library values are thin pointers, so lists, tuples, sets and dicts take a third less memory. The emitter also calls nested functions directly (a recursive `build_topo` inside `backward()`, or a helper defined in a function and called there) instead of through the function value and its argument list, reads a nested function's captured variables in place, and calls one-argument methods such as `list.append` and `set.add` without building an argument list. On [bench/speed/gpt_autograd.py](bench/speed/gpt_autograd.py), a character-level GPT trained with one Python object per number as Karpathy's microgpt is, 100 training steps took 13.1 s with Brevi against 8.1 s for CPython 3.13 (1.6x slower) and are now 1.4x faster than CPython, with the same output. Karpathy's own [microgpt](https://gist.github.com/karpathy/8627fe009c40f57531cb18360106ce95), unchanged, trains and samples in 93 s against CPython's 150 s, and is now on the [Showcases page](site/docs/showcases.md). Typed builds use the new allocator too.
- **Python SDK: `import brevi`.** The `brevi` wheel now holds a small Python library as well as the binary. `brevi.run(code, stdin=..., args=..., cases=..., limits=..., allow=..., allow_read=...)` builds and runs a program sandboxed, with only what the calling code grants, and returns a `Result` with the program's `stdout`, `stderr` and `exit_code` apart, the `path` it ran on, the `limit` that stopped it and `for_model()`; `brevi.verify` returns the certificate, `brevi.check` the errors, and `brevi.TOOL` / `brevi.handle` are the `run_python` tool for a model and its reply. `brevi.Session` and `brevi.AsyncSession` keep one `brevi mcp` process each, so the build cache stays warm. To support it, the MCP `run` tool's structured result now also carries `stdout`, `stderr`, `exit_code`, `timed_out` and `ms` (the text reply is unchanged). tests/sdk_test.py runs every call against the built binary.
- **Node SDK: `import { run } from "brevi"`.** The npm package now has the same calls as the Python SDK: `run`, `check`, `verify`, `handle`, `TOOL` and `Session`, with TypeScript types, over one `brevi mcp` process per session (ESM and `require`). `Result` gives `stdout`, `stderr`, `exitCode`, `path`, `limit`, `certificate` and `forModel()`. tests/sdk_node_test.mjs runs every call against the built binary.
- **GitHub Action: `ets-public/brevi/setup`.** `uses: ets-public/brevi/setup@v0.27.0` installs the release binary for the runner (checked against SHA256SUMS), `brevi-python` beside it and the Rust toolchain if missing, and keeps Brevi's build cache between runs, keyed on the version. Inputs `version` and `cache`; outputs `version` and `path`. [setup/README.md](setup/README.md)
- **Dev container feature: `ghcr.io/ets-public/features/brevi`.** Adds Brevi, `brevi-python` and the Rust toolchain (if missing) to any Debian, Ubuntu or Fedora based dev container, through the same checked `install.sh`; option `version`. release.yml publishes it with the image. [devcontainer/README.md](devcontainer/README.md)
- **`brevi-python`, a drop-in for `python3`.** The brevi binary acts as `python3` when started as `brevi-python` (installed by the wheel, `install.sh` and the release archives), `python3` or `python`, or as `brevi python ARGS`. A script, `-c CODE` or a script on stdin builds natively when Brevi can and runs with CPython otherwise, with CPython's stdout, stderr and exit status and nothing printed by Brevi (`BREVI_SHIM_VERBOSE=1` shows the path line). Other flags, `-m`, the REPL, and programs that read what differs between a native run and CPython (`sys.executable`, `sys.argv[0]`, argparse) go straight to CPython; `BREVI_SHIM=0` turns it off. It is never installed as `python3` on your machine; only the `:python3` image does that.
- **Published sandbox image.** Each release now pushes `ghcr.io/ets-public/brevi-sandbox:VERSION` and `:latest` (Brevi, the Rust toolchain, python3, the SDK), and `:VERSION-python3` / `:python3` with `brevi-python` as `python3`, from templates/image/Dockerfile (one Linux job in release.yml). The image now also warms Brevi's dynamic runtime library, so the first SDK or MCP call in a fresh sandbox takes about 0.6 s instead of about 54 s. The E2B, Daytona, Modal and Docker templates start from a published tag when `BREVI_IMAGE` names one. `install.sh` takes `BREVI_VERSION` to install a given release.
- **Verify by default for agents.** `brevi run --verify x.py` runs the program natively and checks the run against CPython on the same stdin and arguments, with CPython running alongside. When they match you get the native output and a certificate line on stderr; when they differ you get CPython's output and exit code, the first differing line (`{"verify":"differs",...}`), and a local log of the case. `--strict` prints neither run's output instead. A program a second run would change (writes files, uses the network or native code, reads the clock, unseeded random numbers or the environment) runs once, unverified, and the line says why. If CPython takes more than 10x as long (at least 2 s), the native answer comes back marked unverified. The MCP server's `run` now does this by default with `sandbox: false` (`verify: false` turns it off), and once Brevi gets a program wrong, that program runs with CPython for the rest of the session. A `run` case that fails natively but passes under CPython is reported as a Brevi bug, not the model's. New `brevi report` prints every logged wrong answer as a GitHub issue body ready to paste. The release test (`bench/e2e.py`) now runs every native test under CPython too and logs how many outputs differed (`verify_differs`), and `--verdict` prints the count: any number above zero is a P0 bug. Nothing is sent anywhere. The skill, editor rules, `llms.txt` and agent docs now recommend `brevi run --verify` ([design](docs/VERIFY_BY_DEFAULT.md)).
- **Local CI in a quarter of the time.** `tools/ci_local.sh` took about 1.5 hours: `tests/check.sh` ran some 1,000 native builds one at a time on one core, the build cache (1 GB) was smaller than one pass and evicted itself, and the script ran the suite twice. `tests/check_parallel.py` now runs check.sh's sections side by side, one job per CPU, with the same checks and the same report (a cold run on 4 cores: 25 minutes, a warm rerun under 2). ci_local.sh keeps a 6 GB cache, runs the suite once, and like CI skips the old-solutions rebuild, the grammar check and the tests of Brevi's library copies (mcp, openai, polars, ...) that nothing on the branch can affect; `--full` runs everything, and GitHub CI and releases still do. CI and the release build use the parallel runner too, which saves Actions minutes.
- **Silent wrong outputs fixed: mixed tuple scores and lists taken out of a dict.** Both found by the DeepSeek release test (poker and warehouse, 7 outputs that differed from CPython). A function that returned a tuple on one path and `(5,) + tuple(ranks)` on another kept the second as a list in the typed build, so it printed as `[5, ...]` and sorted below every tuple; such a file now takes the dynamic build. And `lst = waiting.get(sku)` (or a method returning `self.waiting.get(sku, [])`) followed by `lst.append(x)` or `lst.pop(i)` changed a copy, because a list of shared rows was treated as shared itself; the dict now changes as in CPython (dynamic build). tests/fallback covers both.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: `x or y` ran `x` twice.** In the typed build, `g() or 5` and `f() and y` evaluated the first operand once to test it and again to return it, so `input() or "default"` read two lines and a counter went up twice. The first operand now runs once.
- **Claims now match the measurements.** After an outside review, the README, the site, llms.txt, the agent skills and editor rules, and the MCP server's instructions say what the data shows. CPython's own test suite (58.2% match, 44 built programs that differ, 5 crashes) comes first, and the 591 saved programs are described as a regression set. Speed is given as 1.7x to 39.5x, median 8.5x, on 12 compute benchmarks, replacing "2 to 40x" and the agent-facing "10 to 50 times". Cost is described as about the same as CPython, and the 0.58x heavy-task figure is marked as having no CPython control yet. The sandbox is described as a limit layer, not an isolation boundary. Codon's 36 wrong programs are given as 36 of the 206 it built, and the stale FAQ answer about pip and npm is fixed. The benchmark's native-code prompt line uses the measured range.
- **GUI windows on eframe 0.37.** `brevi build --window` now builds tkinter programs on eframe/egui 0.37 (was 0.31), with the same widgets, canvas drawing, dialogs and key bindings. Brevi keeps eframe's OpenGL renderer rather than 0.37's new default (wgpu), so window builds stay as quick and need no GPU driver beyond OpenGL. `after()` timers now keep running while the window is minimized. Dialog and toplevel windows are drawn a little wider, as egui 0.37 draws them. `tools/ci_local.sh` now compiles the window front end, so an eframe update can't slip in unbuilt.
- **A roadmap to the default runtime for AI-written Python.** [docs/ROADMAP.md](docs/ROADMAP.md) sets the goal, the decisions (SDK and base images before a hosted service; `.bv` and new library ports paused until 1.0; Brevi stays 0.x until the 1.0 gates are met) and the 30-day plan, and [docs/METRICS.md](docs/METRICS.md) defines every number it steers by, with where each current value comes from. Designs for the next steps: [verify by default](docs/VERIFY_BY_DEFAULT.md) and [the SDK, a `python3` shim and published base images](docs/SDK_AND_IMAGES.md). The [roadmap page](https://brevi-lang.org/docs/roadmap) follows it.
- **Loop latency is measured.** `bench/latency.py` times what an agent waits for after each edit on a fixed 3-file program, p50 and p95 over 20 runs: on a 4-core Xeon, `brevi check --fast` 16 ms, `brevi check` 94 ms, a build after an edit 0.87 s (typed) and 1.5 s (dynamic), an unchanged `brevi run` 4.5 ms. Results are kept in `bench/latency-results/`. [docs/LATENCY.md](docs/LATENCY.md) shows where the time goes (about 90% of a build is LLVM optimising at `opt-level=1`) and proposes tiered builds for the edit loop.
- **The release test records first-attempt native builds and the cost to a passing answer.** `bench/e2e.py` now logs, for each task, whether each check built natively (`natives`, `first_native`) and the tokens and dollars up to the first passing check (`tokens_to_green`, `usd_to_green`); `--summary` gains the columns `n1` and `usd2grn`, and `--verdict` prints first-attempt native, retries per task and the tokens-to-green ratio against CPython. A new arm, `--show-errors`, runs a program Brevi refuses with CPython as `brevi run` does but shows the model why, so the cost of using Brevi with its errors in view can be measured.
- **CPython conformance is in CI, as a ratchet.** `tools/conformance.py --check` now also fails when a known-wrong line is no longer wrong (the list in `compat/conformance_known_wrong.txt` only shrinks), when a built program crashes where it did not before (crashes are now in that list, next to wrong output and wrong exit codes), and when fewer tests match than `compat/conformance_floor.json` allows (the score only rises). It runs on every pull request that touches the compiler, on one toolchain, and in `tools/ci_local.sh` when `uv` is installed.
- **Architecture doc at v0.26.0** (`docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` now lists every file in `src/` and `src/dynpy`), and `bench/showcase/run.py` records the commit and the CPU in `results.json`.
- **The cost benchmark has a CPython-plus-hint control.** `bench/e2e.py --lang pyh` runs the Python prompt with the same native-code line the Brevi side gets, checked with CPython, so a heavy-task saving that comes from the line alone shows. Run twice per model on 9 November: with the same line on both sides, Brevi's side cost 0.46x of CPython's on Sonnet and 0.34x on Haiku, because the plain solution runs over the time limit on CPython and the model has to make it fast. The site's benchmarks page has the table next to the old uncontrolled 0.58x.
- **CPython conformance 58.2% -> 67.2%: 25 fewer wrong builds, 3 fewer crashes.** On CPython's own test_grammar, test_scope, test_int, test_list and test_dict, 209 of 311 tests now print exactly what CPython prints (was 181); 19 built programs still differ (was 44) and 2 still crash (was 5), all listed in `compat/conformance_known_wrong.txt`. Fixed in the dynamic build: an exhausted list iterator stays exhausted; `lst * huge` raises MemoryError instead of being killed; slice steps near `sys.maxsize` no longer overflow; `@=`, `/=`, `//=`, `%=`, `**=`, `^=`, `<<=`, `>>=` on objects call their in-place methods; `list.pop` rejects two arguments, `list.__init__` replaces the items, `list == list` checks the lengths again when an item's `__eq__` changes a list; `dict | dict`, `dict |= other`, `dict.update` raising when the other dict changes under it, `hasattr(dict, "__missing__")` is False; subclasses of `str`, `int` and `tuple` hash and compare by value, `super().__eq__`, `super().__hash__` and `super().__new__` work on them, `int(obj)` calls `__int__` and checks the result; a `__repr__` that is a staticmethod is called without self and `repr(lst)` reads the list as it changes; `int("10", 2, 1)` is a TypeError, `int("१२३")` reads Unicode digits in both builds; reading a local before its assignment in a function whose inner function captures it is an UnboundLocalError, not a NameError; functions have `__call__`; `sys.path` has the program's directory.
- **A timing-dependent asyncio test is now deterministic.** `tests/lib/asyncio_agen_errors.py` used a 3 ms timeout against a generator that slept 2 ms per item, so a slow CI machine could print a different line. Its timeout checks now run against a generator that stalls after its first item.
- **A build killed halfway no longer holds up later builds for 20 minutes.** The lock that makes concurrent builds of the same runtime or library copy wait for one build now names its owner, so a lock left by a process that was killed, or by a machine that restarted, is taken over at once instead of after 20 minutes.
- **Changelog entries go in `changelog.d/`.** Each PR now adds its entry as its own file in `changelog.d/` instead of editing CHANGELOG.md, so PRs no longer conflict on the changelog and rerun CI for it. `tools/changelog.py` prints the Unreleased entries, checks the files (in `tools/ci_local.sh` and CI) and, for the release PR, folds them into CHANGELOG.md under the new version (`--release vX.Y.Z`). The release notes, `tools/credits.py` and the site's changelog page read the fragments too.
- `chr()` of an int outside 0..0x10FFFF now raises CPython's `ValueError("chr() arg not in range(0x110000)")` on
  every build, including ints past 64 bits (it was IndexError or OverflowError) and negative ints on the typed
  build (which returned ""); the typed build can catch it. A lone surrogate (U+D800..U+DFFF) stops as unsupported
  instead of giving a different string. Found by the fuzz harness. (DeepSeek-authored, Claude-reviewed.)
- **CI guards against leaked credentials and workflow takeovers.** `tools/secret_scan.py` fails CI when a tracked file holds a credential (Anthropic, OpenAI-style and DeepSeek, GitHub, AWS, Google, Slack and Replicate shapes, private keys) and, run locally, also when one holds the exact value of any of the machine's secret variables, so session logs and transcripts can be checked before they are committed or shared. `tools/workflow_guard.py` fails CI when a workflow uses an action that is not pinned to a commit SHA, gives secrets to a `pull_request` run, uses `pull_request_target`, `workflow_run` or `issue_comment`, runs on `issues` without an author check, or expands outside text into a shell script. Every action is now pinned to a SHA (Dependabot keeps them current), the release workflow passes its tag through `env:`. The auto-fix workflow is removed: it handed issue text built from upstream release notes to a Claude agent holding the Anthropic key and a write token, and printed its transcript into the public job log. Toolchain and upstream issues are still opened, for a maintainer to pick up; auto-fix can come back with a sandboxed design.
- `brevi run file.py a b` passes `a b` to the program, as `python3 file.py a b` does; before, arguments were dropped unless they followed `--`. Brevi's own options may still follow the file, and a dashed word after the file that is no Brevi option (a misspelt `--sandbox`, say) is refused with exit 2 instead of being ignored; put it after `--` to pass it to the program.
- A `#` in an f-string's format spec (`f"{255:#x}"`, `f"{n:#010b}"`) is no longer read as a comment, so these files build instead of being refused with "unterminated string literal".
- An f-string value the typed build cannot read (`f"{ {1: 2}[1] }"`) is refused there and built over dynamic values, instead of being printed as literal text.
- A file that starts with a UTF-8 byte order mark builds, as CPython reads it.
- An uncaught exception on the typed build names its class: `{"e":"runtime","line":3,"type":"MyErr","msg":"custom failure"}`.
- `brevi report`, `brevi guide`, `--help` and `--version` end quietly when the reader closes the pipe (`brevi report | head -1`) instead of panicking with exit 101.
- **A second conformance number: the stdlib slice.** `tools/conformance.py --slice stdlib` runs CPython 3.13's `test_itertools`, `test_collections`, `test_re`, `test_textwrap`, `test_functools` and `test_string` the same way as the core slice. It has its own floor and known-wrong list, and the daily CI run ratchets it. Now: 34.8% (202 of 554 scored tests match; 36.8% on the forced dynamic build) and 84 wrong. It was 22.2% before the extractor followed `from itertools import *` and the itertools fixes below. Most of the wrong results are in `namedtuple`, `Counter`, `functools.total_ordering` and `re` (pattern repr, bytes patterns, `scanner`, and a stack overflow on `(x)*` over 50,000 characters on the typed build). These are the next fixes, and the number is published as measured ([report](bench/conformance/stdlib/report.md)).
- **DeepSeek as a contributor on CPython conformance.** `tools/ds_contrib.py TEST_ID` gives DeepSeek V4 Pro (reasoning effort max) one failing conformance test and a small set of tools: read, search and edit files in the repository (never `.git`, the harness or the test list), rebuild, rerun that one test, and build a snippet next to CPython. It cannot run other commands, push or reach the network. When it finishes, the harness reruns the test itself and, only if the test now matches on both builds and nothing that matched broke, commits the change on a branch with DeepSeek as the author. Spend is counted from the API's usage and capped per run (`--budget`, default $1.50); every step is logged to `bench/logs/ds_contrib/`. A person reviews each branch before it becomes a pull request, and DeepSeek's pull requests are never merged without review.
- **`d1.items() == d2.items()` compares item by item.** Comparisons between two `dict_items` views and `(k, v) in d.items()` look the key up and compare the stored value, as CPython does, so a value whose `__eq__` raises propagates the error instead of a TypeError about hashing (fixes `test_dict.test_errors_in_view_containment_check`; written by DeepSeek V4 Pro, reviewed by Claude).
- A key's `__eq__` or `__hash__` that changes the dict in the middle of a lookup, insert, `setdefault`, `pop` or `del` no longer crashes the dynamic build; after 200 restarts it raises RuntimeError (written by DeepSeek, #316).
- **`d.keys().mapping` and friends.** Dict views now have `.mapping`, a live read-only `mappingproxy` of their dict, and `Class.__dict__` is a mappingproxy too, as in CPython (fixes CPython conformance test `test_dict.test_views_mapping`; written by DeepSeek V4 Pro through `tools/ds_contrib.py`, reviewed by Claude).
