- **Dict view unions iterate in CPython's order.** `d1.items() | d2.items()` and `d.keys() | other` now build the result the way CPython's `dictviews_or` does, so the resulting set prints in the same order (fixes `test_dict.test_dictview_set_operations_on_items`; written by DeepSeek V4 Pro, reviewed by Claude).
- Functions have `__code__` with `co_name` and `co_flags` (coroutine, generator, async generator, varargs, nested) in dynamic builds, so code that checks a function's kind through its flags works (written by DeepSeek, #317).
- `weakref.ref` (with callbacks) and `gc.collect()` are native in dynamic builds: `gc.collect()` breaks reference cycles that nothing outside them can reach, starting from the program's weak references; the rest of `gc` and `weakref` still runs in CPython where the bridge is allowed (written by DeepSeek, #319).
- Typed builds read `int(text)` and `int(text, base)` of any length into a big integer where the text is visible (a literal, `input()`, `parts[i]`, `s.strip()`), including Unicode digits; `int('010', 0)` is a ValueError, as in CPython (written by DeepSeek, #318).
- Generator expressions are lazy in dynamic builds: only the outermost iterable runs where the expression is written, so `any()`/`all()` stop early and later changes to the names it reads are seen, as in CPython; `sum(x*x for x in ...)` is also about 10% faster (written by DeepSeek, #314).
- Instances have a live `__dict__` in dynamic builds: writes through `o.__dict__` or `vars(o)` change the attributes, `o.__dict__ = {...}` replaces them, and non-string keys are kept, as in CPython (written by DeepSeek, #315).
- `sys.modules` is one live dict for the whole program, and `import` registers each module in it, so `sys.modules['m'] is m` (written by DeepSeek, #313).
- **`__del__` works in dynamic builds.** A class's `__del__` runs when the last reference to an instance goes, as in CPython: on rebinding or `del`, when a list or dict is cleared, when a loop variable moves on, when a function returns, and at exit (globals finalized while every global can still be read). Tuples and `list.clear()` free their items last first, as CPython does. An exception in `__del__` prints "Exception ignored in" on stderr and the program goes on. A `__del__` or `__eq__` that clears a dict during `==` between dicts no longer crashes. Before, the dynamic build refused any class with `__del__`. Known gaps: the order objects are finalized in at exit, and when a list is freed whole, can differ from CPython's (CPython does not promise it either); reference cycles are not collected. 7 more CPython `test_dict` and `test_scope` tests build and match.
- **CPython conformance 74.0% -> 78.5%** (244 of 311 on both builds; 44 refused, 8 wrong, no crashes), with `__del__` above and the DeepSeek fixes merged before this. `compat/conformance_floor.json` is raised to 244 and 244.
- **`tools/ds_contrib.py` runs everything the model can influence in a box.** The compiler it edits, the programs it builds, its `try_python` snippets and the task check run in their own PID, mount and network namespaces as user `nobody`, with an allowlisted environment. There is no network, `/root` shows only the toolchains (read-only), and no other process, token file or session folder is visible. A self-check refuses to start a run if the box leaks. This replaces the name-based filtering of #372.
- `tools/ds_contrib.py`: an empty or malformed reply from the DeepSeek API is retried instead of ending the run with a traceback.
- `tools/ds_contrib.py` no longer passes credentials to anything the model runs: subprocesses get an environment without secret-looking variables, and secret values are masked in tool output before it reaches the model or the session log.
- `tools/ds_contrib.py` has a `typed_rust_errors` tool: it emits the typed build's Rust for a file, compiles it, and shows each rustc error with the Python line it came from.
- `tools/ds_contrib.py --task BRIEF --id NAME --check CMD`: the DeepSeek contributor harness takes any task, not only a failing conformance test; the harness judges it by the exit code of CMD. `tools/ds_typed_check.py` is the check for typed-path tasks: the target programs must build typed, guard programs must stay typed, and every one must print what python3 prints on its task's tests.
- **DuckDB works on Windows.** Brevi's own duckdb copy now loads DuckDB's library with LoadLibraryA/GetProcAddress: libduckdb.dll, or the C API exported by the duckdb Python package (`pip install duckdb`), and on Windows x64 a create/insert/select program runs natively and `brevi verify` says identical. The DuckDB library inside the Python wheel imports CPython's DLL (`python310.dll`); Brevi preloads it, as it preloads CPython's library on Linux and macOS, so no Python runs. A failed load now names what Windows wanted: "The specified module could not be found. (Win32 error 126)".
- **Faster method calls and attribute access in the dynamic build.** Class attribute lookups keep a two-way cache per class that no longer allocates on a miss; `o.x = v` on a class that has properties only takes the slow path for the property names; `type(x)` no longer hashes a class name; a missing instance attribute (as when `o.method()` looks in the instance first) is ruled out with one bit test; short attribute names compare as two machine words; `int * int`, `int % n` and `int // n` skip the general operator code. Rendering a chat template with jinja2 runs about 15% fewer instructions, a method-call loop is 8% faster, and `{{ m.role }}` on a plain dict no longer raises and catches an AttributeError inside Brevi's jinja2. New benchmarks against CPython are in `bench/speed/gaps/` (`sh bench/speed/gaps/run.sh`).
- The dynamic build enforces `__slots__` as CPython does: assigning a name that is not a slot raises `AttributeError` when the class has no `__dict__`, inheritance and `"__dict__"` in slots keep a `__dict__` where CPython has one, `vars()` and the read-only and class-variable conflict errors match, and `@dataclass(slots=True)` is enforced too.
- **More Python builds natively in dynamic builds:**
  - `eval()` of literal text is parsed when the program is built. Invalid number tokens raise SyntaxError by CPython's own rules (`09`, `1__0`, `0x`, `1e`, and `1Else` but not `1else`). The build is refused when a name in the text would resolve differently than in CPython.
  - `locals()` in a function returns a snapshot in CPython's order: parameters and locals first, then free variables.
  - f-string fields can hold strings in the same quotes and can span lines (PEP 701, Python 3.12).
  - `nonlocal x, y` works when the function never uses `y`.
  - `SyntaxError`, `IndentationError`, `SyntaxWarning` and the other warning classes exist as names.
- **CPython conformance 78.5% -> 80.4%** (250 of 311 on the normal build, 251 on the forced dynamic build; 38 refused, 8 wrong, no crashes). `compat/conformance_floor.json` is raised to 250/251.
- **A fuzzer for the builds** (`tools/fuzz.py`). It writes seeded random Python in the style agents write, runs it under CPython, then builds it on the normal and forced dynamic paths. A Rust panic, a signal or a compiler crash fails the run, and a wrong answer is reported. A failing batch is cut down to the one case that fails, and `--reduce` shrinks that case further. CI replays `bench/fuzz/corpus/` on every pull request, and the daily run fuzzes 60 fresh batches seeded by the date. The first 1,500 cases found no panics and three wrong-answer bugs (`0 ** -1`, the print order of set literals, the `chr()` error type), which are being fixed one PR each.
- **Generators run their cleanup as CPython's do.** `gen.close()`, and a generator dropped part way (its last reference gone, or still suspended at exit), raise GeneratorExit at the yield it stopped at, so `finally` blocks and `with` exits around a `yield` run. A generator that yields again is RuntimeError, and `close()` gives the generator's return value (Python 3.13). `gen.throw(exc)` and `gen.throw(type, value)` work on plain generators. `@contextmanager` functions with `try/finally` around their `yield` therefore build natively. Before, the dynamic build refused any `yield` inside `try/finally` or `with`.
- **CPython conformance 67.2% -> 74.0%** (230 of 311 tests on the normal build, 229 on the forced dynamic build), with the int-digit limit and generator cleanup above and the DeepSeek fixes merged before this; 12 wrong results left, no new ones. `compat/conformance_floor.json` is raised to 230 and 229.
- **`sys.set_int_max_str_digits` and `sys.get_int_max_str_digits` work in dynamic builds.** `int(text)` past the limit (CPython counts the digits, not the sign, spaces or underscores, and only for a base that is not a power of two) and `str(n)` past it raise CPython's ValueError, with the limit set by the program (default 4300, 0 for none). Before, the setter did nothing and the getter refused the build. 12 more CPython `test_int` tests build and match.
- **itertools are lazy on the dynamic build.** `chain`, `chain.from_iterable`, `accumulate`, `groupby`, `zip_longest`, `cycle`, `starmap`, `takewhile`, `dropwhile`, `pairwise`, `compress` and `filterfalse` now pull one item at a time, as CPython's do. Before, they read their whole input up front, so they hung on infinite inputs (`chain(count())`), missed items added after they were made, and ran key functions and callbacks in the wrong order. A grouper from `groupby` ends when the parent moves on. `map`, `zip`, `groupby` and `pairwise` can be re-entered from a callback, and so can a class's `__next__`. Re-entering an iterator that can't be re-entered raises `ValueError` instead of crashing. `iter()` raises CPython's `TypeError` when `__iter__` returns an object without `__next__`, and `zip(strict=True)` reports a longer later argument. Found by the new stdlib conformance slice: on `test_itertools`, coverage goes from 4.5% to 55% and wrong answers fall from 26 to 5.
- **Brevi's jinja2 compiles templates to closures.** Each template node is turned into a closure on its first render instead of being walked by an interpreter on every render; a `{% for %}` whose body never uses `loop` makes no loop object (as Jinja does), and each render copies the globals into its context in one pass. A ChatML chat template with tools went from 2.8 s to 1.35 s for 20,000 renders (CPython with real jinja2: 0.60 s; was about 4.8x slower, now about 2.2x). New test `tests/jinja2/compiled.py` checks loops, includes, macros, filters, tests, finalize, autoescape and blocks against jinja2 3.1.6.
- **Fixed: in Brevi's jinja2 a template included inside a `{% for %}` could read the loop's `loop` variable;** in Jinja it cannot.
- **Library copies are compiled once and reused.** A program that imports one of Brevi's library copies (jinja2, mcp, openai, pydantic, polars, ...) used to compile the whole copy, about 940 KB of Rust and 40 to 150 s of rustc, into every program. Debug and test builds now compile the copies into a separate cached library, keyed by its exact source, and build only the program against it, so the second jinja2 program builds in about 1 s instead of 98 s. Release builds (`brevi build --release`) are unchanged. Two builds that need the same library or runtime now wait for one build instead of both building it. A cold `tools/ci_local.sh --full` run on 4 cores went from 25 to about 15 minutes, with every check unchanged.
- **MCP change events are native.** A server on the official `mcp` SDK that calls `ctx.notify_tools_changed()`, `notify_prompts_changed()`, `notify_resources_changed()` or `notify_resource_updated(uri)` now builds with Brevi's own `mcp` instead of falling back to CPython. The events go to each open `subscriptions/listen` stream that asked for them, stamped with its subscription id, over stdio and over streamable HTTP. `MCPServer(subscriptions=...)` takes a custom bus, `mcp.server.subscriptions` (`InMemorySubscriptionBus`, `ListenHandler`) and `mcp.shared.subscriptions` are there for the low-level `Server`, and `resources/subscribe` / `resources/unsubscribe` handlers with `session.send_resource_updated` stay native for handshake-era clients. New tests (`tests/mcp_sdk/subs.py`, `subslow.py`, and a listen stream in the HTTP transcripts) match CPython's mcp 2.3.0 line for line.
- `tools/modelbox.py`: a folder passed into the box through a symlink (such as `/mnt/project-files`) can be reached by that path inside, not only by the resolved one, so checks that name `/mnt/project-files/...` run in the box again.
- **Model-written code runs only in a box, now for the release test too.** The box from `tools/ds_contrib.py` moved to `tools/modelbox.py` and got tighter: `/run`, `/home`, `/var/tmp` and `/dev/shm` are empty inside (no host sockets such as telemetry or databases), it has its own IPC and hostname, the whole box is killed on a timeout, and a self-check scans every file the box can read and refuses to start if one holds the value of any of the harness's secret variables. `bench/e2e.py` and `bench/replay.py` now build and run every solution a model writes in the box (they used to run with the full environment, so a solution that printed it would have sent the keys to the model's provider), with their own build cache. The DeepSeek harness may edit only `src/`, `tests/`, `compat/` and `docs/` (the files it commits), so it can no longer change scripts that run outside the box. `tests/modelbox_test.py` (in `tools/ci_local.sh`) checks the box holds and refuses a leaky one. The environment dump in one committed DeepSeek session log is removed.
- CI rejects a pull request that adds a built program or a file over 2 MB (`tools/no_binaries.py`), and `.gitignore` ignores files without an extension at the top of the checkout, where `brevi build` without `-o` writes its output. This removes 44 test programs that were committed by mistake in #347.
- Native numpy: `argsort()` with the default kind on arrays with repeated values (int64, int32, float64, float32)
  now gives numpy's exact order instead of refusing. It follows numpy's own CPU path (x86-simd-sort on AVX-512 or
  AVX2, introsort otherwise); ties off x86, and the few cases numpy hands to
  std::sort, still refuse. (DeepSeek-authored, Claude-reviewed.)
- **Native numpy float32 `@`, `dot` and `matmul`** match numpy bit for bit on AVX-512 CPUs, by reproducing OpenBLAS 0.3.34's SkylakeX kernels (sdot, sgemm, sgemv_t/sgemv_n and the small sgemv_t kernel). Matrix @ vector with 6 or 7 columns, and every float32 product off AVX-512, still runs in CPython.
- Native numpy: `np.exp`, `np.tanh`, `np.sin`, `np.cos` and `np.log1p` of float32 arrays, and `np.linalg.norm` of a float32 array (default norm), now run in Brevi's own numpy with numpy's exact float32 results, on CPUs with AVX-512. Other CPUs keep running these programs in CPython. float32 `@` / `np.dot` still runs in CPython.
- **A fresh cache no longer hangs dynamic builds that use a library copy.** With an empty `~/.cache/brevi`, a program using numpy, onnxruntime or another bundled library waited forever for a build lock whose folder did not exist yet; the lock now makes its folder, and a lock that can't be made at all no longer blocks the build.
- **onnxruntime tests pass on onnxruntime 1.31.1.** 1.31 changes the last float32 digits of one encoder output in `tests/onnxruntime/inference.py`, which now prints that output rounded; Brevi's onnxruntime matches CPython's on 1.29 and 1.31 (pin bumped to 1.31.1).
- **ONNX Runtime works on Windows.** Brevi's own onnxruntime copy now loads onnxruntime.dll with LoadLibraryA and gets the C API through OrtGetApiBase, skipping a loaded library that is not ONNX Runtime's (an old wheel's onnxruntime_providers_shared.dll). Model, optimized-model and profiling paths reach the C API as the UTF-16 ORTCHAR_T strings it uses on Windows. On Windows x64 with onnxruntime 1.16 or later (`pip install -U onnxruntime`; 1.13 and earlier wheels ship no capi/onnxruntime.dll), a minimal InferenceSession program runs natively and `brevi verify` says identical.
- **Brevi's polars runs its per-row loops in Rust.** Comparisons, `+ - *` on int and float columns (with polars' integer wrapping), float sums, filters, row gathers, one-key `group_by` and building a column from a Python list now run as native column kernels (`_brevi_col`), with the same results as the Python loops they replace, which still run for anything the kernels don't model (big ints, nested values, mixed kinds). A 20,000-row filter, `with_columns` and `group_by` went from 0.30 s to 0.06 s (CPython with real polars: 0.02 s; was about 14x slower, now about 3x). New test `tests/polars/kernels.py` covers nulls, NaN, -0.0, mixed int/float comparisons, Int8/UInt8 wrapping and bool arithmetic.
- `0 ** -1`, `0.0 ** -2.5`, `pow(0, -1)` and `x **= -1` with a zero base now raise CPython's
  `ZeroDivisionError("0.0 cannot be raised to a negative power")` on every build instead of printing `inf`, and
  the error can be caught on the typed build. `pow(a, -1, m)` returns the modular inverse, or raises
  `ValueError("base is not invertible for the given modulus")`. Found by the fuzz harness. (DeepSeek-authored,
  Claude-reviewed.)
- **Brevi's pydantic validates fields without validators faster.** A field with no `@field_validator` no longer builds a `ValidationInfo` and a closure per value, and a model without model validators skips its `ValidationInfo` too: validating many models runs about 23% fewer instructions.
- **`getattr(f, "name", default)` on a function without that attribute** returns the default without raising and catching an AttributeError inside the dynamic build (jinja2 asks this of every filter it calls).
- **Brevi's pydantic validates models faster again.** `object.__setattr__(obj, name, value)` in the dynamic build stores the attribute directly when no class from `object` on defines `__setattr__` (it used to copy the MRO and build a bound method for every call); a `str` field with no constraints returns the value at once; field validation reads the model's config once per model instead of once per validation, and skips tracking used keys unless `extra` is `forbid` or `allow`. Validating 50,000 nested models went from 1.35 s to 0.99 s (CPython with real pydantic: 0.11 s).
- **Fixed in the dynamic build: `object.__setattr__(obj, name, value)` and `super().__setattr__(name, value)` now go through a property's setter** (and raise AttributeError for a property without one), raise TypeError for a non-str name or a wrong argument count (it used to crash), as CPython does. New test `tests/dyn/object_setattr.py`.
- **A repeated group over a long text no longer overflows the stack on the typed build.** `re.match('(x)*', 50000 * 'x')` and similar patterns crashed with a stack overflow, because the typed regex matcher recurses once for each repetition of a group. Long texts with such a pattern are now matched on a thread whose stack grows with the text. Found by the stdlib conformance slice (`test_re.test_stack_overflow`, `test_bug_418626`).
- `re.Match` on the dynamic build: `m.group()` and `m[g]` take objects with `__index__` and int subclasses, and an out-of-range or huge index raises IndexError ("no such group"); `m[0] = x` raises CPython's TypeError. `str.format_map` reads its mapping key by key, so a `defaultdict` or a dict subclass with `__missing__` works, and positional fields raise ValueError. Fixed by DeepSeek through `tools/ds_contrib.py` and reviewed.
- **`re` flags behave like CPython's on the dynamic build.** `re.IGNORECASE` and the other flags are members of `re.RegexFlag`, an `IntFlag`. They print as `re.IGNORECASE`, combine as `re.IGNORECASE|re.DOTALL`, show unknown bits in hex, and still work as ints. `re.NOFLAG`, `re.UNICODE`/`re.U` and `re.DEBUG` are there too (`re.LOCALE` is for bytes patterns, which are still refused). A compiled pattern's repr shows its flags, as in `re.compile('p', re.IGNORECASE)`, and cuts the pattern at 200 characters. `Pattern.flags` leaves out `re.UNICODE` under `re.ASCII`. `Pattern.groupindex` is a read-only mapping and `Match.regs` gives every group's span. `re.compile(pattern, flags)` with an already-compiled pattern raises CPython's ValueError. A bytes pattern is refused instead of raising a misleading TypeError. On `test_re`, the dynamic build goes from 57 to 68 matching tests.
- `Pattern.scanner()` on the dynamic build, with `match()` and `search()` that step through the text as CPython's do. A repeat count of 2**32 - 1 or more (`a{4294967295}`) raises OverflowError, as in CPython. On the typed build, `re.sub` replacement templates read `\n`, `\b` and the other escapes, octal escapes such as `\101` and `\0`, and `\g<1>`, and a bad group reference is an error even when nothing matches. Fixed by DeepSeek through `tools/ds_contrib.py` and reviewed.
- The typed build's regex matcher no longer misreads syntax it lacks. A program whose pattern has a named group, a back-reference, a look-around, an atomic group, a possessive quantifier, `\B`, a scoped or mid-pattern inline flag, or a CPython compile error now builds on the dynamic path, which has the full syntax. A compiled pattern used as an object (`p.split(s)`, `repr(p)`, `print(p)`, `p.pattern`, `p.flags`) does the same. Before, the typed build gave wrong matches, or treated `p.split(s)` as `str.split`.
- Regex `\d`, `\w` and `\s` on the typed build now follow Python's Unicode rules, so accented and non-Latin letters and digits match as in CPython (they were ASCII-only). `(?a)` and `re.ASCII` keep the ASCII meaning. On the dynamic build, `\d`, `str.isdigit()` and `str.isdecimal()` no longer take characters such as `Ⅵ` and `½`, which are numeric but not decimal digits.
- `repr()` of a string escapes every character Python treats as non-printable (for example `​` and `\xad`), on both builds, and `str.isprintable()` agrees with it.
- A repeated group whose last iteration matches the empty string keeps that iteration's groups, as CPython does.
- **`brevi build --release` reuses Brevi's compiled library copies too.** Release builds now link jinja2, pydantic, httpx, polars, the MCP SDK and the other library copies from a library compiled once per toolchain and kept in the build cache, as debug builds already did, instead of recompiling them for every program. A `--release` build of an httpx program after a one-line edit went from 50 s to 4.8 s; the first release build that uses a library still pays for it once. The built programs run the same (callgrind instruction counts within 0.4% of a whole build). `BREVI_DYN_SPLIT=0` still builds each program whole.
- The dynamic build's asyncio is faster (task stepping, `gather` and the ready queue run natively, falling back to the Python implementation for subclasses and patched methods), and long-running programs no longer grow memory per coroutine started.
- The dynamic build runs `collections.Counter` natively (all methods and operators, subclasses included), and functions, classes, methods and generators carry CPython's dotted `__qualname__` (`outer.<locals>.f`, `K.m`), used in reprs and argument errors.
- The dynamic build runs `int.to_bytes` and `int.from_bytes` natively, with CPython's defaults, signed two's complement, `__index__` lengths and error messages.
- csv.DictReader keeps a fieldnames list it was given as-is, the `unix` dialect quotes every field as CPython's does, and a dialect given as a class or object is refused rather than read with the default settings (Fable review l03). Written by DeepSeek, reviewed by Claude.
- Classes have `__subclasses__()`, as in CPython (review l04).
- `sys.version_info` is a named struct sequence: `.major`, `.minor` and the rest read as attributes (review l05).
- **`OSError` and its friends now match CPython 3.13.** `OSError(errno, msg, file)` builds the subclass the errno names on each platform, `str()`/`repr()`/`args`/`vars()` of the exception and its `errno`/`strerror`/`filename`/`filename2` fields behave as CPython's C slots do, and `ImportError(name=..., path=...)`, `AttributeError(name=..., obj=...)` and `NameError(name=...)` accept their keyword fields with CPython's exact error messages (tests/lib/review_l06_oserror_errno.py).
- Enum and Flag members can combine `auto()` members in the class body (`RW = R | W`), and Enum members whose names start with one underscore (`_a`) are members, as in CPython.
- **`functools.wraps` and `update_wrapper` copy what CPython copies** (`__module__`, `__name__`, `__qualname__`, `__doc__`, `__dict__`, `__wrapped__`, with `assigned`/`updated`), a def's `__dict__` is writable and kept, `__name__` assignments show in its repr, and `lru_cache` now evicts least recently used results at `maxsize`, keys keyword arguments and `typed=True` apart, and has `cache_info()`, `cache_parameters()` and a `cache_clear()` that resets the counts (review l08). Reading a def's `__annotations__` is still refused, since Brevi does not evaluate annotations.
- `str.maketrans` works as a static method called on the class, with CPython's argument checks and errors (review l09).
- `str.format` numbers fields inside nested format specs as CPython does, and raises its errors for mixed manual/automatic numbering and too-deep nesting (review l11).
- Calling a function with a positional-only parameter by keyword now raises CPython's TypeError (or lands in `**kwargs`), and too-many-positional errors name the keyword-only arguments given, as in CPython.
- `in` on a Flag class accepts composite members (`Perm.READ | Perm.WRITE in Perm`) and valid int values, as CPython 3.12+ does.
- `del` on a name with no value now raises CPython's exact error: `UnboundLocalError` in a function, `NameError: name 'x' is not defined` in a class body (also when the name is deleted twice, or belongs to an enclosing function).
- math.modf keeps the sign of zero (`modf(-0.0)`, `modf(-2.0)`) and gives `(0.0, inf)` for infinity, as CPython does.
- `itertools.islice`, `permutations`, `combinations` and `product` reject negative, oversized and non-integer sizes with the same errors as CPython, in the order CPython checks them.
- A regular expression that defines the same named group twice (`(?P<x>a)(?P<x>b)`) raises `re.error` with CPython's message and position.
- `str.casefold()`, `str.isdigit()`, `str.isdecimal()` and `str.isnumeric()` now follow CPython's Unicode tables exactly (full case folding such as `"ß".casefold() == "ss"`, superscript and non-Latin digits, `"一".isnumeric()`), in the typed and dynamic builds; `tools/gen_unicode.py` regenerates the tables (Fable review m07). Written by DeepSeek, reviewed by Claude.
- **NaN values in containers keep CPython's identity rules.** `x in xs`, list/tuple/dict/set `==`, `.index`, `.count`, `.remove` and dict/set lookups now compare with `is` before `==`, as CPython does, so `n = float('nan'); n in [n]` is True while `float('nan') in [float('nan')]` is False, and computed NaNs are new objects (a fresh identity per `nan + 1`, `inf - inf`, ...). The typed build stops with "NaN identity" and re-runs on the dynamic build only when a NaN is actually compared; programs without NaN keep their speed.
- Dynamic build: changing the size of a set while iterating over it raises `RuntimeError: Set changed size during iteration`, as in CPython (iteration over a set no longer copies it first).
- `asyncio.as_completed` schedules its awaitables in the order given, so equal-time results come back in a stable order, as they do in CPython (review m10).
- `heapq.merge` is lazy and merges exactly as CPython does (it no longer sorts its inputs, and works on infinite iterators).
- Passing the same keyword twice (`f(**a, **b)` with a shared key, `f(x=1, **{"x": 2})`) raises CPython's "got multiple values for keyword argument" TypeError instead of silently keeping one value.
- A caught `StopIteration` has `.value` (the generator's return value or the first argument, else `None`), as in CPython.
- `functools.cmp_to_key` keys compare equal when the cmp function returns 0, raise CPython's TypeError against non-keys, and expose `.obj`.
- `timedelta` raises CPython's OverflowError past 999999999 days and sums its components exactly, big ints included; the typed build re-runs on the dynamic build instead of printing a date CPython would refuse (review m15).
- The dynamic build's `collections.namedtuple` matches CPython: `defaults`, `rename`, `_make`, `_replace`, `_asdict`, `_fields`, `_field_defaults`, subclasses, and the constructor's argument errors (`P.__new__() missing 2 required positional arguments: 'x' and 'y'`). Field reads stay as fast as before.
- `functools.total_ordering` in the dynamic build matches CPython 3.13: the root comparison is chosen as CPython chooses it (`__lt__` over `__le__` over `__gt__` over `__ge__`, inherited ones included), the derived methods short-circuit and pass `NotImplemented` through like CPython's helpers and carry their `__qualname__` and docstring, and a class with no ordering raises `ValueError`.
- **`brevi mcp` no longer lets the model widen its own access.** Over MCP the model writes the calls, so a plain `brevi mcp` now keeps every program sandboxed: a call with `sandbox: false` is refused, and a call can grant only what the person who started the server allowed with `brevi mcp --allow ... --allow-py ... --allow-net ... --allow-read DIR --allow-write DIR` (nothing by default). `brevi mcp --trust-calls` keeps the old behaviour for a client trusted as yourself; the `verify` tool needs it. `--http` passes these flags on. The Python and Node SDKs start `brevi mcp --trust-calls`, since their calls are the host program's, so they work as before. (Found by the 10 November review.)
- **Sandboxed runs are checked by default, without CPython.** The MCP `run` tool under the sandbox, and `brevi run --verify --sandbox` on the command line, now compare the typed build's answer with Brevi's dynamic build, built under the same grants and run under the same limits. When they differ, the answer is the dynamic build's, with a `{"verify":"differs","against":"dynamic",...}` line. This catches all nine critical typed-build repros from the review (lists shared or sorted in a function, `int(1e20)`, `round(x, -2)`, ...). Programs that read the clock, write files or use the network, or that take another build, are not compared and say so. Before, the sandboxed default returned the typed answer unchecked.
- **`--limit written` holds to the byte.** It was read from `/proc` between writes, so one large write could pass it (2 GB got through a 1 MB limit). It is now counted by Brevi's own file layer before each write, on every OS, and like `files` and `net` it needs `--sandbox` without `--allow native` or `--allow-py` (refused otherwise). It counts bytes written to files; stdout and stderr stay `output`, the network `net`. Under it SQLite, DuckDB and ONNX Runtime write no files of their own (their bytes could not be counted): database files open read-only and temporary data stays in memory. `files` and `net` are now passed to the program on Windows too.
- **A sandboxed program starts without the caller's environment.** Under `--sandbox` the program gets only the search path, locale, time zone, terminal size, temp folders and Brevi's own settings (with `--allow-py` or `--allow native` also the home folder and proxy and certificate settings), so keys and tokens in the caller's environment are not in `/proc/self/environ`, even with a read grant.
- **MCP argument checks.** Each `allow_read` / `allow_write` item is one folder or file (an item with a comma is refused, no longer split in two); `code`, `patch` and `text` must be strings (a number is no longer taken as program text); and after a wrong answer with `sandbox: false`, only that same code runs with CPython for the rest of the session, not every later program with the same file name.
- **`--limit threads` is held by the kernel.** It was the kernel's thread count read every few milliseconds, so a program could start many threads between two reads, and processes it started were not counted. Now each program runs in a cgroup of its own whose `pids.max` is the limit, set before its first instruction, so the kernel refuses the thread or process past it, counting everything the program starts. It needs a pids cgroup Brevi can create (the cgroup v1 pids controller, or a cgroup v2 subtree it may write) and is refused elsewhere, as outside Linux. The MCP `written` limit's description no longer says Linux only.
- Dynamic build: set displays of constants print in CPython's order: a display whose values repeat an earlier one in the module shares its order (CPython merges equal constants), and an add after a discard reuses the freed slot as CPython does.
- **Programs that use different parts of one library now share its build.** The cached library of Brevi's copies now holds only the large copies (32 KB of Python or more) and what they import. Small re-export modules such as `mcp.types` or `mcp.server.lowlevel` build with the program, so MCP servers that import different ones share one library build: on a cold cache, five of the six MCP test servers now build in 2 to 8 s instead of about 100 s each.
- **Showcases rerun on Brevi 0.26.0.** On 10 November: SQLite MCP server 26x, the official MCP time server 21x, token count 11x for one file and 2.2x for a tree, micrograd 9.7x, microgpt 1.3x, all with the same output as CPython (site docs and `bench/showcase/results.json`).
- Websites: brevi-lang.org and brevi.run are redesigned. A dark header, hero and footer around light or dark pages (with a switch in the header), the benchmark table with a bar per program, CPython's own test results as one bar per version, a Copy button on the install command, and the install command and GitHub link at the foot of every page. No numbers or claims changed.
- sqlite3 is about as fast as CPython on point queries, inserts and row iteration (it was 1.7x to 3x slower): statements are cached per connection and a repeated `execute()` and `fetchone()`/iteration run natively, with CPython's errors, `row_factory`, `description`, converters and `rowcount` unchanged. Written by DeepSeek, reviewed by Claude.
- **The DeepSeek provider test runs again.** `bench/test_deepseek.py`'s end-to-end case put its task folder in `/tmp`, which the model box (used by the release test since #389) hides, so the check could not find the program and `tools/ci_local.sh` failed; the folder is now under `bench/work`, which the box can see.
- Typed build: reading a missing key of a `defaultdict` (`d[k]`) inserts the key with its default, as in CPython (so `len`, iteration and `in` agree afterwards), instead of sending the program to the dynamic build. +6 saved agent programs now build typed.
- **Typed path: `except ZeroDivisionError` and `except IndexError`.** A `try` that catches these no longer sends the program to the dynamic build: inside such a try, and in every function it can reach (directly, through methods, or through `map`/`sorted` lambdas), division, modulo and indexing raise a catchable error with CPython's message; code outside such tries is unchanged. An uncaught `raise ValueError` with no message now reports the class name. On the agent-program corpus 5 more programs build typed (571 to 576 of 833), and every one prints what python3 prints. Written by DeepSeek through `tools/ds_contrib.py`.
- Python ints on the typed build are never wrapped or capped. `int(1e20)` printed 9223372036854775807 and `int(-1e30)` printed None; a program whose int outgrows 64 bits (a factorial, `int()` of a big float, a big power used in a list) stopped with "attempt to multiply with overflow". Now `brevi run` (and the MCP `run` tool without cases) runs such a program again from the start on the dynamic build, whose ints have no limit: it gets the same stdin, only output the first run had not written yet is passed on, and that run must write the same output first or it is stopped with an error. A program that writes files, reaches the network or runs native code is not run twice: it stops with an error that says so, as does a binary from `brevi build` run by itself (build with BREVI_DYN=1 for big ints there).
- `(h * 1000003 + ord(c)) % MOD` and other int sums of products taken `%` a number are worked out in 128 bits, so a rolling hash modulo 2**61 - 1 stays on the typed build and gives Python's answer.
- `round(x, n)` matches CPython for floats with negative n (`round(1234.5678, -2)` is 1200.0, was 1235.0) and n over 15 (`round(1e-20, 20)` was 0.0), and for Fractions with negative n; `round(Decimal, n)` outside 0..9 runs on the dynamic build.
- `x << n` no longer drops bits past 64 on the typed build (`(1 << 62) << 2` was 0), and `x >> n` past 63 is 0 or -1 as in Python instead of an error.
- **The typed build no longer gives wrong answers when two names share a list.** The 10 November review found seven ways the typed build copied a list, dict or set where Python shares it: `xs.sort()` in a function, the same row appended in a loop, a stored list changed later, inner lists shared (`[row] * 3`), a global set again to another type after a function uses it, a field list sorted in a method, and a list cleared while a loop walks it; deleting from a list in a loop also stopped the program. The alias check matched patterns; it now has a proof behind it over the whole program (`src/share_guard.rs`) that follows which names, fields, function results and containers can hold the same list, through calls, returns, loops and lambdas. A program is built typed only when no shared list is changed while another name for it is still used, or when the typed build provably shares it too: the parameter is passed by `&mut`, the loop walks the list in place, or the object is a class instance or row, which rustc checks. Anything else is built on the dynamic build, which follows Python's rules. A function that sorts, reverses, extends or adds to a list it is given now changes the caller's list in the typed build as well, so `tests/verify/shared_sort.py` is typed and identical. A loop that rebinds its loop variable (`line = line.strip()`) no longer writes into the list.
- **The fuzzer shares lists.** `tools/fuzz.py` makes aliasing cases (one case in four; `--alias-only` for only those), and the review's repros and the fuzzer's finds are in `bench/fuzz/corpus/share_*.py`, which every pull request replays against CPython on both builds.
- A result equal to one of the typed build's marks for None (`-(2**62) * 2`, which is -2**63; `-1.7976931348623157e308`; `chr(0) + "None"`) printed None. It now runs on the dynamic build like an int past 64 bits. `int("123456789012345678901234")` on the typed build no longer raises ValueError.
- Re-running a program on the dynamic build after an int passed 64 bits no longer slows ordinary runs: the program writes straight to stdout and stderr and reads stdin itself (the prelude counts and hashes what it wrote, and keeps what it read from a pipe), so output and file input cost about the same as before (measured within 2%), and piped input about 1% more wall time and 3-5% more CPU. Past 64 MB of piped input the copy is dropped and an int past 64 bits stops with an error that says so.
- Typed path: a module-level `MISSING = object()` sentinel that is also told apart from None (or only compared with
  `is`/`==` and guarded before real use) now stays on the typed build as its own value, instead of sending the
  program to the dynamic build. +6 corpus programs typed. (DeepSeek-authored, Claude-reviewed.)
- **More programs keep the typed build under the sharing proof.** The proof now follows a call of a local lambda into its body, passes on what a nested function captures to the nested functions that call it (`expr()` calling `eat()`, which changes the enclosing `pos`), counts a callback's change to its enclosing function's own list as that list's change only, and knows that compiled regular expressions and their matches, and standard-library classes such as `datetime.datetime.strptime` and `os.path.join`, change none of their arguments. Of the 551 corpus files the first version of the proof sent to the dynamic build, 391 are typed again (was 356). Setting the current key of a dict a loop walks keeps the typed build too.
- **A test hook for the sandboxed verify check.** `BREVI_TEST_TYPED_DIFFERS=1` adds a line to the typed build's output under `run --verify --sandbox`, so `tests/mcp_test.py` checks that a wrong typed answer is caught without depending on a real typed-build bug staying open.
- **More agent-written programs build typed.** `f(*T)` builds typed when T is a tuple literal set once at the top level, or a local or global set once from a function whose every return is a tuple of one length (`ok(*parse(sq))`-style code; it used to say "takes 2 arguments, got 1" and take the dynamic build). A compiled pattern's `pat.match(s, pos)` and `pat.search(s, pos)`, the loop of a hand-written tokenizer, build typed, with spans counted from the start of the text as in CPython. `float.is_integer()` (and `int.is_integer()`) build typed. Of the 676 Python programs agents wrote in `bench/solutions`, 453 built typed before this change.
- **Poker and warehouse programs back on the typed path, and six more copy-versus-alias wrong outputs fixed.** `(5,) + tuple(ranks)` and `(3, trip) + kickers` (kickers a tuple(...)) are now tuples in the typed build, so a hand score function that returns them next to real tuples builds typed and sorts and prints as CPython does. A list taken from a dict and then changed is written back typed after an `if lst is None:` branch that stores a new list, when a getter function or method gives it (`book.backorders_for(sku)` returning `self.waiting.get(sku, [])`), and when `lst.pop(i)` is the last line of an if branch or loop body. A sweep of 22 aliasing patterns found six more silent wrong outputs, now fixed: `for k, v in d.items(): v.pop(0)`; `lst = d.get(k) or []` then changed; one list put in two dicts then changed; a function returning `x.kids`, or one of two dict items by a condition, whose result is changed; and `get_bucket(k).append(x)` on a function returning `mem[k]`. The last five take the dynamic build. tests/typed and tests/fallback cover each.
- **Windows: `--limit mem`, held by the OS.** On Windows the program now starts suspended, goes into a Job Object with a committed-memory limit, and only then runs, so it is held from its first instruction; processes it starts are in the same job and count too, and anything left in the job is killed when the run ends. At the limit it stops with the usual `{"e":"limit",...}` line and exit 124, under `brevi run`, `brevi verify` and the MCP server's `run`. `cpu`, `file` and `fds` are still refused on Windows: its job CPU limit counts user-mode time only, and it has no file-size or handle limit.
- **Windows: native MCP servers over stdio and HTTP.** Brevi's own mcp copy now runs its server transports natively on Windows x64 instead of sending the program to CPython. A stdio server claims the process's stdin/stdout with `DuplicateHandle` for the protocol and moves the standard handles to NUL and stderr with `SetStdHandle` (as the SDK's `stdio_server` does on Unix with `dup`/`dup2`); `PeekNamedPipe` says when the pipe has bytes or is at EOF (a bare `WaitForMultipleObjects` signals an open pipe even with no bytes), with 1–10 ms sleeps so asyncio timers run while stdin is idle. A server over `run(transport="streamable-http")` or `"sse"` keeps its listening socket and accepted connections in tables of owned `TcpStream`s, and the event loop waits on them with `WSAPoll` (whose readable/writable bits are translated to the poll numbers the loop uses). Session ids come from `BCryptGenRandom`, and the sandbox's listen-host check is unchanged. A minimal stdio server answers `initialize`, `tools/list` and `tools/call` over pipes and exits 0 at EOF, including a tool that awaits a timer while stdin is idle; a streamable-HTTP server answers an `initialize` POST at `http://127.0.0.1:PORT/mcp` (SSE reply, `mcp-session-id`, 421/403 host checks, 413 body limit, DELETE), and the whole `tests/mcp_sdk` transcript suite, including the SSE transport, matches the CPython-generated `.expected` files on Windows.
- **Windows: native requests and httpx.** Brevi's own requests and httpx now use Winsock on Windows instead of sending the program to CPython, as they do on Linux and macOS: a non-blocking connect, `select` for the connecting socket and `WSAPoll` for readiness, `ioctlsocket`/`closesocket`, with the same sandbox host checks, timeouts and CPython-style errors (`[WinError 10061] No connection could be made because the target machine actively refused it`). TLS loads OpenSSL with `LoadLibraryA`/`GetProcAddress` from absolute paths only: `BREVI_SSL_LIB` / `BREVI_SSL_DIR`, or the Python DLL folder embedded at build time (no program-folder, CWD, PATH or bare-name candidate). OpenSSL on Windows has no path to the Windows certificate store, so verification also loads a CA bundle: `SSL_CERT_FILE`, `REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE` or `CURL_CA_BUNDLE`, else certifi's bundle embedded at build time. A local HTTP and HTTPS server, the whole `tests/http` requests and httpx suites, and `https://example.com` build, run and verify natively on Windows x64.
- **Windows: native sqlite3.** Brevi's own sqlite3 now loads Windows' `winsqlite3.dll` from System32 with `LOAD_LIBRARY_SEARCH_SYSTEM32` instead of sending the program to CPython. A `sqlite3.dll` in the program's folder or CWD is never loaded; `BREVI_SQLITE_LIB` can name another absolute library outside the sandbox. Windows' SQLite version can differ from CPython's, so version-specific output may differ.
- **Python libraries on Windows.** A Windows program that imports a library Brevi does not build itself (PyYAML, pandas, ...) now builds natively and runs those calls in CPython through the bridge, as on Linux and macOS, instead of handing the whole file to `python3`. Both typed and dynamic builds use it; the bridge loads `pythonNN.dll` (found from the `python` on PATH, or `BREVI_PYTHON`), and `brevi verify` certifies its output on Windows. `--standalone` is still Linux only.
- **Windows text output uses CPython's CRLF.** A native run on Windows now writes `\r\n` for every text newline, as CPython's text streams do; before, it wrote `\n`, so `brevi verify` reported every program with a newline as differing on Windows. This covers `print` with `sep=`/`end=`, `sys.stdout.write`, csv rows and stderr in both builds.
- **Generators and async on Windows.** Dynamic builds on Windows now run generator and async stacks on Win32 fibers instead of refusing them, so `yield` and `async def` build, run and verify natively there (no CPython fallback).

## v0.26.0 (9 Nov 2026)

The libraries agents use most now build natively. A Python program that uses numpy, tiktoken, Hugging Face tokenizers, requests, httpx, pydantic, the `anthropic` and `openai` SDKs, the official `mcp` SDK (over stdio and HTTP), jinja2, polars, sqlite3, duckdb, safetensors or onnxruntime builds with Brevi's own copies of them, with the same output and no CPython at run time, and so do `async` / `await`, `logging`, `open()` and `zoneinfo`. `.bv` files get the same libraries, and a `.bv` file written in plain Python builds like a `.py` file. The sandbox can hold file access to named folders, holds CPython to those grants on Linux, takes more resource limits, and closes two holes in the tokenizers. `brevi verify` certifies that a native build answers as CPython does, and `pip install brevi`, `npx brevi` and `cargo install brevi` all work. In the release test (24 tasks, two runs) Brevi cost 1.07x (Sonnet) and 1.00x (Haiku) of CPython, every task passed on both sides and all 96 final programs built natively. On CPython's own tests, 58.2% of scored tests match (57.9% in v0.25.0) and 44 give a different answer (45). Thanks to @timoconnellaus for the fix that makes a `def` with no `return` give `None` ([#205](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/pull/205)).

- **MCP servers over HTTP build natively.** A server on the official `mcp` SDK run with `mcp.run(transport="streamable-http")` or `transport="sse"` (or `run_streamable_http_async` / `run_sse_async`) now builds into one native binary, on Brevi's own HTTP/1.1 server. It supports sessions, SSE and JSON responses (`json_response`), stateless mode (`stateless_http`), the standalone GET stream, `DELETE`, idle timeouts and session limits, and the 2026-07-28 single-request exchange with its routing headers. It applies the Host and Origin checks a local server turns on (`transport_security`), and it answers request for request as the SDK on uvicorn does: the tests compare four recorded transcripts with CPython's (`tests/mcp_sdk/http/`) and run the official client over both transports. `MCPServer` also serves `subscriptions/listen` now (acknowledged, with no events, since publishing them is not native yet), so `server/discover` reports the same capabilities as the SDK. Under `--sandbox` an HTTP server listens only on a host the caller granted (`--allow-net 127.0.0.1`). Still on CPython's SDK: the Starlette apps (`streamable_http_app()`, `sse_app()`, custom routes), `event_store` and auth.
- **`brevi mcp --http`: Brevi's MCP server over HTTP.** `brevi mcp --http [HOST:]PORT` serves the same tools over Streamable HTTP (each POST one JSON-RPC message, a request answered with its reply as JSON) at `/mcp`, so a client that cannot start a process, such as one outside a hosted sandbox, can use Brevi. Any address other than 127.0.0.1 needs a bearer token (`--token` or `BREVI_MCP_TOKEN`), and requests from non-local web origins are refused unless allowed with `--allow-origin`. It works with the official MCP client (tests/mcp_http_test.py); [templates/README.md](templates/README.md) says how to reach it on E2B, Daytona, Modal and Cloudflare.
- **asyncio child processes run natively.** `asyncio.create_subprocess_exec` and `create_subprocess_shell` (with `stdin`, `stdout`, `stderr` as `PIPE`, `STDOUT` or `DEVNULL`, `cwd` and `env`), `Process` (`communicate`, `wait`, `returncode`, `pid`, `send_signal`, `terminate`, `kill`) and its pipe streams (`read`, `readline`, `readuntil`, `readexactly`, `async for`, `write`, `drain`, `close`) used to send the program to CPython. Brevi's asyncio now has them, with pipes read as the child writes, as CPython reads them, so a child filling its pipe never stalls. Under `--sandbox` a child process needs `--allow native`, since the program it starts runs outside the sandbox. Loop methods (`loop.subprocess_exec`) and file or fd redirections still run in CPython.
- **Async code next to a library CPython runs builds natively.** A program with `async def` that also uses a library Brevi has no copy of (one CPython runs inside the program) used to go to CPython whole. It now keeps the dynamic build, its event loop and its speed, unless a coroutine could cross between the two: a coroutine function or lambda handed to the library, an async method on a subclass of one of its classes, an async def it decorates, or something it made being awaited or handed to asyncio (an async library such as `aiohttp`). Those still run in CPython.
- **`logging` runs natively, and MCP servers that log build natively.** A program that imports `logging` used to take it from CPython, and an MCP server (or any async program) that logs went to CPython whole. Brevi now has its own logging, written to answer as CPython 3.13's does: loggers and their hierarchy, levels, `basicConfig`, `StreamHandler`, `NullHandler`, `Formatter` (`%`, `{` and `$` styles, `asctime`, `datefmt`), filters, `LoggerAdapter`, `extra=`, `exc_info=`, `logger.exception`, `disable`, and the same stderr text, but for traceback frames, which Brevi leaves out as it does for an uncaught exception. Also new in dynamic builds: `sys.exception()`, `sys.exc_info()` and `__context__` on an exception raised inside an `except` block. Handlers that write files, `logging.handlers`, `logging.config` and formats that name the caller's line, file or function still run in CPython.
- **Showcases page.** [brevi-lang.org/docs/showcases](site/docs/showcases.md) times real Python programs built with Brevi against CPython running the same files, with matching output required. First up: an MCP server over SQLite on the official `mcp` SDK, which answers its first tool call in 29 ms where CPython takes 614 ms (21x), as one 16 MB binary that doesn't need Python. `python3 bench/showcase/run.py` reruns every showcase. Second: the official MCP time server ([mcp-server-time](https://pypi.org/project/mcp-server-time/)), built from its published `server.py` with no edits, answers its first tool call in 29 ms where CPython takes 650 ms (22x), as one 17.5 MB binary without libpython.
- **Native `open()`, `os.listdir` and `os.walk`.** Reading and writing files with the builtin `open()` (text and binary, `r` `w` `a` `x`, encodings, newline handling, `with`, iteration, `print(file=f)`), listing folders with `os.listdir` and `os.walk`, and `os.path.exists`, `isfile`, `isdir`, `islink` and `getsize` used to send a program to CPython; they are now native, with CPython's order, reprs and error messages. Under `--sandbox` they need the read (or write) grant and stay inside `--allow-read` / `--allow-write` folders. A third showcase uses them: a ttok-style token counter that counts one file in 27 ms where CPython takes 371 ms (14x), and Brevi's own 1.8M-token source in 0.32 s against 0.73 s (2.3x). A fourth showcase is Karpathy's micrograd, built unchanged, training an MLP for 30 steps in 5.5 s where CPython takes 30.3 s (5.5x).
- **Native `zoneinfo` and `tzlocal`.** `ZoneInfo` reads the system's tz database (TZif files and their POSIX TZ rules) and gives CPython's answers for every zone and transition in it: offsets, DST, names, PEP 495 `fold` for repeated and skipped times, `available_timezones` and its errors. `tzlocal.get_localzone_name` and `get_localzone` follow tzlocal 5.4 on Linux and macOS. These used to send a program to CPython.
- **Native `importlib.metadata.version`.** A program asking for the version of a distribution it names gets the version installed for the build machine's python3, baked in when it is built; any other name raises `PackageNotFoundError`.
- **MCP server middleware, natively.** `server.middleware.append(fn)`, `MCPServer(middleware=[...])` and `mcp.server.context` (`ServerRequestContext`, `CallNext`, `HandlerResult`) now build with Brevi's own `mcp`, as does `MCPServer(subscriptions=False)`. A pydantic model whose field names a class defined later can call `model_rebuild()`, and a field typed as an optional model gets no `title` in its schema, as pydantic does.
- **More resource limits.** `--limit` (and the MCP tools' `limits`) now also takes `written=MB` (bytes written to files, pipes and sockets, not counting stdout and stderr; Linux), `threads=N` (threads at once; Linux), `fds=N` (files and sockets open at once), `files=N` (files the program creates) and `net=MB` (bytes sent and received over the network). `files` and `net` are counted by Brevi's own file and network code, so they need `--sandbox` without `--allow-py` or `--allow native`; the rest hold any program. A program stopped by one exits 124 with the usual JSON line naming it.
- **Sandbox file grants can be held to folders.** `--allow-read DIR,...` and `--allow-write DIR,...` (MCP tools: `allow_read`, `allow_write`) let a sandboxed program read or write files only inside those folders, where `--allow read` and `--allow write` grant every file the user can reach. Each file the program opens is checked by its real path, so `..` and symlinks that lead outside are refused with a `PermissionError`. It holds for `open()`, polars, safetensors, sqlite3 (`ATTACH` too), duckdb SQL (through DuckDB's `allowed_directories`), onnxruntime and the CA files of `requests` and `httpx`.
- **Fixed sandbox holes in Brevi's tokenizers.** Under `--sandbox`, `Tokenizer.from_file(path)` read the file while the program was built with no read grant, and `Tokenizer.from_pretrained` downloaded from the Hugging Face Hub with no network grant. `from_file` now needs `--allow read` (or `--allow-read` covering the file), and a download needs `--allow-net huggingface.co`; a model already in the Hugging Face cache needs nothing.
- **CPython is held to the sandbox's file grants.** Under `--sandbox`, code that CPython runs (granted with `--allow-py` or `--allow native`, and the C extensions it loads) used to have every file the user can reach, whatever `--allow-read` / `--allow-write` said. On Linux, a program that loads CPython under `--sandbox` now holds itself to its file grants with Landlock before CPython starts: CPython can read the granted folders, its own files and a few system files (certificates, DNS and time zone files), and write only in the folders granted write; anything else is a `PermissionError`. With no read or write grant at all it reaches no file of yours, so a program that used `--allow-py` to read files now also needs `--allow read` (or `--allow-read DIR`). A library granted from the program's own folder (`--allow-py mylib`) still imports. Where Landlock is missing (macOS, Windows, Linux before 5.13), a run that names folders is refused rather than given every folder. Linux kernel limits (`cpu`, `mem`, `threads`, `fds`, `written`, ...) always held CPython; `files` and `net` still need a program without CPython.
- **numpy runs under `--sandbox` with no grant.** Brevi's own numpy (and so its safetensors and onnxruntime) used to need `--allow-py numpy` under `--sandbox` and the MCP tools, a grant that loads CPython and with it full trust. The sandbox check now knows which numpy functions Brevi has: they build natively with nothing granted (Brevi's numpy has no file, network or process access), and anything it lacks (`np.save`, `np.linalg.eig`, `float16`, ...) is refused by name, with `--allow-py numpy` as the fix. `.bv` files that import Brevi's library copies (numpy, jinja2, tokenizers, sqlite3 and the rest) also run natively under `--sandbox` with no grant, on the dynamic build; tests/check.sh now runs every tests/bv_libs program that way.
- **Brevi files use Brevi's native libraries.** A `.bv` file that imports numpy, `tiktoken`, `tokenizers`, `requests`, `httpx`, the `anthropic` and `openai` SDKs, `pydantic`, `jinja2`, `markupsafe`, `polars`, `sqlite3`, `duckdb`, `safetensors`, `onnxruntime`, `textwrap`, `io` or `argparse` now runs them on the same native engine a `.py` file gets, instead of CPython: no libpython at run time, and the same output (numpy arrays print and compute exactly as in a `.py` build). The typed program calls into the dynamic build's runtime (new `src/engine.rs` and `src/engine_rt.rs`); the dynamic build still checks every library call the program makes at build time, and a program using anything Brevi's copies lack keeps CPython's library, as before. Methods with Brevi names on library values now mean the library's method (`z.max()`, `a.round(3)`, `s.strip()`), `{v:.3f}` formats a library value, `int(v)` and `float(v)` work on any library result, and a Brevi lambda passed to a library sees library values. Also fixed for `.py` files: a program using numpy together with jinja2, polars or another of Brevi's library copies was refused (the copies' own `.partition()` or `.fields` were checked as numpy calls), and numpy's "cannot reshape" message now prints the shape as numpy does (`(4,4)`, `(2,newaxis,4)`). In the `.bv` parity audit (every native `.py` test saved as `.bv`), programs that run natively went from 31 to 50 of 196, and ones that needed CPython from 18 to 1. Not yet: `os` and `pathlib` (CPython), and `.py` files built typed, which keep trying the dynamic build first.
- **Model weights in float16 and the small integer types load natively.** safetensors tensors and onnxruntime results of float16, int8, uint8, int16, uint16, uint32, uint64 and complex64 used to stop the program with NotImplementedError; they are now numpy arrays of those dtypes, as with CPython's numpy: `dtype`, `shape`, printing (float16's own shortest digits), indexing and slicing, `reshape`/`transpose`/`copy`, `tolist()`/`item()`, `astype(...)` to a type Brevi computes in, saving them back with safetensors and passing them to onnxruntime. Math on them (`+`, `.sum()`, `np.sqrt`) raises NotImplementedError naming the `astype` that makes it work, so nothing is computed in another type. BF16 and the float8/float4 types fail as they do with numpy (which lacks them): `KeyError` from `load()`, numpy's error from `safe_open`.
- **Faster dynamic builds, and Karpathy's micrograd builds natively.** A program's own package imported as `from pkg.module import ...` now builds with the program when everything it imports can (it used to go to CPython, which also gave the package its own copy of modules like `random`), as does a package with relative imports (`from .engine import Value`, `from . import nn`, `from ..engine import x`) or `from pkg import submodule`. So micrograd's MLP training runs natively, 5.5x faster than CPython with the same output. Attribute reads and writes use per-site caches, `for a, b in zip(x, y)` and two-name tuple unpacking no longer build tuples, objects keep their first four attributes inline, and tuples are one allocation. A recursive inner function (micrograd's `build_topo`) no longer leaks every object it saw: microgpt's training used 1.4 GB and now uses 95 MB. Also fixed: `urllib.request.urlretrieve` was reported as a misspelt `urllib` method. Later: float `+ - * /` and `+=` take an inline path, attribute caches also match a name whose text sits at another address, list and tuple loops step inline, and glibc keeps freed memory instead of returning it to the kernel each step. microgpt's forward pass is now 1.5x faster than CPython's; its backward pass is still slower, so the whole run is too.
- **anthropic's structured outputs run natively.** `client.messages.parse(output_format=Model)` and `output_format=` on `messages.stream` and `count_tokens` used to send the program to CPython's anthropic; Brevi's copy now has them, built on Brevi's pydantic: the request carries the model's JSON schema transformed as the SDK transforms it (`additionalProperties: false`, unsupported constraints moved into the description), text blocks get `parsed_output`, `ParsedMessage.parsed_output` gives the first one, and reprs, `to_dict()` and `model_dump()` print as the SDK prints them. `output_format` takes a model, `list[int]` or any type a pydantic field can have. A type alias (`Fmt = list[int]`) works too. Tool runners stay CPython's.
- **openai's structured outputs and stream helpers run natively.** `client.chat.completions.parse(response_format=Model)`, `chat.completions.stream(...)`, `client.responses.parse(text_format=Model)`, `responses.stream(...)` and `openai.pydantic_function_tool` used to send the program to CPython's openai; Brevi's copy now has them, built on Brevi's pydantic. The request carries the model's strict JSON schema as the SDK makes it, `message.parsed`, `output_parsed` and tool calls' `parsed_arguments` come back as the program's models, and the stream helpers give the SDK's events (`content.delta` with the partly parsed JSON so far, `content.done`, `tool_calls.function.arguments.delta` / `.done`, `refusal.*`, `logprobs.*`, the Responses API's `response.output_text.delta` with its snapshot) and `get_final_completion()` / `get_final_response()`, sync and async. Streaming an existing response (`responses.stream(response_id=...)`) stays CPython's. Also fixed: a response field with an API name of its own (`schema` for `schema_`) was also kept as an extra field, so its repr showed it twice.
- **pydantic's JSON schemas and TypeAdapter run natively.** `Model.model_json_schema()` (with `by_alias=` and `ref_template=`) and `TypeAdapter(T)` (`validate_python`, `validate_json`, `validate_strings`, `dump_python`, `dump_json`, `json_schema`) used to send the program to CPython's pydantic; Brevi's pydantic now has them, with pydantic's output byte for byte: `$defs` for nested models and enums, a `$ref` for a model that refers to itself, sorted keys, field titles, defaults, constraints and docstrings, and TypeAdapter's errors titled as pydantic-core titles them (`list[int]`, `constrained-int`). `TypeAdapter(list[int])` takes any type a model field can have. Still CPython's: `mode="serialization"` schemas and custom `schema_generator`s.
- **Python-style `.bv` files written with Brevi's spellings get the dynamic build too.** When the typed build refuses a Python-style `.bv` file (one with `def`, `class` or `import` lines, where Brevi already gives Python's meaning) that uses Brevi's own spellings, Brevi now writes the same program in Python's spellings and builds it over dynamic values, as it does a `.py` file: `fn` is `def` (and `async fn` is `async def`), `fn f(x) = e` returns `e`, a function's last bare value is returned, `x => e` is `lambda x: e`, `"{n}"` strings are f-strings (`{{` and `}}` as Brevi reads them), `f(n: 1)` passes `n=1`, `&&` `||` `!` are `and` `or` `not`, `true`/`false`, `.push(x)` is `.append(x)`, and `for i in a..b` is `range(a, b)`, in comprehensions too. So `async fn` with `await`, `**kwargs`, dict subclasses and the rest of what only the dynamic build has now work in such files. A spelling with no exact Python form (`(a, b) => ...`, `match` arms, `fail(...)`, Brevi-only methods such as `.len()`) keeps the typed build's error. `BREVI_BV_DYN=0` turns it off, and `BREVI_BV_DYN=print` shows the Python it builds.
- **Methods in `type` blocks, and `super()` in methods.** A Brevi `type` can now hold `fn` methods that take `self` (`fn deposit(self, n): self.balance += n`, `fn rich(self) = self.balance > 100`), called as `a.deposit(5)`; the type keeps its fields, defaults, keyword constructor and printing. In a class, `super().name(...)` calls the base class's method from any method, not only `super().__init__(...)`, and methods the base calls on `self` still reach the subclass's own.
- **A `.bv` file written in plain Python builds like a `.py` file.** When a `.bv` file is Python-style (`def`, `class`, `import`), is valid Python, uses none of Brevi's own names (`fail(...)`, `.push(...)`) has no plain string Brevi would interpolate (`"total {n}"`) and no function ending in a bare value Brevi would return (`x * 2` as its last line), it now gets everything a `.py` file gets: Python's meaning for every construct, and the dynamic build when the typed one refuses it (dict subclasses, `super()`, `**kwargs`, `nonlocal`, `async def`, sets, the full standard library). It never falls back to python3: a `.bv` file is a Brevi program. A file that misses any of these tests is built as before, as Brevi. In the `.bv` parity audit, programs that run natively as `.bv` went from 50 to 160 of 196; of the rest, 19 need a library or local test server the audit did not have (their `.py` versions too), and 17 stay Brevi files: plain scripts with no `def`, `class` or `import` (in Brevi `3 / 2` is 1), or files with Python escapes and `{name}` strings that mean something else in Brevi. `BREVI_BV_PY=0` turns it off.
- **`safetensors` and `onnxruntime` build natively.** A Python file that loads weights with `safetensors` or runs a model with `onnxruntime` now builds on Brevi's own numpy: no CPython at run time, and the same arrays, printed output and errors as safetensors 0.8 (numpy framework) and onnxruntime 1.29. safetensors is a port of the format's Rust crate (header checks with serde_json's messages, the crate's tensor order and padding, its slicing rules): `safe_open` (`keys`, `metadata`, `get_tensor`, `get_tensors`, `get_slice`), `save`, `save_file`, `load`, `load_file`, for F64, F32, I64, I32 and BOOL tensors. onnxruntime runs ONNX Runtime itself through its C API (the library inside the installed package, or `libonnxruntime`): `InferenceSession` from a path or bytes, inputs, outputs and model metadata, `run` with output names and `RunOptions`, `SessionOptions`, the enums and the binding's `[ONNXRuntimeError]` exceptions, with float, int and bool tensors and ZipMap outputs (lists of dicts). Under `--sandbox`, weights and model files need `--allow read`; `save_file`, optimized models, profiles and config entries naming files need `--allow write`; a model given as bytes needs nothing unless its weights are in external data files. A small safetensors script runs in about 5 ms where CPython takes about 100 ms, a small ONNX script in about 70 ms against 245 ms; inference runs on the same engine. What they lack (other frameworks, half-precision and 8-bit dtypes, string tensors, `OrtValue`, `IOBinding`, GPU providers) runs with CPython's library or is raised as `NotImplementedError`; see [safetensors and onnxruntime](site/docs/compatibility.md#safetensors-and-onnxruntime).
- **`sqlite3` and `duckdb` build natively.** A Python file that uses `sqlite3` now builds with Brevi's own sqlite3, a port of CPython 3.13's over the system's SQLite library, and one that uses `duckdb` with Brevi's own duckdb, written to answer as the duckdb 2.0.0 package does over DuckDB's own engine (the library inside the installed duckdb package, or `libduckdb`): no CPython at run time, and the same rows, types, `description`, `rowcount`, transactions and error messages (exception classes, SQLite's and DuckDB's own texts, DuckDB's `LINE 1:` context), down to DuckDB's printed tables (column widths, shortened and hidden columns, `? rows` footers, wrapped values). sqlite3: connections, cursors, `Row`, `?` and named parameters, `executemany`, `executescript`, the legacy and `autocommit` transaction modes, adapters, converters (`PARSE_DECLTYPES`, `PARSE_COLNAMES`), `iterdump`, limits and every constant. duckdb: connections and cursors, the default connection and the module functions, the unfinished-result rule of 2.0, parameters (positional and named), relations (`sql`, `table`, `filter`, `project`, `order`, `limit`, `aggregate`, the aggregate shortcuts, joins, `union`, `except_`, `intersect`, `create`, `insert_into`, `show`) and values of every common type (DECIMAL, HUGEINT, dates and times, intervals, lists, arrays, structs, maps, enums, blobs). Under `--sandbox`, a database file needs `--allow read` (and `--allow write` unless opened read-only), and SQL cannot reach other files: SQLite's file layer is replaced so `ATTACH` and `VACUUM INTO` open nothing ungranted, and DuckDB's external access, extensions and settings are locked unless both grants are given. A small duckdb script runs in about 40 ms where CPython takes about 145 ms; queries run on the same engine. What they lack (Python functions in SQL, backups, blobs, data frames, Arrow, DuckDB's expression API, extensions) runs with CPython's library as before; see [sqlite3 and duckdb](site/docs/compatibility.md#sqlite3-and-duckdb).
- **`polars` builds natively.** A Python file that uses polars dataframes now builds with Brevi's own polars, written to answer as polars 2.1.0 does: no CPython at run time, and the same output, down to the printed tables (column widths, wrapping and truncation, float formatting, `pl.Config`), dtypes and error messages. Covered: `DataFrame`, `Series` and `LazyFrame` (each step runs at once; `collect` returns the result), expressions (`pl.col`, `pl.lit`, `pl.all`, `pl.exclude`, dtype selection, arithmetic with polars' integer, float and null rules, comparisons, Kleene `&`/`|`, `when`/`then`/`otherwise`, `over`, `filter`, `sort_by`, `cast`, `fold`/`reduce`, the `*_horizontal` functions, `concat_str`, `format`, `int_range`), aggregations (`sum`, `mean`, `median`, `std`, `var`, `quantile`, `min`, `max`, `n_unique`, `first`, `last`, `count`, `len`, `skew`, `kurtosis`, `corr`, `cov` and more, with polars' own summation order), `group_by` (with `maintain_order`, iteration and `agg` of any expression), joins (`inner`, `left`, `right`, `full`, `semi`, `anti`, `cross`), `pivot`, `unpivot`, `explode`, `unique`, `sort`, `top_k`, `describe`, `value_counts`, `rank`, cumulative and rolling functions, the `str` and `list` namespaces, `rows`, `to_dicts` and `to_dict`, and CSV, JSON and NDJSON reading and writing with polars' inference and number formatting. Files are read and written through a new native file helper: under `--sandbox` a program that reads or writes a file needs `--allow read` or `--allow write` (`read_csv(io.StringIO(...))` and `write_csv()` to a string need nothing). Small scripts run far faster than CPython with polars, which takes about 200 ms just to start (3.6 ms for a group-by on a few rows, in a 15 MB binary); on large frames Brevi's copy is slower than polars' Rust engine (about 13x on a 20,000-row filter and group-by). Not native yet (the program runs with CPython's polars, or `--sandbox` refuses it unless the caller grants `--allow-py polars`): temporal, struct, categorical, decimal and 128-bit dtypes, Parquet, IPC, Excel, databases and cloud storage, SQL, `polars.selectors`, numpy, pandas and Arrow interop, `list.eval`, `sample`, `rolling` by time, `explain`. Also fixed in dynamic builds: a comparison on a value from a CPython library (`df["a"] > 1` with CPython's polars or pandas) gave a plain `True`/`False` instead of the library's result, and so did `operator.lt`, `operator.eq` and the rest on objects; `isinstance(x, slice)` raised. [polars](https://brevi-lang.org/docs/compatibility#polars).
- **`jinja2` builds natively.** A Python file that renders Jinja templates (chat templates, prompts, reports) now builds with Brevi's own jinja2 and markupsafe, written to answer as jinja2 3.1.6 and markupsafe 3.0.3 do: no CPython at run time, and the same output, whitespace, escaping and error messages (syntax errors with their line numbers, undefined values under `Undefined`, `StrictUndefined`, `ChainableUndefined` and `DebugUndefined`, missing templates). Jinja's own lexer and parser are ported; where Jinja compiles a template to Python and `exec`s it, Brevi's copy runs the parsed template directly, following what the generated code does (scoping of loops, macros, blocks and includes, `loop` variables, `namespace`, `caller`, `super()` and `self`). Covered: `Environment` (all its delimiter, whitespace, `autoescape`, `finalize`, `undefined` and line-statement options, `filters`, `tests`, `globals`), `Template`, `from_string`, `get_template`, `select_template`, `compile_expression`, `make_module` and `module`, every built-in filter and test, `{% extends %}`, `{% block %}` (with `scoped` and `required`), `{% include %}`, `{% import %}`, `{% macro %}`, `{% call %}`, `{% filter %}`, `{% set %}` blocks, `{% with %}`, `{% autoescape %}`, `{% raw %}`, the `do` and `loopcontrols` extensions, `pass_context` / `pass_eval_context` / `pass_environment`, `DictLoader`, `FunctionLoader`, `PrefixLoader`, `ChoiceLoader`, and markupsafe's `Markup`, `escape` and `soft_str`. Hugging Face chat templates (ChatML with tools, Llama 3, Mistral with `raise_exception`) render byte for byte. Checked against jinja2 on 1,800 templates across every whitespace, undefined and delimiter option. Compiling a template is a little faster than CPython's jinja2; rendering one is slower for now (about 0.3 ms for a ChatML chat with tools, 8x CPython's). Not native yet (the program runs with CPython's jinja2, or `--sandbox` refuses it): `FileSystemLoader`, `PackageLoader` and `ModuleLoader`, `enable_async` and `render_async`, `generate` and `stream`, the sandboxed environment, the `i18n` extension and `{% trans %}`, custom extensions, bytecode caches, `NativeEnvironment`, and the `jinja2.meta`, `nodes`, `lexer` and `parser` APIs. Also fixed in dynamic builds: `hasattr(x, "__html__")` was `True` for strings and numbers, and `str()` of a `str` subclass with its own `__repr__` printed the repr. [jinja2](https://brevi-lang.org/docs/compatibility#jinja2).
- **Agent platform templates: E2B, Daytona, Modal and Cloudflare.** The new `templates/` folder runs the Python an agent writes inside a hosted sandbox, compiled and held by Brevi: an E2B template, a Daytona snapshot, a Modal image, a Cloudflare Sandbox Worker (`POST /run` with a bearer token), and plain Docker for your own machine. All use one image (`templates/image/Dockerfile`: Debian, the Rust toolchain, the latest Brevi release checked against its SHA256SUMS, python3) and one model tool, `run_python(code, stdin)`, which has no field for permissions: the grants (`--allow`, `--allow-net`) and limits (`--limit`) come from the caller's code, and the platform's network switch follows the grant (no internet unless the program is granted the network). `templates/agent.py` is a Claude tool loop over any of them (`--platform e2b --allow-net api.github.com --limit cpu=5,mem=256`). `templates/test.py --docker` runs refused reads, network and subprocesses and each limit in the real image; the Cloudflare Worker was run under `wrangler dev` with its container. The E2B, Daytona and Modal templates are checked against their SDKs but not yet run on those platforms.
- **The weekly benchmark is public.** `bench/weekly.py` turns each Monday's release-test runs into a row of [bench/WEEKLY.md](bench/WEEKLY.md), [bench/weekly.json](bench/weekly.json) and the new "Weekly agent-code benchmark" table on the site's benchmarks page: cost per run with Brevi and with CPython for Claude Sonnet and Haiku, the ratio, tasks finished, programs built natively, and how long the finished programs took on their tests. `bench/e2e.py` now logs each task's wall time (`secs`) and the test run time of its last passing check (`run_ms`); on two small tasks Brevi's programs ran their tests in 9 ms where CPython took 83 ms.
- **Resource limits for programs, set by the caller.** `brevi run`, `brevi test` and `brevi verify` take `--limit cpu=SECS,mem=MB,time=SECS,output=KB,file=MB` (any subset; `mem=1gb`, `output=512kb`, `time=500ms` work too), and the MCP `run`, `test` and `verify` tools take `limits: {"cpu": 2, "mem": 512, "time": 10, "output": 64, "file": 10}`. CPU time, address space and the size of each written file are kernel limits set in the program's process before it starts, so nothing in it (CPython under `--allow-py` included) can raise them; wall-clock time and output are held by `brevi`, which stops the program when either runs out, keeping exactly the output up to the limit. A program stopped by a limit exits 124 with one JSON line naming it (`{"e":"limit","on":"cpu","limit":"2 s",...}`); in `run` with `cases` the case reports it, and `brevi verify` gives the new verdict `limit`, which certifies nothing (its certificate gains a `"limit"` count). Limits work with or without `--sandbox`, hold the program and not the build, and a limit the OS cannot enforce is refused instead of ignored (`mem` on macOS; `cpu`, `mem` and `file` on Windows). New [docs/THREAT_MODEL.md](docs/THREAT_MODEL.md) says what the sandbox and the limits protect against and what they do not.
- **MCP servers build into one native binary.** A Python MCP server written with the official `mcp` SDK now builds with Brevi's own copy of mcp 2.3.0: `MCPServer` (tools, resources and resource templates, prompts, completions, `Context` logging and progress, lifespan, structured output) and the low-level `Server`, over stdio, on both protocol eras (2024-11-05 to 2025-11-25 and 2026-07-28). No CPython or virtualenv at run time; the binary answers its first request in about 25 ms where CPython takes about 0.75 s, and needs no sandbox grant.
