Schemas, validation errors, JSON-RPC errors, cancellation and hang-up behave as the SDK's, checked by eight recorded client sessions against four servers (`tests/mcp_sdk/`). `brevi verify` plays a `.jsonl` input to an MCP server as a client session (each request once the last reply is in), so a server can be certified against CPython. Not native yet: the HTTP transports, sampling, elicitation and roots ([details](https://brevi-lang.org/docs/compatibility#mcp-mcp-servers)).
- `raise X from Y` sets `X.__cause__` (and `__suppress_context__`) in dynamic builds.
- **Brevi watches everything it depends on.** Dependabot now also updates the GUI runtime's crates, CI's Python tools and the Docker base image, one grouped PR per ecosystem each Saturday. The Upstream releases workflow now also checks the MCP specification, runs new releases of the Python libraries Brevi mirrors (numpy, requests, httpx, pydantic, tiktoken, tokenizers, anthropic, openai, jinja2) against Brevi's test programs in CPython, next to the pinned version, and opens an issue showing exactly which outputs changed (`tools/parity_drift.py`, pins in `tools/upstream_pins.json`), and looks up OSV security advisories (RustSec, GitHub, PyPA) for all of it (`tools/advisories.py`). numpy 2.5.4 checked: output unchanged from 2.5.3.
- **`brevi mcp` speaks MCP 2026-07-28** besides the handshake versions 2024-11-05 to 2025-11-25: `server/discover`, the per-request `_meta` envelope (protocol version, client info and capabilities), results stamped with `resultType` and the server's info (lists with `cacheScope` and `ttlMs`), and the SDK's errors for an unsupported version (-32022) or an incomplete envelope (-32602), as mcp 2.3.0 sends them ([#241](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/241)).
- **The `anthropic` and `openai` SDKs build natively.** A Python file that calls Claude or GPT models through the official SDKs now builds with Brevi's own copies of anthropic 2.6 and openai 3.46, over Brevi's httpx: no CPython at run time, and a one-call program starts in about 10 ms where CPython spends 0.5 to 0.9 s importing the SDK. Sync and async clients, `messages.create` and `chat.completions.create` (also streamed), `messages.stream` with `text_stream` and `get_final_message`, tool use and tool-call deltas, `count_tokens`, embeddings (the SDK's base64 default decoded as float32), the Responses API with `output_text`, `models.list` pages, `with_options`, the SDKs' errors, retries and timeouts. Response objects are the SDKs' own models, generated from the SDKs, so reprs, `to_dict()`, `to_json()` and `model_dump()` print the same; six test programs against a mock API (`tests/llm/server.py`) give output identical to CPython's (`brevi verify`). Under `--sandbox` they need the network granted (`--allow-net api.anthropic.com`). Not native yet: beta APIs, batches, files, tool runners, raw responses, Bedrock, Vertex and Azure ([details](site/docs/compatibility.md#anthropic-and-openai)).
- **pydantic builds natively.** A Python file that uses pydantic v2 models now builds with Brevi's own pydantic, written to answer as pydantic 2.13 (pydantic-core 2.46) does: no CPython at run time, and the same values, reprs, dumps, JSON and validation errors, down to each error's type, location, message, input and context, union labels, cut-short input reprs and JSON parse errors with their columns. The compiler reads each model's annotations into the field types (annotations are still never evaluated), so models, `Optional`, `Union` (smart mode), `Literal`, `list`, `set`, `frozenset`, `dict`, `tuple`, `Annotated`, enums, nested and self-referencing models (string annotations and `from __future__ import annotations` too), type aliases and the constrained types (`PositiveInt`, `StrictStr`, ...) work. Covered: `BaseModel` (`model_validate`, `model_validate_json`, `model_validate_strings`, `model_construct`, `model_dump` and `model_dump_json` with `mode`, `include`, `exclude`, `by_alias`, `exclude_unset`/`defaults`/`none` and `indent`, `model_copy`, `model_fields`, `model_fields_set`, `model_extra`, `model_post_init`), `Field` (defaults, `default_factory`, aliases, number and length constraints, `pattern`, `strict`, `frozen`, `exclude`, `repr`), `field_validator` (before, after, wrap and plain, `"*"`, `ValidationInfo`), `model_validator` (before, after, wrap), `computed_field`, `AfterValidator`/`BeforeValidator`/`PlainValidator`/`WrapValidator`, `PrivateAttr`, `ClassVar`, and `ConfigDict` (`extra`, `frozen`, `strict`, `validate_assignment`, `populate_by_name`, `from_attributes`, `use_enum_values`, `str_strip_whitespace` and the other `str_` options, `validate_default`, `arbitrary_types_allowed`, `title`, `hide_input_in_errors`). A built program validates under `--sandbox` with nothing granted, and starts in 4 ms where CPython with pydantic takes 120 ms; validating many models in a loop is slower than pydantic's Rust core for now (about 15x on 100,000 small models). Checked against pydantic on 1,300 random programs and hand-written ones. Not native yet (the program runs with CPython's pydantic, or `--sandbox` refuses it): `model_json_schema`, `TypeAdapter`, generic models, `RootModel`, discriminated unions, `datetime`/`UUID`/`Decimal`/URL/email fields, `field_serializer` and `model_serializer`, the v1 API (`.dict()`, `@validator`, `class Config`), `alias_generator`, `context=` and `validate_call`; serializer warnings are not printed. Also fixed: what Brevi's tokenizers, tiktoken, requests and httpx copies lack was only noticed after `import M`, not after `from M import ...`, so such a program could build with a copy that then failed at run time. [pydantic](https://brevi-lang.org/docs/compatibility#pydantic).
- **`requests` and `httpx` build natively, and the sandbox gets a network grant.** A Python file that uses `requests` or `httpx` now builds with Brevi's own copies of requests 2.34 and httpx 0.28, written over native sockets and TLS (the system's OpenSSL, loaded on the first HTTPS connection): no CPython at run time, and the same headers on the wire (and their order), URL quoting, redirects, cookies, encodings, decoded gzip and deflate bodies, and exceptions with their messages as CPython's libraries. Covered: requests' module functions, `Session`, `Response` (`iter_content`, `iter_lines`, `stream=True`, `json`, `raise_for_status`, ...), basic auth, timeouts and environment proxies; httpx's module functions, `Client`, `AsyncClient`, `stream`, `URL`, `QueryParams`, `Headers`, `Cookies`, `Timeout`, `Auth` flows, event hooks and every exception class. `httpx.AsyncClient` runs on Brevi's asyncio, so `gather` over many requests and server-sent events read with `aiter_lines` work as in CPython; 100 requests at once finish in about half CPython's time. Under `--sandbox` they need the new `net` capability: `--allow net`, or `--allow-net api.example.com,localhost` to allow only those hosts (MCP tools: `allow_net`), which the program cannot widen; no `allow native`. Not native yet (these run with CPython's library): HTTP/2, `files=`, custom transports and adapters, client certificates, SOCKS proxies, `DigestAuth`, and Windows. Also fixed in dynamic builds: `hasattr(3, "__iter__")`, `hasattr(gen, "__aiter__")` and similar were `True` for every dunder name. [requests and httpx](https://brevi-lang.org/docs/compatibility#requests-and-httpx).
- **`brevi verify`: proof that the native build answers as CPython does.** `brevi verify x.py --input tests/ -- ARGS` builds the file natively (no fallback), runs it and `python3` on each input, and compares stdout byte for byte, the exit code and stderr (CPython's traceback frames left out). The last line is a certificate an agent or CI job can keep: the verdict (`identical`, `differs`, `unstable`, `timeout` or `not built`), the SHA-256 of the file and of every input, the Brevi and Python versions, the build path and the speedup; it exits 0 only when every input matched. CPython runs each input twice, so output that changes run to run (set order, times, unseeded random) is reported as unstable rather than certified by luck. The MCP server has a matching `verify` tool (`inputs`, `args`, `once`); it runs CPython on the code, which the sandbox cannot hold, so it needs `sandbox: false`. Release notes now also carry the latest CPython conformance number from `bench/conformance/report.md`. [Verify against CPython](https://brevi-lang.org/docs/usage#verify-against-cpython).
- **Silent wrong output fixed: decorators in typed builds.** The typed build of a `.py` file left out every decorator it did not know, so a function under `@tenacity.retry(...)`, `@atexit.register`, `@app.route(...)` or your own decorator ran undecorated. A decorator outside the ones the typed build implements (`staticmethod`, `classmethod`, `property`, `functools.lru_cache`/`cache`/`total_ordering`, `dataclass`, `abstractmethod` and typing's markers) now sends the file to the dynamic build, which applies it.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: `json` options.** The dynamic build ignored `object_hook`, `object_pairs_hook`, `parse_float`, `parse_int`, `parse_constant` and `strict` in `json.loads`/`load`, and the typed build ignored `allow_nan`, `default`, `check_circular` and `skipkeys` in `json.dumps`. All of them now work as in CPython, and so do `cls=` with a `JSONEncoder` or `JSONDecoder` subclass (the usual `default` override with `super().default(o)`), `json.loads` of bytes (UTF-8, UTF-16 and UTF-32 as `json.detect_encoding` reads them), CPython's `Circular reference detected` and `Out of range float values` errors, the UTF-8 BOM error, `Illegal trailing comma before end of object`, and `TypeError` for an unknown keyword. `sort_keys` now sorts before converting keys, as CPython does, so mixed key types give its error.
- **`typing` builds natively, as CPython runs it.** The dynamic build mapped every typing name to `object`, so a `NamedTuple` class could not be built (`takes no arguments`) and `isinstance(x, Iterable)` was `True` for everything (the typed build got that `isinstance` wrong too). Now `NamedTuple` (class syntax with defaults and methods, and `NamedTuple("P", [...])`), `TypedDict` (a plain dict, `total=`), `Generic[T]` and `Protocol` bases, `TypeVar`, `ParamSpec`, `NewType`, `cast`, `assert_never`, `assert_type`, `reveal_type`, `overload`, `final`, `override`, `TYPE_CHECKING` and type aliases build natively with CPython's results and errors. Annotations are never evaluated, as CPython's own `from __future__ import annotations` behaviour. A typing name used any other way (`isinstance(x, Mapping)`, `get_type_hints`, `__annotations__`) is refused at build time and runs in CPython (with `collections.abc` names bridged), never a wrong answer.
- **`enum.IntEnum`, `StrEnum`, `Flag` and `IntFlag` build natively.** Ported from CPython 3.13's enum.py: `auto()` values (the lowered name for `StrEnum`, powers of two for flags), members that act as their int or str everywhere (`format`, `%d`, `json`, `round`, `range`, indexing, `str` methods, `join`), flag `|`, `&`, `^`, `~`, `in`, iteration and `len` over the canonical members, composite values and their repr (`<P.R|W: 3>`), and the `STRICT` (`Flag`) and `KEEP` (`IntFlag`) boundaries with CPython's errors. Also fixed: `format()` and f-strings of a `(str, Enum)` member gave the value where CPython gives `str(member)`, and `'%d' % IntEnum` failed.
- **`dataclasses` follow CPython's dataclasses.py.** `fields()` returns real `Field` objects, `field()` takes all its options (`init`, `repr`, `hash`, `compare`, `metadata`, `kw_only`), and `KW_ONLY`, `kw_only=True`, `InitVar` (passed to `__post_init__`), `ClassVar` (not a field), inheritance (fields in MRO order), `unsafe_hash` and CPython's `__hash__` table, `FrozenInstanceError` on assignment and `del`, `is_dataclass`, `__match_args__`, and the mutable-default and "non-default argument follows default argument" errors match CPython.
- **`atexit` builds natively.** `atexit.register` (also as a decorator) and `unregister` now run in the program itself; before, the file went to the dynamic build's bridge, where every callback failed at exit with "called a function of this program from another thread".
- **Fixed with libraries run in CPython:** keyword arguments to `orjson` (`orjson.dumps(x, option=...)`) failed with `unexpected keyword argument`; keyword names are now interned, as CPython's are. `__doc__` of functions and classes was always `None` in dynamic builds; `__hash__` results above 2**61 were reduced a second time; `del` on an object did not call its `__delattr__`.
- **`tiktoken` builds natively.** A Python file that counts or splits tokens with `tiktoken` now builds with Brevi's own tiktoken: no CPython, no network and no file reads at run time, and the same tokens as tiktoken 0.14 for every OpenAI encoding (`gpt2`, `r50k_base`, `p50k_base`, `p50k_edit`, `cl100k_base`, `o200k_base`, `o200k_harmony`). The compiler builds the vocabularies the program names into the binary (from tiktoken's own cache, else downloaded once from OpenAI's servers with tiktoken's hashes checked), so a built program tokenizes under `--sandbox` with nothing granted. Covered: `get_encoding`, `encoding_for_model` and `encoding_name_for_model` (tiktoken's model tables), `list_encoding_names`, `Encoding(...)` with your own vocabulary or extra special tokens (tiktoken's README recipe), `encode` with `allowed_special` / `disallowed_special`, `encode_ordinary`, the batch functions, `decode` (with `errors=`), `decode_bytes`, `decode_tokens_bytes`, `decode_with_offsets`, `encode_single_token`, `decode_single_token_bytes`, `token_byte_values`, `n_vocab`, `eot_token`, `special_tokens_set`, with tiktoken's error types and messages. Text is split by a regex engine written for tiktoken's patterns (possessive repeats, lookahead, Unicode classes read off tiktoken's own regex library, so its Unicode version matches), and BPE merges in tiktoken's order. Checked against tiktoken on about 60,000 random strings across the encodings; encoding runs at tiktoken's own speed (it is Rust underneath too), and a short token-counting script starts in 40 ms where CPython with tiktoken takes 550 ms. Not native yet (these run with CPython's tiktoken): `encode_to_numpy`, `encode_with_unstable`, `tiktoken.load`, plugins, and `Encoding(pat_str=...)` with a pattern the engine lacks (lookbehind, for one).
- **Hugging Face `tokenizers` builds natively.** A Python file that loads a tokenizer with `Tokenizer.from_pretrained("<model id>")`, `Tokenizer.from_file("<path>")` or `Tokenizer.from_str(...)` now builds with Brevi's own port of tokenizers 0.23.3: no CPython at run time, and the same ids, tokens, offsets, word ids, masks and decoded text. The compiler reads each `tokenizer.json` the program names (from huggingface_hub's cache, else downloaded once) and builds it into the binary, so the program needs no network or file reads and runs under `--sandbox` with nothing granted. Covered: BPE (byte-level as in GPT-2, RoBERTa, Qwen and Llama 3; SentencePiece-style with byte fallback as in Llama 2 and TinyLlama), WordPiece (BERT, MiniLM) and WordLevel models; the normalizers, pre-tokenizers, post-processors and decoders those use; added and special tokens; `encode` (pairs, pre-tokenized input), `encode_batch`, `encode_batch_fast`, `decode`, `decode_batch`, truncation (with stride and overflow) and padding, `add_tokens`, `add_special_tokens`, `get_vocab`, `token_to_id`, `id_to_token`, and every `Encoding` field and position method. Checked against tokenizers on 18 Hub tokenizers with 3,000 random strings each; a short script starts in 35 ms where CPython with tokenizers takes 260 ms. Not native yet (these run with CPython's tokenizers): Unigram models (T5, XLM-R), building a `Tokenizer` from `tokenizers.models`, training, saving, the `tokenizers.*` submodules, and a model id or path the build cannot see (not a literal).
- **Fixed in dynamic builds:** a `UnicodeDecodeError` from `bytes.decode` held only its message, so `repr(e)`, `e.args`, `e.start`, `e.end`, `e.reason` and `e.object` differed from CPython; it now carries CPython's five arguments and attributes.
- **`ExceptionGroup` and `except*` build natively.** `BaseExceptionGroup` and `ExceptionGroup` (with `split`, `subgroup`, `derive`, `message`, `exceptions` and notes, and subclasses that override `derive`) and `try` / `except*` now build in the dynamic build, with CPython's rules for matching, nesting, re-raising and combining what handlers raise.
- **`async` / `await` and `asyncio` build natively.** `async def`, `await`, `async with` and `async for` now build in the dynamic build instead of sending the whole file to CPython, and `asyncio` is Brevi's own: CPython 3.13's asyncio (futures, tasks, the event loop's `_run_once`, locks, queues, timeouts, `TaskGroup`, `asyncio.run`) ported line for line and cut down to callbacks and timers, so tasks start, wake, get cancelled and print in exactly CPython's order. Covered: `run`, `Runner`, `create_task`, `gather` (with `return_exceptions`), `sleep`, `wait` (all three `return_when`s and `timeout`), `wait_for`, `as_completed` (both loops), `shield`, `timeout`/`timeout_at`, `TaskGroup`, `Queue`/`PriorityQueue`/`LifoQueue` (with `join` and `shutdown`), `Lock`, `Event`, `Condition`, `Semaphore`, `BoundedSemaphore`, `Future`, cancellation with messages, `current_task`, `all_tasks`, `get_running_loop`, `new_event_loop` with `run_until_complete`, and the loop's `call_soon`, `call_later`, `call_at` and `time`. A coroutine awaited by another runs in place on the awaiting task's stack, so a chain of awaits costs plain calls; only each task gets its own stack. A `TaskGroup` whose tasks fail raises its `ExceptionGroup` as CPython does. Async generators (`async def` with `yield`, with `asend`, `athrow` and `aclose`) and async list, set and dict comprehensions (`async for` and `await` inside them) build natively too, and a generator left part way is closed by the event loop at the point CPython's is, so its `finally` prints in the same place; `asyncio.run` closes the ones still open at its end. Not native yet (the file runs in CPython, or `--native` refuses it): asynchronous generator expressions, printing a `Task`, and sockets, subprocesses, threads and signals. Checked against CPython on hand-written programs and on random programs mixing tasks, events, queues, locks, semaphores, `gather`, `shield`, `wait_for` timeouts and cancellation.
- **Silent wrong behaviour fixed: `time.sleep` did not sleep in dynamic builds**, and `time.monotonic()` and `time.perf_counter()` read the wall clock (they now read CPython's clock, `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, and `time.process_time()` the process's CPU time). `time.sleep` also checks its argument as CPython does.
- **String arrays build natively.** `np.array(["a", "bb"])`, `dtype=str` / `"U5"` / `np.str_`, `astype(str)` and the scalars they hold now run in Brevi's numpy with numpy 2's rules: the `<U` width from the longest item (or numpy's width for the type in `astype(str)`, `<U21` for int64), assignments that cut a longer string to the width, comparisons and sorting by code point, `+` joining the strings, `==`/`!=` against a number giving all `False`/`True`, and numpy's printing (`array(['a', 'bb'], dtype='<U2')`, with non-ASCII text wrapped by characters). `where`, `unique`, `sort`, `argsort`, `concatenate`, `stack`, `reshape`, `swapaxes`, masks and fancy indexing, `np.random.choice` and the `zeros`/`full`/`empty` family take them too. A `np.str_` works wherever a Python `str` does: dict keys, `str` methods, `join`, `re`, `json`, `int()`, `f"{x:>8}"`. What numpy has no loop for (`a * 2`, `a - "x"`, `sum`, `sqrt`, `mean`) raises numpy's own error.
- **Fixed in native numpy:** `x in array` raised `TypeError`; `np.meshgrid` returned a list where numpy gives a tuple; `np.count_nonzero` returned a Python int where numpy gives `np.int64`; `type(np.float64(1)).__name__` was `numpy.float64` where Python says `float64`. `np.str_(x)` and `swapaxes` (method and function) are native now; they went to CPython's numpy.
- **`float32` and `int32` arrays build natively.** `dtype=np.float32` / `np.int32` (and `"f4"`, `"i4"`, `np.single`, `np.intc`) in constructors, `astype`, `arange` and `linspace`, plus the scalars `np.float32(x)` and `np.int32(x)`, now run in Brevi's numpy with numpy 2's rules: NEP 50 promotion (a Python float keeps a float32 array float32, `int32 + int64` is int64, a Python int too big for int32 raises numpy's `OverflowError`), float32 arithmetic rounded once per operation as numpy's float32 loops do (`//` and `%` too), int32 wrapping with numpy's overflow warnings, float32 pairwise sums, means and variances, and float32 printing (the shortest float32 digits, `np.float32(0.1)`, `1e+06`, `dtype=float32`). On 240 random programs mixing float32, int32, float64 and int64 the output matches numpy byte for byte. Not native yet for float32/int32 (these go to CPython's numpy): `@`/`dot` (OpenBLAS sgemm), `np.linalg`, ufunc `reduce`/`accumulate`, and float32 `exp`, `log`, trig and non-square powers (numpy uses SVML there on AVX-512).
- **Silent wrong output fixed in native numpy.** `np.arange(0, 2.5, 0.5, dtype=int)` gave `[0 1 2 3 4]` where numpy gives `[0 0 0 0 0]` (numpy fills from the first two values cast to the dtype); `np.rint` of an int array gave ints where numpy gives float64; a sum of `-0.0` values gave `-0.0` where numpy gives `0.0`; `int()` of a numpy float that was `nan` gave a number instead of raising; a float array `** 0.5` gave `0.0` for `-0.0` and `inf` for `-inf` (numpy runs `sqrt` for a scalar 0.5, `reciprocal` for -1); the repr of an array over 1,000 items left out numpy 2's `shape=(...)`; a float32-sized tie in the last printed digit could round the other way from numpy (`2.3333332e+06`), and Python's own `repr` of such a float could too (`-254.62014770507812`, typed and dynamic builds); the int64 `//` of the minimum by -1 and `-`/`abs` of the minimum int64 scalar now warn as numpy does. `np.argsort` of repeated values and sorting `0.0` with `-0.0`, without `kind="stable"`, gave a stable order where numpy's quicksort gives its own; they now stop with a NotImplementedError instead (`kind="stable"` works).
- **Fixed with numpy run in CPython:** `np.where(...)` failed with `module 'numpy' has no attribute 'where_'`, and `x ** -1` on an array with `only 0-dimensional arrays can be converted to Python scalars`.
- **Float matrix @ vector and vector @ matrix match numpy to the last digit.** Brevi now adds in the order of OpenBLAS's dgemv kernels on AVX-512 and AVX2 CPUs: a matrix stored row-first goes to dgemv_t (four-row FMA kernel, then the two- and one-row kernels), a transposed one and vector @ matrix to dgemv_n, a single row to ddot, and from 460,800 values the split across threads OpenBLAS makes, which changes which kernel a row gets or splits the sum. `@` on a sliced matrix numpy cannot hand to BLAS adds left to right, as numpy's own loop does. Before, about 1 in 3 such products could differ from numpy in the last digit; 18,090 values from 1 to 13 rows and up to 5,000 columns now all match.
- **Float matrix @ matrix matches numpy to the last digit.** Brevi follows OpenBLAS's dgemm as numpy ships it: on AVX-512 CPUs the small-matrix kernels up to 100 x 100 x 100 (two of the four sum some values in eight lanes and reduce them in one of two orders), beyond that and on AVX2 CPUs the regular kernels, with the k-blocks and the pieces OpenBLAS cuts the product into on one thread or, from 524,288 multiplies, across as many threads as the machine has cores. Transposed, sliced and reversed operands are read the way numpy passes them (numpy 2.5's matmul copies an operand BLAS can't describe; `np.dot` copies to C order), and a one-row or one-column matrix goes to dgemv or ddot as in numpy. Checked on 1.24 million values from 2 x 1 x 2 up to 260 x 900 x 260, on both kinds of CPU. Not yet: the result when `OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS` limits numpy's threads.
- **`A @ A.T` and `A.T @ A` match numpy to the last digit.** numpy hands a matrix times its own transpose to OpenBLAS's dsyrk, which computes one triangle and copies it; Brevi follows dsyrk's blocking on one thread and across threads (from (n + 1) x n x k > 439,776), its 32 x 32 diagonal blocks and its kernels, on AVX-512 and AVX2. Sliced inputs numpy copies first go through dgemm as in numpy.
- **`np.linalg.lstsq`, `np.polyfit` and `np.polyval` build natively, matching numpy to the last digit.** numpy solves least squares with LAPACK's dgelsd; Brevi ports the routines it runs line by line (QR or LQ first when the matrix is much taller or wider, then the bidiagonal form, the bidiagonal SVD with its rotations, and the small-matrix solve), with the BLAS calls they make following OpenBLAS's kernels on AVX-512 and AVX2, including dnrm2's 80-bit x87 sums. `polyfit` supports `full`, `w`, `rcond` and `cov` (scaled and `"unscaled"`), and `lstsq` returns the rank as `np.int32`, as numpy does. Up to 25 columns (or rows, for a wide matrix), which covers `polyfit` to degree 24; larger ones, and inputs holding `nan` or `inf`, stop with a NotImplementedError for now. On large fits Brevi takes about 1.3x (lstsq, 2,000 x 8) to 1.8x (polyfit, 200,000 points) numpy's time, as numpy splits those across threads; small fits are faster than numpy. The corpus of LLM-written numpy programs is now 37/40 native.
- Native numpy's fused multiply-adds now use the CPU's own instruction where it has one (the runtime is built for baseline x86-64, where they were a software routine), which speeds up matrix products and LU with the same results.
- **The rest of numpy's random distributions build natively, with numpy's own numbers.** `poisson`, `binomial`, `geometric`, `negative_binomial`, `hypergeometric`, `zipf`, `logseries`, `gamma`, `standard_gamma`, `beta`, `chisquare`, `f`, `standard_t`, `standard_cauchy`, `lognormal`, `laplace`, `logistic`, `gumbel`, `weibull`, `pareto`, `power`, `rayleigh`, `wald`, `vonmises`, `triangular`, `multinomial`, `dirichlet` and the noncentral `chisquare` and `f`, on both `default_rng` and the older `np.random.seed`/`RandomState` API, which numpy keeps on its own older algorithms (Brevi follows each). Brevi ports numpy 2.5's C code step for step, so a seeded program draws the same values: 1,728 random calls with random parameters (edge cases included, such as `binomial` near its switch between methods) all match numpy exactly, values, errors and the state left for the next draw. Argument checks match too (`lam < 0 or lam is NaN`, broadcast-shape errors), and `normal(0, nan)` now gives `nan` as numpy does instead of an error.
- **`pip install brevi`, `npx brevi`, `cargo install brevi`.** Each release is now published to PyPI, npm, crates.io and the MCP registry (`io.github.ets-public/brevi`, with both the PyPI and the npm package) by `release.yml`, using trusted publishing, so no registry tokens are stored anywhere. The PyPI wheels repack the five release binaries (`tools/make_wheels.py`), so they cost no second build; the npm package fetches the matching release binary on first run; the crate builds from source.
- **`np.linalg.det`, `slogdet`, `solve` and `inv` build natively, matching numpy to the last digit.** Brevi runs OpenBLAS's LU the way OpenBLAS does: its recursive blocking, the getf2 panel, and the order each kernel adds in (the AVX-512 and AVX2 dgemm, dgemv, ddot, daxpy and trsm kernels). On 37,819 values from 28 matrix sizes up to 99 x 99, every one equals numpy's, on both kinds of CPU. `slogdet` returns numpy's `SlogdetResult`, and `matrix_power` takes negative powers. Above 99 x 99 numpy splits the LU across threads; Brevi gives numpy's one-thread result.
- **`np.linalg.norm`, `histogram`, `cov` and `corrcoef` build natively.** Also `np.linalg.matrix_power`, `np.trapezoid`, and `reduce`/`accumulate` on `np.add`, `np.multiply`, `np.maximum`, `np.minimum`, `np.logical_and` and `np.logical_or`. Float `np.dot` of two vectors now adds in OpenBLAS's own order for the CPU (AVX2 or AVX-512, threads split as OpenBLAS splits them), so it matches numpy to the last digit. Also fixed: `"-" * np.int64(3)` raised a TypeError instead of repeating the string.
- **`np.random` builds natively, with numpy's own numbers.** `np.random.default_rng(seed)` gives the same draws as numpy (its SeedSequence, PCG64, ziggurat normal and exponential, and Lemire bounded integers), and so does the older `np.random.seed(n)` API (MT19937, the polar `randn` with its cached value, masked `randint`). `random`, `integers`, `normal`, `uniform`, `standard_normal`, `exponential`, `choice` (with `replace=False` and `p=`), `shuffle`, `permutation`, `rand`, `randn`, `randint` and `RandomState(n)` are covered; any other distribution sends the program to CPython's numpy. 31 of 40 numpy programs written by Sonnet and Haiku use `np.random`: 27 of the 40 now build fully natively, all matching numpy's output.
- **numpy builds natively.** A Python file that uses numpy's core now builds with Brevi's own numpy, written in Rust, with no CPython or numpy at run time: `float64`, `int64` and `bool` arrays with numpy's type rules, views that share memory, masks and integer-array indexing, broadcasting, operators, about 60 ufuncs, reductions with `axis`/`keepdims` (sums in numpy's pairwise order), sorting, `unique`, stacking, and printing that matches numpy 2.x byte for byte. A program using anything it lacks (`np.random` and `np.linalg` for now) gets CPython's numpy, as before. The dynamic build no longer calls CPython's numpy itself: in 5 of 40 numpy programs written by Sonnet and Haiku that gave wrong output or crashed, and those now build typed with CPython's numpy. A loop over small arrays runs 3.7x faster than CPython with numpy, and 2-million-value array work about the same. [Details](https://brevi-lang.org/docs/compatibility#numpy).
- **Silent wrong output fixed: a `def` with no `return`.** In a typed build of a `.py` file, a function's last line was its return value, as in Brevi, so `def f(x): ... x + 1` gave `2` from `f(1)` where CPython gives `None`, and `if f(3):` could take the wrong branch. Only `return` gives a value now, down every branch of a last `if`. Lambdas and `.bv` programs are unchanged. ([#205](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/pull/205), thanks @timoconnellaus)
- **Silent wrong output fixed: `isoformat(timespec=...)`** ([#211](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/211)). The dynamic build ignored `timespec`, so `datetime(..., 123456, tzinfo=timezone.utc).isoformat(timespec="seconds")` printed the microseconds. `datetime.isoformat` and `time.isoformat` now take `hours`, `minutes`, `seconds`, `milliseconds`, `microseconds` and `auto` (as `timespec=` or by position) and refuse any other value as CPython does. `time(..., tzinfo=tz)` used to drop the offset silently; it is now refused with a plain message (or run by CPython for a zoneinfo zone).
- **`zoneinfo.ZoneInfo` works as a datetime's tzinfo** ([#211](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/211)). `datetime(..., tzinfo=ZoneInfo(...))`, `combine(d, t, zone)`, `now(zone)`, `fromtimestamp(ts, zone)`, `astimezone(zone)` and `replace(tzinfo=zone)` stopped with `TypeError: tzinfo argument must be None or of a tzinfo subclass`. The dynamic build now hands those calls to CPython's datetime, so daylight saving time and zone names come out as in python3; fixed-offset datetimes stay native.
- **`datetime.time.fromisoformat`** ([#211](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/211)) gave `AttributeError: 'str' object has no attribute 'fromisoformat'` in dynamic builds. It now parses `HH`, `HH:MM`, `HH:MM:SS[.ffffff]`, the compact `HHMM`/`HHMMSS` forms and a leading `T`; `time.min`, `time.max` and `time.resolution` are there too. `fromisoformat` now refuses one-digit fields (`"19:5"`) as CPython does.
- **Text files read as UTF-8 under the C locale** ([#211](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/211)). python3 reads text as UTF-8 when no locale is set (PEP 538/540), but the CPython a native build embeds for libraries such as `pathlib` decoded as ASCII, so `Path.read_text()` on a non-ASCII file raised `UnicodeDecodeError` in cron jobs and containers with `LANG` unset. The embedded CPython now starts in UTF-8 mode under the C or POSIX locale (unless `PYTHONUTF8` is set).
- **`globals()` lookups build natively** ([#213](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/213)). `globals()` stopped the dynamic build with `globals() is not supported in dynamic builds`, which kept the pipeline's `report.py` from building. `globals()[name]`, `globals().get(name[, default])` and `name in globals()` now read the module's globals live: names not bound yet or deleted are missing, functions get their own module's globals, and a missing name raises `KeyError` as in python3. Anything else on `globals()` (assigning through it, `update`, looping over it, keeping it in a variable) is still refused at build time, so `brevi run` runs that file with python3; a lookup of a dunder name CPython sets itself (`__file__`, `__builtins__`, ...) stops the run rather than give a different answer.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: `except ... as e` names outlived the handler.** Python deletes the name when the handler ends, so a later `print(e)` raises `NameError`; the dynamic build kept the exception, also after a `continue`, `break` or `return` out of the handler.
- **Website copy.** brevi-lang.org and brevi.run say what Brevi does in plainer words: concrete headings, no slogans, sentence-case labels, and the hosted sandbox described correctly (the request grants access, not the program). A broken llms.txt card on the home page is fixed, and the FAQ cost answer and roadmap are shorter.

## v0.25.0 (4 Nov 2026)

The launch release, out ahead of the weekly Monday release. Most of it is correctness: Brevi now measures itself against CPython's own test suite (`tools/conformance.py`), and the fixes it led to cut built programs that give a wrong answer there from 79 to 45 while the share of tests that match rose from 49.2% to 57.9%; every other silent wrong output found in the release runs is fixed too, including `d.copy()` and `list(d)` on a dict in typed builds. 567 of 813 saved agent programs now take the fast typed build, with none differing from CPython. Python files exit 1 on an uncaught exception, Brevi-only syntax in a `.py` file is refused as CPython refuses it, `install.sh` checks its download against `SHA256SUMS`, and the dynamic build's `re` beats CPython on search loops. Release test, two runs on the release tree: Sonnet 1.09x and Haiku 1.01x the cost of CPython, every task passed, no check failed because of Brevi, 95 of 96 final programs native (two earlier runs on the tree just before #207: 1.04x and 1.05x, 96 of 96).

- **Composio example.** [`integrations/composio_agent.py`](integrations/composio_agent.py) gives a LangChain agent Composio's hosted MCP server for app tools (GitHub issues here) next to `brevi mcp`. Composio holds the credentials and makes the calls; the data reaches Brevi's `run` as `stdin`, so the agent's program runs sandboxed with nothing granted.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: list items that start with `\x01`.** On the typed path `print(["\x01a", "b"])` printed `['b']`, because regex matches kept their group positions as a hidden last string and printing a list dropped any such string. A match is now its own type holding the positions apart, so a list shows every item, printing a match shows `<re.Match object; ...>`, and `m.group(9)`, `m[-1]`, `len(m)` and `for x in m` fail as in CPython instead of exposing the hidden text. ([#200](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/pull/200), thanks @timoconnellaus)
- **CPython conformance measure.** `tools/conformance.py` cuts each test of CPython 3.13.13's `test_grammar`, `test_scope`, `test_int`, `test_list` and `test_dict` into a standalone script and compares `brevi build` output with CPython's: at first 49.2% of 311 scored tests matched (153), 59 were refused and 79 built programs gave a wrong answer; after the fixes below from that run, 57.9% match (180), 67 are refused and 45 give a wrong answer (57.6% and 45 with `BREVI_DYN=1`). [Report](bench/conformance/report.md); `--check` fails only on a wrong result not in `compat/conformance_known_wrong.txt`. Not in CI. ([#199](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/pull/199), thanks @timoconnellaus)
- **`install.sh` verifies its download.** The script now checks the release archive against the release's `SHA256SUMS` before unpacking it, and stops with nothing installed on a mismatch or when the check cannot be made (`BREVI_FROM_SOURCE=1` still builds from source). ([#198](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/pull/198), thanks @timoconnellaus)
- **`time.gmtime`, `time.strftime` and `datetime.timezone` build natively** ([#194](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/194)). The dynamic build refused them, so a shared module formatting a UTC timestamp sent 23 of the 27 files of the pipeline in [#175](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/175) to CPython. Now `time.gmtime()` gives a `time.struct_time` (fields, indexing, `tm_zone`), `time.strftime(fmt, t)` formats it, and `time.time_ns()` is there. Datetimes can be aware: `datetime.timezone.utc`, `datetime.UTC`, `timezone(timedelta(...))`, `tzinfo=` in `datetime(...)`, `combine(d, t, tz)`, `replace(tzinfo=...)`, `now(tz)`, `fromtimestamp(ts, tz)`, `utcfromtimestamp`, `astimezone(tz)`, `timestamp()`, `utcoffset()`, `tzname()`, `%z`/`%Z`, offsets in `isoformat()`, `str()` and `repr()`, comparison and subtraction as instants, and CPython's error for mixing naive and aware datetimes. `fromisoformat("...Z")` and `"...+02:00"` now give aware datetimes; before, the `Z` was dropped and the result was naive (silent wrong output). Local time (`time.localtime`, `strftime` without a tuple, a naive `timestamp()`, `fromtimestamp` without tz) still runs in CPython. Aware datetimes passed to a Python library keep their offset.
- **A dynamic-build blocker in an imported module names that module's file** ([#194](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/194)). `brevi check main.py` said `main.py line 4: time.gmtime is not supported` when line 4 was in `_m.py`; it now says `_m.py line 4`, in check, in run's path line and in `--explain`.
- **`(x or "").lower()` on a JSON value builds typed** ([#195](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/195)). `x or ""` where `x` came from `json.loads` was typed as a bool, so the typed build's Rust did not compile and the file took the slower dynamic build. A JSON value `or` a string or int is now a JSON value. Also: `re.search`, `re.sub`, `re.findall` and friends on a JSON string build typed (they stopped the typed build with `no method named re_find found for enum Json`).
- **`json.load(open(path))` builds typed** ([#195](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/195)). It stopped check with `unknown function __open_r()`, which read like an internal error; it now reads the file as `with open(path) as f: json.load(f)` does. Other uses of an `open()` file the typed build cannot take (passing it to a function, for example) get a plain message instead.
- **The dynamic build's `re` is faster than CPython on search loops** ([#195](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/195)). A search now skips straight to the characters a match can start with (also after `\b`, `^` or `\A`, and with `re.I`), a one-character pattern such as `[^a-z ]` scans without backtracking, and searching one text with many patterns converts the text once. On a stand-in for the issue's bench (`re.search(rf"\b{w}", scene, re.I)` over 147 topics and 79 scenes, plus `re.sub`) the dynamic build went from 0.86x to about 1.8x CPython's speed.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: `\N{NAME}` in `.py` files.** `print("\N{BULLET} item")` printed the escape as text on the typed path and sent the program to CPython from the dynamic build. Both builds now resolve names (any case, aliases) from a table in the compiler, 400 KB of binary size; a bad name, or a computed `CJK UNIFIED IDEOGRAPH-`/`HANGUL SYLLABLE` one, is refused and left to CPython. ([#185](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/pull/185), thanks @timoconnellaus)
- **Silent wrong output fixed: float `sum()`.** Typed builds added floats one by one, so `sum([0.1] * 10)` printed `0.9999999999999999` and `sum([1e100, 1.0, -1e100])` printed `0.0`. Every typed float sum (lists, generators, JSON numbers, Python library values, `sum(xs, start)`) is now compensated as in CPython 3.12+ and the dynamic build, and `sum([-0.0])` prints `0.0`. ([#190](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/pull/190), thanks @timoconnellaus)
- **Uncaught exceptions in Python files exit 1, as in CPython.** Native typed builds exited 101, Rust's panic status, and `bench/compare/compare.py` hid it by only comparing zero with non-zero; it now compares exact exit codes. `.bv` programs still exit 101 after a runtime error. ([#187](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/pull/187), thanks @timoconnellaus)
- **A `.py` file with Brevi-only syntax or methods is refused, as CPython refuses it.** `if a && b:`, `x => x + 1`, `fn f(x):`, `0..n`, `true`, `fail("x")` and `xs.push(1)` on a list used to build and print an answer; now `check` and `build` report one error with the Python spelling and `brevi run` falls back to CPython, which gives the real error. A class's own `push` still works, and `.bv` files keep every Python spelling. ([#189](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/pull/189), thanks @timoconnellaus)
- **Silent wrong output fixed: print arguments and tuple items changed by a later one.** `print(xs, grow(xs))`, where `grow` changes the list (or a global, or `self`), printed the list from before the change in typed builds, and `r = (q, q.pop())` kept a copy of `q`; both now go to the dynamic build and print what python3 prints. None of the 580 saved solutions moved off the typed path. ([#188](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/pull/188), thanks @timoconnellaus)
- **Silent wrong output fixed: a Python file the guard could not read.** When the dynamic parser could not read a file (or a module it imports) that the typed build accepted, such as one using `except*`, every guard check was skipped, so `print({3, -1, 5})` printed the set sorted. Such a file is no longer built typed: `brevi run` runs it with python3, and `build` and `check` report an error. Files with a bare `allow` line now get the guard's checks too. ([#191](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/pull/191), thanks @timoconnellaus)
- **Silent wrong output fixed: `d.copy()` and `list(d)` on a dict in typed builds.** `d.copy()` gave the dict's keys, so `print(d.copy())` showed `{'a'}` for `{'a': 1}`, and `list(d)` printed as a set (`{'a'}` for `['a']`). `d.copy()` is now a copy of the dict (also with tuple keys) and `list(d)` a list of its keys. Found from the conformance run.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: comparing dict views in typed builds.** `d.items() <= e.items()` and `d.keys() == {1, 2}` follow set rules in CPython; the typed build compared them as lists. Programs that compare `keys()`, `items()` or `values()` with `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`, `==` or `!=` now take the dynamic build, which now compares key and item views with sets and with each other as CPython does (`values()` views compare by identity, so `d.values() == d.values()` is False). Found from the conformance run.
- **Dict subclasses in dynamic builds: `__missing__` and `dict(mapping)`.** `d[k]` on a dict subclass with `__missing__` now calls it for a missing key (a `Counter`-style `c[k] += 1` works), and `dict(sub)`, `dict(obj)` and `d.update(obj)` copy a dict subclass, or any object with `keys()` and `__getitem__`, as CPython does instead of giving an empty dict.
- **A class with `__del__` runs in CPython.** Both builds free objects at other times than CPython, so a `__del__` that counts or logs ran at the wrong moment or not at all. `brevi run` now runs such a file with python3 and says why; `build` and `check` refuse it.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: `return` inside `try`/`finally` in typed builds.** A function whose `try` body returned, with a `finally` after it, returned None in typed builds and the `finally` never ran; `break` and `continue` out of such a `try` skipped the `finally` too. These programs now take the dynamic build, which runs the `finally` as CPython does. `try`/`except` without a `finally` stays typed. Found by the CPython conformance run ([#199](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/pull/199)).
- **Silent wrong output fixed: string escapes in typed builds.** `'say \"hi\"'` and `"don\'t"` kept the backslash (`don\'t`, and a length of 2 for `"\'"`), and a backslash at the end of a line inside a string was kept instead of joining the lines.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: `for k in obj` over a JSON object in typed builds** walked nothing; it now walks the keys (and a JSON string's characters), as in Python. Seen once `meta = d.get("meta") or {}` built typed.
- **`issue["labels"] or []` and `d.get(k) or {}` on JSON build typed.** They stopped the typed build with ``the trait bound `bool: AxEach` is not satisfied`` and took the slower dynamic build.
- **Dynamic builds raise CPython's errors in more places** (from the conformance run): a list, dict or set method that takes no arguments raises TypeError when given one (`xs.clear(None)`, `d.items(1)`); deleting a dict key and adding another while looping over the dict raises `RuntimeError: dictionary keys changed during iteration`; a slice step, `xs.index()` bound or `int()` base past 64 bits is clamped or refused as in CPython instead of stopping with `IndexError: cannot fit 'int' into an index-sized integer`.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: `"%d items" % (n,)` in typed builds.** A string `%` a one-item tuple printed the item as a list (`[3] items`, `['a']`), because the typed build keeps `(x,)` as a one-item list. It now fills in the item, as CPython does.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: one-item tuples shown whole.** `print((5,))`, a list or dict holding `(x,)`, an f-string of one, or one returned from a function or passed to one printed `[5]` in typed builds. These programs now take the dynamic build and print `(5,)`. The check follows only the one-item tuple itself, so a real tuple that holds one (`(n, path + (u,))`, read back by index) stays typed.
- **A module file with a triple-quoted string over several lines is no longer refused.** `TOKEN_RE = re.compile(r'''...''', re.X)` in an imported `.py` file stopped the build with `a used file holds only fn, type, enum and test items` at the pattern's second line. Seen in a release-run `json_query` program, which now builds and passes its tests.
- **`import json.decoder` works in dynamic builds.** `json.decoder.scanstring(...)` after `import json.decoder` was refused (`json.decoder is not supported in dynamic builds`), and in a file the typed build also could not take, the build failed outright. Seen in a release-run `json_query` program, which now builds natively and passes its tests.
- **`e2e.py --verdict` no longer passes on nothing.** A log glob that matched no files printed `release test PASSED`; it now stops with an error. `--verdict` exits 1 when the bar is missed, and a run without the `anthropic` package or `BENCH_ANTHROPIC_KEY` says what to install or set.
- **The result cache no longer replays a refusal caused by the machine.** When the dynamic build's runtime library could not be built (many builds starting on a cold cache at once, a full disk) or rustc was killed, a Python file was refused with the typed build's reason, such as `cannot work out the type of s`, and that refusal was kept and shown again on later runs until the cache was cleared. Such failures are now never kept, and a crate rustc could not load is no longer cached as an error in the program. Seen in the v0.24.6 release run.

## v0.24.6 (4 Nov 2026)

> **Breaking: sandbox.** Under `--sandbox`, and in the MCP tools, which sandbox by default, a program's own `# allow read`, `# allow write` or `# allow native` line no longer grants anything. Only the caller grants capabilities: `--allow read,write,native`, `--allow-py requests,pydantic` (CPython for just those Python libraries), or the MCP tools' `allow` and `allow_py` arguments. A sandboxed program whose allow line asks for more than the caller granted is refused. If your setup relied on an allow line in the file, pass the flag or MCP argument instead. Without `--sandbox` nothing changes: a program with an allow line is still held to it.

A security fix release, out ahead of the weekly Monday release: from v0.24.1 to v0.24.5 code written by an AI (or anyone) could free itself from the sandbox by adding an allow line, for example to reach the network through requests. It also brings dynamic builds that call Python libraries instead of falling back to CPython, and native `argparse`, `textwrap`, bytes, `hashlib`, `base64` and a faster native `random`. Release test over two runs on the release commit: Sonnet 1.03x of Python's cost (95% range 0.97x to 1.09x), Haiku 1.02x (0.97x to 1.08x), every task passed on both sides, no check failed because of Brevi, and 47 of 48 and 48 of 48 final programs native (one Sonnet `toc` program ran in CPython).

- **Dynamic builds call Python libraries instead of falling back to CPython.** A Python file that the typed build could not take and that imported a library Brevi lacks (requests, pydantic, yaml, sqlite3, logging, ...) used to run entirely in python3. Now the dynamic build hands just those imports to CPython, which it loads into the program as the typed build's pybridge does, and the rest of the program stays native. Classes built on a library's classes (`class Post(BaseModel)`) are run by CPython as a whole, and `open()` is CPython's. The path line says what CPython runs: `{"path":"dynamic",...,"py":["pydantic","requests"]}`. Library values behave as they do in CPython: attributes, calls with keyword arguments, items, operators, iteration (lazy, so generators print in order), `with`, formatting, `isinstance`, and exceptions you can catch by the library's class or a builtin base, with their attributes. Functions of the program can be passed as callbacks, and a list or dict a library changes is copied back. A multi-file pipeline modelled on [#175](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/175) (argparse CLIs, a requests client, pydantic models, JSON files) prints and writes exactly what python3 does. `--sandbox` still refuses these imports unless the caller grants them (see the next entry). `brevi check` and `--native` stay strict.
- **Under `--sandbox` only the caller grants capabilities.** A `# allow read`, `# allow write` or `# allow native` line in the program used to unlock that capability under `--sandbox`, so code written by an AI (or anyone) could free itself, for example to reach the network through requests. Now the sandboxed program's own allow lines grant nothing: one that asks for more than the caller granted is refused with `` `# allow native` grants nothing under --sandbox: only the caller grants native``. The caller grants with `--allow read,write,native`, or `--allow-py requests,pydantic` for CPython on the named Python libraries only (not rust fn, crates, or other libraries); in the MCP tools these are the `allow` and `allow_py` arguments. Either flag implies `--sandbox`. Error hints now name the flag to pass, not a line to add. Without `--sandbox`, a program with an allow line is still held to it. **Breaking:** a sandboxed run that relied on an allow line in the file needs the flag instead.
- **Bytes, `hashlib` and `base64` build natively.** `b"..."` literals were refused by the dynamic build and `hashlib`/`base64` ran in CPython; they now build natively (dynamic build), also under `--sandbox`. Bytes values have `bytes(...)`, `str.encode`/`bytes.decode` (UTF-8, ASCII, Latin-1 with `strict`/`replace`/`ignore`, CPython's error messages), indexing, slicing, iteration, `in`, `+`, `*`, comparisons, hashing and the common methods. `hashlib` has MD5, SHA-1, SHA-224/256/384/512 and `new` (pure Rust, no crates); `base64` has b64, urlsafe and b16 encoding and decoding with CPython 3.13's padding rules and `binascii.Error` messages. Fuzzed against python3: 102 hashes over lengths around the block edges, 900 UTF-8 decodes of random byte soup, every base64 edge case tried. Also: `repr()` of a str in dynamic builds now escapes non-breaking and other separator spaces as CPython does (`'\xa0'`).
- **`argparse` builds natively.** Command-line programs that parse their arguments with argparse (21 of the 27 files in the pipeline of [#175](https://github.com/ets-public/brevi/issues/175)) ran in CPython; they now take the dynamic build, also under `--sandbox`. Brevi builds CPython 3.13's own argparse with the program (tools/mkargparse.py, PSF license), so usage lines, `-h` help, error messages and exit codes are CPython's: 54 runs of three test CLIs (subcommands, groups, choices, `nargs`, `BooleanOptionalAction`, custom types, `parse_known_args`) print exactly what python3 prints. A run takes about 3 ms against 23 ms in CPython. `argparse.FileType` and `fromfile_prefix_chars`, which open files, still run in CPython. Also: `brevi run x.py` gives the program `sys.argv[0] == "x.py"` in dynamic builds, as python3 does; `hasattr(value, "__name__")` and similar dunder data were True for strings, numbers and None (argparse tests it); `raise SystemExit(2)` had no `.code`.
- **`textwrap` builds natively.** `wrap`, `fill`, `shorten`, `dedent`, `indent` and `TextWrapper` (all options but `fix_sentence_endings`) now run in the dynamic build, also under `--sandbox`, breaking lines exactly where CPython does (hyphenated words, em-dashes, long words, `max_lines` and placeholders); 1,000 random texts wrapped at random widths print what python3 prints.
- **`random` builds natively outside the sandbox too.** Brevi's own copy of CPython's generator now has its core (seeding, draws, `getrandbits`, `shuffle`) in native code: a loop of 300,000 `randint` calls and 100 shuffles takes 0.15 s, against 0.62 s with the old copy and 0.42 s through CPython (CPython itself: 0.11 s). So a program using `seed`, `random`, `randint`, `randrange`, `choice`, `choices`, `shuffle`, `sample`, `uniform`, `gauss`, `getrandbits` or `Random` with int seeds now takes the dynamic build instead of calling CPython, and draws the same numbers as CPython for the same seed. Other random functions, or a seed that is not an int literal, still use CPython's random.
- **`brevi check` names what stopped the dynamic build too.** When the typed build could not take a file and the dynamic build refused it as well, check reported only the typed reason (`except KeyError: ... and this file cannot be built over dynamic values`), so fixing that line still left the file unbuilt and the real blocker (`import argparse`, a bytes literal) was shown only by `run --explain`. The message now ends with it, as `(file line N: reason)`, and `brevi run` puts it in its `{"path":"cpython"}` line. The generator hint no longer suggests moving a yield out of a try block when the yield is not in one. Found running check over a 27-file production pipeline, where every file stopped on one shared line in an imported module.

## v0.24.5 (3 Nov 2026)

A fix release for four silent wrong outputs in typed builds, the ones the v0.24.4 release test found in its `trains` and `poker` checks: a list name set to None by a nested function, changes through nested `setdefault`, joining tuples, and comparing JSON values. On the 813-program corpus 565 programs build typed, up from 550. Release test over two runs on the release commit: Sonnet 1.06x of Python's cost (95% range 1.01x to 1.12x), Haiku 1.01x (0.95x to 1.07x), every task passed on both sides, no check failed because of Brevi (5 did in v0.24.4), and 48 of 48 and 47 of 48 final programs native (the one Haiku `cron_next` program ran in CPython).

- **Silent wrong output fixed: comparing JSON values.** In typed builds `j == []` (and `j == {}`) was true for a JSON null, 0, False or `""`, and `False == 0` on a JSON value was false; both now answer as Python does. `v is True` / `v is False` on a JSON value is now an identity test (a JSON 1 equals True but is not True), so template renderers that use it build typed.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: a list name set to None by a nested function.** `players = None` in a nested `def` with `nonlocal players` was not seen by the outer function's `players is None`, so a typed poker program printed an extra empty round and read later hands as duplicates. The outer test now sees it.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: nested `setdefault` changes lost.** `d = labels.setdefault(s, {})`, `lst = d.setdefault(k, [])`, then changing `lst` changed only copies in typed builds, so every trip of a typed trains program printed "no route". The change now reaches `labels`.
- **Fixed: joining tuples.** `(tm,) + lab[k]` gave a list in typed builds (a wrong result, or a TypeError where CPython ran fine); it is now one tuple, also when the right side is held as a JSON value.
- **Crate pins.** The crate test and `examples/checksums.bv` now use `sha2` 0.11 and `base64` 0.23. In sha2 0.11 a digest no longer formats with `{:x}`; the examples build the hex string byte by byte. The docs keep `sha2 "0.10"`, which still works.

## v0.24.4 (3 Nov 2026)

Every one of the 48 final release-test programs from v0.24.3's runs now builds natively, with no CPython: `enum`, `io.StringIO`, `json.JSONDecoder().raw_decode` and path text from `os` and `pathlib` run natively, and exact `fractions.Fraction`, `bin`/`hex`/`oct`, `startswith` with a start index `re.S`/`re.M` and `csv.writer(sys.stdout)` are on the typed path. On the 813-program corpus 550 programs build typed, up from 496, and eight silent wrong outputs are fixed (re groups and tuples, rows changed in a loop, typed `$` and `re.split`, dict keys printed as None). Release test over five runs (four on main before #171 and #172, one on the release commit): Sonnet 1.06x of Python's cost (95% range 1.03x to 1.10x), Haiku 1.03x (0.98x to 1.10x), every task passed on both sides, 120 of 120 and 117 of 120 final programs native. The run on the release commit alone gave 1.02x and 1.03x. Five checks along the way (of 2 tasks, `trains` and `poker`) failed under Brevi where CPython passed the same files; the models fixed their code and passed, and those typed-build wrong outputs are being fixed for the next release. The 3 programs that ran in CPython were split into several files, where a module Brevi could not build (a `with` other than `open`, `except IndexError`) sends the whole program to CPython.

- **Typed path round 3.** On the 813-program corpus 550 programs build typed, up from 522, none moved off it, and the CPython shadow check finds no wrong output. `j.get(k)`, `j.get(k, [])` and `j.get(k, {})` on JSON values, `json.load(sys.stdin)`, `any(f(x, seen) for ...)` where `f` changes `seen`, `deps or []`, names set inside a loop (or just before its `break`) and read after it, and `return None` from a function returning JSON now build typed. A `MISSING = object()` sentinel is read as None when every test of it also tests None. A loop over a set builds typed when its passes cannot depend on order (adding to sets, appending to the element's own entry, counting, setting a flag).
- **Silent wrong output fixed: dicts with different keys.** When a list held dicts with different key sets (`{"id": 1, "error": "bad"}` next to `{"id": 2, "total": "4"}`), the typed build printed and dumped every key on every dict, so a missing key showed as `None`/`null`. Keys only some dicts have are now left out while unset, as in Python, and `k in r` and `r.get(k)` see whether the key is set.
- **`csv.writer(sys.stdout)` on the typed path.** `w = csv.writer(sys.stdout, lineterminator='\n')` (also through `out = sys.stdout` used only for the writer) with `w.writerow(row)` and `w.writerows(rows)` now builds typed, quoting fields as CPython's csv module does (a field holding the delimiter, a quote or a line break is quoted, quotes doubled; `delimiter=` and `lineterminator=` literals). Of the 48 final release-suite programs, haiku_csv_join moved to the typed path and prints what python3 prints on the task's tests.
- **`re.S`/`re.DOTALL` and `re.M`/`re.MULTILINE` on the typed path.** `re.compile(r'\{\{(.*?)\}\}', re.S)` and `re.split(p, s, flags=re.S)`, common in template renderers, sent the program to the dynamic build; the flags (and `(?s)`/`(?m)` written in the pattern) now build typed. **Silent wrong output fixed:** in the typed build `$` matched only at the very end, while Python's also matches before a final newline (`re.search(r'z$', 'z\n')`); `re.split` skipped empty matches (`re.split(r'\b', 'a b')`, Python 3.7 on splits there) and gave `''` instead of `None` for a group that took no part.
- **`io.StringIO` builds natively.** An in-memory text file (`write`, `getvalue`, `read`, `readline`, iteration, `seek`, `tell`, `truncate`, `print(..., file=buf)`, `with`), and so `csv.writer(io.StringIO())` and `csv.DictReader(io.StringIO(text))`, now take the dynamic build, also under `--sandbox` without `# allow native`. The rest of `io` (`BytesIO`, `open`) still runs in CPython. Both saved csv_join fallback programs print what python3 prints. Also: under `--sandbox` the `{"path":"dynamic"}` line for such programs named `allow native` though none was needed; it now says which modules the typed build lacks.
- **`bin`, `hex`, `oct` and `startswith`/`endswith` with a start index on the typed path.** `bin(x).count('1')` and tokenizers that test `s.startswith('<->', i)` built over dynamic values; they now build typed (negative numbers and negative starts as in Python). Of the 48 final release-suite programs, haiku_sudoku moved to the typed path and prints what python3 prints on the task's tests.
- **`json.JSONDecoder().raw_decode` and `json.decoder.scanstring` build natively.** Tokenizers that read a JSON value or string out of a longer text (`v, end = json.JSONDecoder().raw_decode(s, i)`) ran in CPython; they now take the dynamic build, with CPython's indexes and `JSONDecodeError` messages. These were the last 2 of the 48 final release-suite programs that still needed CPython (both `json_query`); both now print what python3 prints on the task's tests.
- **`enum` builds natively.** `class Color(Enum)` with plain values or `auto()`, aliases, methods, properties and an `__init__` taking tuple values, plus `Color(1)`, `Color['RED']`, iteration, `len`, `in`, `__members__`, `@unique`, and `str`/`int` mixins (`class Level(str, Enum)`), now runs in the dynamic build; it used to run in CPython. `repr`, `str`, f-strings, hashing and errors (`ValueError: 7 is not a valid Color`, `TypeError` on `<`) print what CPython prints. `IntEnum`, `StrEnum` and `Flag` still run in CPython. Also: `vars(SomeClass)` works, and a class's attributes keep the order its body defines them in.
- **Exact fractions on the typed path.** `fractions.Fraction` in a typed build held only denominators that divide lcm(1..32)*10, so every Python file importing fractions went to the dynamic build. A Fraction is now a reduced numerator over a denominator (64 bits each, worked in 128-bit arithmetic), so unit tables, matrix elimination and exact ratios such as 1/86400 build typed and print what CPython prints, including `x.numerator`, `x.denominator`, `Fraction("3/4")`, `//`, `%`, `round`, `abs` and `sum(xs, Fraction(0))`. A value that needs more than 64 bits stops with an error naming the limit rather than rounding.
- **More plain Python stays typed.** On the 813-program corpus (bench/typed_corpus.py: saved solutions and repros), 522 build typed, up from 496 on main (386 of the 580 solutions, up from 371); 37 moved onto the typed path and every one with tests prints what python3 prints, and 11 moved off it on purpose (the silent wrong outputs below). `~x`, `yield [x for ...]`, `(only,) = xs`, `d.setdefault(k, {})[j] = v`, `re.sub(pattern, repl, s)` with `repl` a function of the program, a nested function with default arguments, and calls inside a generator inside another generator (`" ".join(fmt(x) for x in r) for r in m`) now build typed. A re group that took no part in the match is None, as in CPython, so `m.group(2) is None` stays typed.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: re groups and tuples.** In typed builds `print(m.group(2))` for a group that did not match printed an empty line instead of `None`, and `print(m.groups())` showed `['a', '', 'c']` instead of `('a', None, 'c')`. A list filled with `tuple(cur)` by a helper function (`out.append(tuple(cur))` on a list passed in, or returned) printed its items as lists. All now match CPython (printing `m.groups()` and such lists goes to the dynamic build).
- **Silent wrong output fixed: rows changed in a loop.** `for r in t[2]: r[1] = v` on rows held in a tuple (a table kept as `(cols, order, rows)`) changed copies of the rows in typed builds, so the update was lost; it now changes the rows themselves, and a loop that changes each row while also reading the rows' owner (`if test(t, r): r[i] = v`) builds over dynamic values. A program whose mixed lists (`[1, 'a']`) have the same item types as one of its real tuples printed those lists as tuples and copied them where Python shares them; it now builds over dynamic values too. Regex patterns also read `\x00`, `\u00e9`, `\f`, `\v` and `\a` escapes, which the typed build matched as letters.
- **Path text from `os` and `pathlib` builds natively everywhere.** A program whose only uses of `os` and `pathlib` are path text (`os.path.join`, `basename`, `dirname`, `splitext`, `normpath`, `os.sep`, `pathlib.PurePath`) used Brevi's own copies of those functions only under `--sandbox`; outside it the program ran in CPython. It now builds natively in both. A program that also reaches files, processes or the environment (`os.environ`, `os.listdir`, `pathlib.Path`, `os.path.exists`) still runs those in CPython, as before.
- **Docs:** the site has a [Changelog](https://brevi-lang.org/docs/changelog) page built from this file, and every diagram is a sharp SVG that opens full size on click; the architecture diagram shows the current build paths.

## v0.24.3 (3 Nov 2026)

`brevi check` gives its verdict in well under a second, and checking or running an unchanged program again replays the cached result in about 10 ms: the median check of a new file went from 559 ms to 144 ms (60 of 65 programs under a second, up from 40), and a re-check from 60 ms to 8 ms median. Standard-library calls now run natively instead of in CPython (of 47 probe programs, 19 needed CPython before and 5 now), `math` and `time` are on the fast typed path (a `math.sqrt`/`math.log` loop over 20 million numbers takes 0.09 s, CPython 1.9 s), and two silent wrong outputs are fixed (`re.findall` with groups, reading a missing defaultdict key). No new release-test run; the v0.24.2 figures stand.

- **Faster `check`, and repeated calls are near-instant.** Agents call `brevi check` and `brevi run` over and over, so every call's wait counts. Three changes, measured on 65 programs (the release suite's saved repros and saved solutions; table in the pull request): (1) `check` and `run` made all eight versions of the generated program up front, each a whole parse and type-inference pass, even when the first one built or was cached; now the others are made only when the ones before fail. (2) A new result cache: `check`, `build` and `run` of files that did not change replay the last result (the same output, `{"path":...}` line and binary) without parsing, inference or rustc, keyed on a hash of the program's files, the command and flags, the `BREVI_*` and Python settings, the Brevi binary and the toolchain. A re-check of an unchanged file went from 344 ms on average (up to 3.9 s) to 11 ms, a re-run from 379 ms (up to 4.7 s) to 18 ms. A refusal is replayed only for programs that import nothing an install could change. The MCP server gets this with no change. (3) `check` of a file on the dynamic path runs rustc's checks instead of a full build (2.8 s to 0.5 s on one program). Type inference also copies its name tables less. `--no-cache` (or `BREVI_NO_CACHE=1`) builds from scratch, `BREVI_CACHE_MB` caps the cache (default 1024 MB, least recently used out first; it was 400 entries), and `BREVI_TIME=1` prints how long each build stage took. Also fixed: a build into a folder that did not exist left its error in the build cache, so building again after creating the folder still failed.
- **Standard-library calls run natively, not in CPython.** A Python file that the typed build could only finish by handing some standard-library calls to CPython (`math.gcd`, `itertools.product`, `statistics.mean`, `copy.deepcopy`, `datetime.datetime.strptime`, `functools.reduce`, `time.perf_counter`) now takes the dynamic build, which has those modules natively: no libpython at run time, and it works under `--sandbox`. Libraries Brevi lacks (numpy, sqlite3) still run in CPython as before. Of 47 small standard-library probe programs, 19 needed CPython before and 5 now (argparse, textwrap, io, os and random; random and textwrap come next); in the 765-program corpus 11 programs moved from CPython to native, and every moved program with tests prints what python3 prints. Also fixed: one missing call such as `math.gcd` sent every `math.` call on its line to CPython, `math.sqrt` included.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: `re.findall` with groups.** With two or more groups `re.findall` returns tuples (`[('a', '1'), ('b', '2')]`), but the typed build gave whole matches (`['a1', 'b2']`). Such calls now build over dynamic values; with no group or one group they stay typed.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: reading a missing defaultdict key.** In Python `d[k]` on a defaultdict puts the key in, so `len(d)`, loops and printing see it; the typed build left the dict unchanged. A program that reads a defaultdict with a key that may be missing and also looks at its keys now builds over dynamic values (a key taken from a loop over the dict's keys, or from `min`/`max` of them, counts as found, so counting and grouping programs stay typed: 1 of the 765 corpus programs moved).
- **`math` and `time` on the fast typed path.** `math.gcd`, `lcm`, `comb`, `perm`, `factorial` (big results grow past 64 bits as in Python), `isclose`, `log` (with or without a base), `log2`, `log10`, `log1p`, `exp`, `expm1`, `sin`, `cos`, `tan`, `asin`, `acos`, `atan`, `atan2`, the hyperbolic functions, `hypot`, `fabs`, `trunc`, `degrees`, `radians`, `copysign`, `fmod`, `isnan`, `isinf`, `isfinite`, the constants `pi`, `e`, `tau`, `inf` and `nan`, and `time.time`, `perf_counter` and `monotonic` now build typed, whether imported with `import math` or `from math import gcd, pi`. Domain errors are a `ValueError` an `except` catches, as in CPython, and `math.sqrt(-1)` raises instead of giving nan. A loop of `math.sqrt` and `math.log` over 20 million numbers runs in 0.09 s (CPython: 1.9 s). Of the 47 probes, `math` and `time` programs moved from the dynamic build to the typed one; no program in the corpus left the typed path.
- **Docs:** a [What runs native](https://brevi-lang.org/docs/compatibility#what-runs-native) table lists, module by module, which functions the typed build has, which run in the dynamic build, and which still need CPython.

## v0.24.2 (3 Nov 2026)

Every build off the fast typed path now says which path it took and which function to rewrite, in one JSON line an agent can act on. More plain Python stays on the typed path: of 765 agent-written programs, 477 build typed, up from 429. `random` and path functions work in the sandbox, Python files can use `# allow read` as a comment, fallback errors are shorter (`--traceback` for the whole one), and printing `tuple(xs)` no longer shows a list. Release test on this build (one run): 1.09x Python's cost for Sonnet and 1.06x for Haiku, every task passed, 23 of 24 final programs native for each model.

- **Every build says which path it took.** For a Python file, `brevi run`, `build` and `check` print one JSON line on stderr whenever the file is off the typed fast path: `{"path":"dynamic","line":3,"fn":"show","why":"print() of a set shows CPython's hash order, ..."}`, `{"path":"pybridge","why":"numpy run in CPython","py":["numpy"]}` or `{"path":"cpython","line":2,"fn":"g","why":"Brevi could not build it: unknown function eval()","by":"python3"}`. `line`, `fn` (and `file` in a module) name the code to rewrite to get the typed path back. The typed path stays silent; `--explain` prints it too and adds the fix, up to five more reasons and why the dynamic build refused a CPython run; `BREVI_PATH=1` prints the line for every build, `BREVI_PATH=0` never. The CPython fallback's `note: ran with python3; ...` line is replaced by its `{"path":"cpython"}` line. bench/e2e.py logs each pyb task's final path and adds a `typ` column (tasks on the typed path).
- **More plain Python stays on the typed fast path.** On a corpus of 765 agent-written programs (the saved benchmark solutions and past fallback repros), 429 built typed before and 477 now (372 of the 580 solutions, up from 326), none moved off the typed path, and every moved program with tests prints what python3 prints on them. Loops, comprehensions and prints of a set of strings (or of tuples led by one) build typed: CPython draws string hashes afresh each run, so no order is CPython's own (rustc checks the element type; sets of numbers still go to the dynamic build, which follows CPython's order). A comprehension over a set handed straight to `max`, `min`, `sorted`, `any`, `all`, `len` or `set` no longer counts as an order-dependent use. A sentinel `MISSING = object()` used only to mark "no value" (`d.get(k, MISSING)`, `if old is MISSING:`) is read as None when the program cannot tell the two apart (no other None value reaches it, no truth test on it). Also typed now: `[[int(x) for x in row] for row in rows]` and other comprehensions inside comprehensions that can fail, `d.get(k, {})` and `d.get(k, [])` on JSON objects, `t[:2]` and `t[1:]` on tuples, a lambda set inside a loop or if body, and `datetime.date(...)` in a function with a local named `date`.
- **Silent wrong output fixed: `print(tuple(xs))`.** A tuple made with `tuple(...)` from a list or iterator is held as a list by the typed build, so printing it (or a list of them) showed `[1, 2]` instead of `(1, 2)`. Such programs now build over dynamic values.
- **random, path text and `# allow` work in the sandbox.** Under `--sandbox` and in the MCP tools (sandboxed by default), `import random; random.seed(1)` gave two confusing errors (`unknown method .seed()` and `unknown name 'random'`). random now runs natively there from Brevi's own copy of CPython's generator, so a seeded program draws the same numbers as CPython (`seed`, `random`, `randint`, `randrange`, `choice`, `choices`, `shuffle`, `sample`, `uniform`, `gauss`, `getrandbits`, `Random`); any other random function gives one error naming it and saying it needs `allow native`. Path text no longer needs `allow native` either: `os.path.join`, `basename`, `dirname`, `split`, `splitext`, `normpath`, `isabs`, `os.sep` and `pathlib.PurePath` (POSIX rules) run without CPython, while every call that reaches files, processes or the environment (`os.listdir`, `os.environ`, `os.system`, `pathlib.Path`, `os.path.exists`, `os` passed as a value) is still refused, now by name. New tests in tests/sandbox show each of those stays blocked, and that Brevi's own PurePath has no file access at all. In Python files the allow line can be a comment, `# allow read`, so the file stays valid Python (the bare `allow read` still works and stays the form for `.bv` files), and fixes suggest the comment form.
- **Shorter fallback output.** When `brevi run` falls back to CPython on a sure mistake, it printed the note and then CPython's whole traceback, so the error was read twice. It now prints the note and only the traceback's last line, with where it was raised (`NameError: name 'totl' is not defined (main.py line 3)`). `--traceback` or `BREVI_TRACEBACK=1` shows the whole traceback; other fallbacks are unchanged.

## v0.24.1 (3 Nov 2026)

A hardening release before the public preview. `brevi run --sandbox` can no longer be bypassed, and the MCP server survives programs that never end or flood their output. Plain Python comes first everywhere: the MCP server builds code as `main.py`, and `brevi check`, `build` and `run` report sure Python mistakes (an undefined name, a method no type has) instead of building them. Dataclasses and string escapes print as in CPython, programs killed by a signal say so, and the npm wrapper is now named `brevi`, as on crates.io and PyPI. The README and docs were rewritten for first-time visitors, and brevi-lang.org has a documentation section.

- **npm wrapper renamed to `brevi`.** The npm package is now `brevi` (`npx brevi mcp`, `npm install -g brevi`), the same name as on crates.io and PyPI; it was `brevi-lang`. Nothing is published yet.
- **Newcomer pass before the public preview.** README rewritten for a first-time visitor: install, a quick start in plain Python, agents, how it works and the v0.24 status, with the v0.8 to v0.23 result tables moved to bench/HISTORY.md. The site and docs now describe the v0.24 behaviour: sure mistakes in Python files (unknown names and methods, text plus a number) are reported by check and build and named in run's fallback note, a new small program builds in well under a second after a one-time runtime build, and the release-test figures match the v0.24.0 release notes. The skill allows installed packages, release notes give the Windows install step, and examples/top.py is a first Python example.
- **Sandbox fixes (security).** `brevi run --sandbox x.py` could be bypassed in three ways. A program refused for an undeclared `read()` was then run with python3, which read the file anyway (the inner build was also started without `--sandbox`). Python libraries that CPython runs inside the program (`import os`, `import subprocess`, and even `sys.modules["os"]` through Brevi's own `sys`) were not checked at all. And files that go to the dynamic build skipped the check. Now the capability check runs first and its refusal is final on every path. Python libraries need `allow native` (a Python value reaches everything CPython can, so there is no safe subset), and a program held to its allow lines (`--sandbox` or an `allow` line) never falls back to python3. Dynamic builds have no file, network or library access, so a program that passes the check may still build that way; a unit test keeps it so.
- **Dataclasses print as in Python.** In typed builds `print(P(1))` for `@dataclass class P: x: int; y: int = 0` printed `P_D { x: 1, y: 0 }` instead of `P(x=1, y=0)`, also inside lists, dicts and tuples. Dataclasses now print CPython's generated repr (fields in order, `field(repr=False)` left out), a class with `__str__` still shows its `__repr__` (or the dataclass one) inside containers and for `repr()` and `!r`, and a class with neither prints `<__main__.C object at 0x...>`. Dynamic builds also leave `field(repr=False)` fields out.
- **Python first everywhere.** The MCP server builds code as plain Python by default (kept as `main.py`, built like `brevi run main.py`); before, it kept every program as `main.bv`, so plain Python failed there (`type(it).__name__` gave `unknown function ax_type_of()`, a class method call gave `unknown method .speak()`, and `file: "main.py"` on a fresh server said "no program yet").
