Brevi code is still recognised by its `fn` items or a `.bv` file name. `patch` replaces Python `def`s and classes by name (decorators included) and adds new ones after the file's last def. The server's instructions now say to write plain Python and the plain solution, instead of reading the guide first. `brevi --help` lists `FILE.py` first.
- **Python mistakes are reported, not built.** `brevi check`, `build` and `run` on a `.py` file exited 0 on mistakes such as `print(totl(nums))` (a name defined nowhere), `x.uppr()` (a method no Python type has) or `"n=" + len(nums)`: the typed build refused them, the dynamic build accepted them, and the error only showed when that line ran. `check` and `build` now stop with the error and a hint (`name 'totl' is not defined`, `did you mean total?`); `run` runs the file with python3 and names the mistake in its note, so a mistake in a branch that never runs costs nothing. Only mistakes CPython would also stop on are reported this way: a name bound anywhere in the file, a method any built-in type or common module has, a file that catches the error itself, and lines that may never run (under a `sys.version_info`, `sys.platform` or `TYPE_CHECKING` test, an `if False:` or `x if False else y`, or an except block) still build as before (0 of 1,152 saved Python files are refused). No message names Brevi's internal helpers (`ax_...`) any more.
- **The MCP server is hardened for the public preview.** A program that never ends used to hang the whole server for good: the 20 s limit stopped `brevi`, but the program it started kept running and holding the output pipe. Every call now runs in its own process group, and a timeout, a cancel or the client leaving stops everything it started (on Linux children also die with the server). Output is read while the program runs, so a program printing more than 64 KB no longer always timed out; long output is cut in the middle (first 16 KB and last 4 KB kept). The JSON reader is now strict and complete: emoji and other characters sent as `\ud83d\ude00` pairs (Python's default `json.dumps`) were dropped, deeply nested input crashed the server, and a line that was not UTF-8 ended it. Batches, non-objects and bad ids get JSON-RPC errors, an unknown tool is a -32602 error, a misspelt or mistyped argument (`stdn`, `timeout: "abc"`) is reported to the model instead of ignored, and a bad file name (`../x.py`) no longer breaks every later call. New: `notifications/cancelled` stops the named call, ping and tools/list are answered while a call runs, protocol versions are negotiated (newest 2025-11-25), tool titles and read-only hints, `timeout` on `run` and `test`, `brevi mcp --timeout S --mem-mb MB` (Linux: a program gets at most half the machine's memory by default), `run` with `cases` falls back to CPython like `run` does (sandbox off), and the work folder and its build folders are removed when the client disconnects (folders left by crashed servers are removed at start). The first call after an install or upgrade timed out while Brevi built its runtime libraries (about 20 s), and kept timing out, since each try was stopped before the build finished; the server now builds them in the background as soon as it starts. When the client's PATH leaves out `~/.cargo/bin` (Claude Desktop on macOS), the server finds rustc there; when rustc is missing the reply says how to install it. Tested with the official MCP Python SDK and with Claude Code, and tests/mcp_test.py covers each case.
- **Python string escapes in native builds.** `"\x41"`, `"\u00e9"`, `"\U0001F600"`, octal `"\101"` and `\a \b \f \v` printed their backslash text instead of the character, and `repr()` of a text with control characters showed them raw instead of as `\x07`; both now match CPython.
- **Killed programs say so.** A program stopped by a signal (out of memory, a crash) exited `brevi run` with 1 and no message; it now prints one JSON line (`{"e":"signal",...}`) on stderr and exits 128 + the signal. `BREVI_MEM_MB` limits a program's memory on Linux.
- **Docs and new websites.** brevi-lang.org has a documentation section (`/docs/`): install, using Brevi (commands, flags, errors, CPython fallback, sandbox, GUI test mode), for AI agents (MCP, skill, plugin, llms.txt, the plain-solution advice), architecture with its diagrams, what works and limits, benchmarks, the Brevi language guide, FAQ, roadmap and contributing. Every page is also served as Markdown (`/docs/NAME.md`). Both sites share one stylesheet, work on phones, and switch their GitHub links and install command on once the repository is public. `site/gen.py` builds the pages from `site/docs/*.md` and the repo's own docs; `bash site/deploy.sh --preview` deploys to workers.dev for review.

## v0.24.0 (3 Nov 2026)

Agents now pay less with Brevi than with Python on heavy work. Told that their Python is compiled to native code, models write the plain solution instead of hand-optimising it; on four tasks with a 10 s limit the whole job cost 0.58x Python's with Sonnet and 0.53x with Haiku, every program built natively and no check failed because of Brevi. On the 24-task release suite, ordinary work, the bar is still met (1.07x Sonnet, 1.00x Haiku). Plain generators and string indexing now build typed and fast, first builds take about 35% less time, and seven bugs found by the new shadow check (each pyb check also runs CPython) are fixed.

- **Write the plain version.** The skill, the editor rules, the MCP server's text and llms.txt now tell agents that their Python is compiled to native code, which runs plain loops 10 to 50 times faster than CPython, so they should write the plain, direct solution and not hand-optimise it. In a test on the four `speed` tasks, this one line cut output tokens by 50% for Sonnet and 28% for Haiku, and cost by 44% and 25%. Every plain program then ran natively within the 10 s limit, where CPython needs 29 to 59 s. bench/e2e.py adds the line to the pyb prompt (`--no-hint` leaves it out).
- **Plain idioms build typed and fast.** An endless generator read only with `next()` (a seeded random number source: `def rng(seed): x = seed; while True: ...; yield x`) used to send the whole program to the dynamic build. It is now rewritten into a small class, so the program builds typed. The speed set's plain reference programs go from 24 to 29 s to 1.8 to 2.5 s for knapsack, and from over 60 s to 2.3 s for Game of Life (CPython: 25 s and 30 s). Indexing a long text (`a[i]`, `b[j - 1]`) scanned the whole text each time to check for ASCII; the answer is now kept until the text is freed, so a 10,000-letter edit distance takes 1.8 s instead of 15 s.
- **Faster cold builds.** A build without `--release` (what `brevi run` uses) of a program it has not seen takes about 35% less time: the 12 speed programs in `bench/compare` went from 405 ms to 257 ms each on average on a 4-core machine (a hello world from 186 ms to 148 ms), and they run as fast as before (all 12 still beat CPython; all 591 saved solutions still match it). Brevi now calls rustc directly instead of through the rustup proxy (the proxy's choice of toolchain is remembered until PATH, rustup's settings or a rust-toolchain file change), uses one codegen unit per core (4 to 16) instead of rustc's 16, looks up rustc while the front end works, and no longer compiles a program twice when the build cache is off (`BREVI_NO_CACHE=1`, which `bench/compare` uses, so its build times there were doubled).
- **Speed and correctness on fast-Python idioms** (found by the new time-limited bench set, where models write optimised Python first). Typed builds: a row taken from a grid (`nrow = new[r]`) and changed cell by cell was copied back into the grid after every cell, so a 600x600 Game of Life took 16 s; it is now written back once after the loop when nothing in the loop reads the grid (2.6 s; CPython 30 s). Small runtime helpers (indexing, `%`, `//`, `+`) are inlined into default builds. `min(a, b, c)` and `max` of three or more values, and built-ins passed to `map` (`map(sum, rows)`, `map(abs, xs)`), build typed instead of falling to the slower dynamic build. Programs that pack many fields into one int (`2 ** (F * K)`, `x << (F * w)`) build over dynamic values instead of overflowing 64-bit ints. Dynamic builds: `&`, `|` and `^` on ints past 128 bits work (they raised NotImplementedError); `bin`, `oct`, `hex` and `int(s, 2)` / `int(s, 16)` on big ints are linear (a 360,000-bit `bin()` took 11 s); `2 ** n` is a shift (a bit-packed knapsack went from 20 s to 3 s). Big-int arithmetic borrows its operands instead of copying them (another bit-packed knapsack: 10.5 s to 7.1 s; CPython 6.2 s).
- **Silent wrong answers fixed.** `out.extend(xs)` or `out += xs` on a list parameter did not change the caller's list (found by the e2e shadow check in a Haiku poker solution, where it cost 7 checks and the task). A list parameter changed and also returned (`xs.append(x); return xs`) was not changed for the caller. In dynamic builds, `x >> n` with n over about a million bits shifted by a million instead. From the first release-suite runs with the shadow check (19 checks that failed only because of Brevi): a function giving `None` on one path and a list or dict on another (`parse(tokens)` returning None for bad input) read an empty list as None, so empty input was reported as invalid (such results are now dynamic values; the "may be None or empty" build error is gone). `isinstance(r, tuple)` was false for a tuple held as a dynamic value. `all(c in digits for c in s)` and `any(...)` over a text held as a dynamic value walked nothing, and `[c for c in s]` over a text gave the text instead of its characters. A list slot set by a nested function, in a list that held an int and None (`found = [0, None]`), was set on a copy (programs like this now build over dynamic values). Programs using `fractions` build over dynamic values, where a Fraction takes any denominator (unit tables stopped with "can't be held exactly"). Dynamic builds add `datetime.time` (`dt.time()`, `time(9, 30)`, `datetime.combine(d, t)`) and `Decimal.scaleb`.
- **Bench.** e2e.py's `pyb` check also runs CPython on the same files and logs the pair per check (`shadow`); the summary's `bug` column counts checks that failed only because of Brevi. New set `speed` (bench/ref/gen_speed.py): Game of Life, knapsack, edit distance and Collatz chains, small inputs and tens of millions of steps under the 10 s test limit. bench/replay.py replays saved conversations from their first failed check with different feedback (shorter diff feedback made retries 14% cheaper, about 2% of a whole run).

## v0.23.0 (3 Nov 2026)

Closes the last big-int gaps from v0.22.0: `//` and `%` now work on big integers, and growing numbers shared between functions (closures, `nonlocal`, `global`) grow as in Python. bench/compare still matches CPython on all 591 saved agent programs, 13 of 13 traps and 18 of 18 large programs. `brevi build x.py --standalone` now puts CPython inside the binary, so a program that uses Python libraries runs on a machine without Python. A new architecture doc explains how a file becomes a native program.

- **Standalone builds of programs that use Python libraries.** `brevi build x.py --standalone` puts CPython inside the binary: libpython, the shared libraries it and the extension modules need (the C library aside), the standard library, and the installed packages the program imports with everything they require. The binary runs on a Linux machine with no Python installed. It unpacks once into `~/.cache/brevi/py` (or `$BREVI_PY_CACHE`), about 1 to 3 seconds on first run, then starts as fast as before. Results stay CPython's own: numpy, pandas, sqlite3/hashlib/zlib, BeautifulSoup with lxml, Pillow, Jinja2 and openpyxl programs, plus one reading argv and stdin, print exactly what python3 prints when run in an empty root holding only the C library. Sizes: about 13 MB with only the standard library, 31 MB with numpy, 42 MB with pandas. Linux only for now.
- `from PIL import Image` (a from-import of a submodule the package does not import itself) failed with "module 'PIL' has no attribute 'Image'" in every build that calls Python libraries; it now imports the submodule, as Python does.
- **The last big-int gaps closed.** `//` and `%` work on big integers with Python's floor and sign rules. Examples: `fact(50) % MOD`, a digit-sum loop `s += x % 10; x //= 10` over a 40-digit number, and `-h // 7`. Top-level int constants such as `MOD = 1000000007` join big arithmetic. A growing number shared between functions builds over dynamic values, where it grows as in Python. This covers a closure reading it, `nonlocal acc; acc *= v`, `global total; total = total * 7 + k`, and a top-level hash that a function prints. Before, these stopped with an overflow error.
- **Architecture doc.** [docs/ARCHITECTURE.md](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md) explains, with diagrams, what Brevi does between a `.bv` or `.py` file and a native program: front end, typed build, the guard that sends Python to the dynamic build, `src/dynpy`, library calls through pybridge, rustc and the build cache, and the CPython fallback.

## v0.22.0 (3 Nov 2026)

A bug hunt and a head-to-head with Codon and Nuitka. Four rounds of bug hunting fixed the silent wrong answers they turned up (slices with negative steps, list aliasing on the first build attempt, catchable errors, `copy.copy`, and more), and the comparison found six more bugs, which are also fixed. On the same unchanged Python files, Brevi builds all 591 saved agent programs and all 18 large programs, and every one prints exactly what CPython prints; Codon builds 206 of the 591 and gets 36 of those wrong. Builds are faster by default: `brevi run` and `brevi build` without `--release` beat CPython on all 12 speed programs (1.7x to 40x). With `--release`, Brevi is level with Codon where Codon builds, and it also runs the object-heavy programs Codon refuses. Sets and deques in typed builds now give constant-time lookups and `popleft()`. Ints that grow past 64 bits, including values returned from functions (factorials, Fibonacci, recursion), become big ints as in Python. A growing value captured by a closure or `nonlocal` still stops with an overflow error.

- **Bug hunt, part 1: silent wrong answers fixed** (each printed something other than CPython, with no error). Typed build: negative-step slices (`xs[::-2]`, `xs[-1:-3:-1]`, `s[4:0:-1]` gave the wrong items or none; any `xs[a:b:k]` now picks as Python does, also on text); `zs = xs` followed by `xs.sort()`, `xs.extend(...)`, `xs += [...]` or `del xs[a:b]` left zs unchanged (now built over dynamic values, where both names share the list); format specs with a fill character or the `b`, `o`, `g`, `e`, `#`, `_`, `=` and nested `{x:{w}.2f}` forms (`'{:-^5}'.format('a')` printed `a`); f-strings `{x!r}`, `{x=}`, `{ x }` and `{1e16:g}`; a plain (not f-) string such as `"{x}"` was filled in from a variable named x; a list mixing ints and floats printed `1000.0` as `1000`. Python files that print or walk a set (the typed build keeps a set in sorted order, CPython walks its hash table: `{-1, 5}` prints `{5, -1}`, and a set inside a dict printed as a list), or that change a value inside the print call showing it (`print(q, q.popleft())`), are now built over dynamic values. Dynamic build: `raise ValueError("msg")` (any built-in exception) lost its message and args; `str.center` padded odd widths on the wrong side; a set literal of 3 or more constants printed in a different order than CPython. 11 new parity probes cover these.
- **Bug hunt, part 2.** Silent wrong answers: the checks that make Python names share a list (`zs = xs; xs.sort(); print(zs)`) were skipped on the first build attempt of every Python file, so most such programs printed an unchanged copy (they now build over dynamic values, where both names share the list); a change through one name followed by setting it anew (`xs.sort(); xs = [...]; print(zs)`) was also missed. `'-'.join("xyz")` printed `-` (now `x-y-z`). `-0.0 % 5.0` printed `-0.0` (Python's zero remainder takes the divisor's sign), and float or JSON-number `%` and `//` by zero gave nan instead of ZeroDivisionError. In a Python file, an unknown method was silently renamed to a near one (`s.rpartition` ran as `s.partition`); Python files are never auto-fixed now. Printing a `re` match showed its groups (`['12']`, `[]`) instead of `<re.Match object; ...>` or None (such files now build over dynamic values). Speed: in dynamic builds every `s.add(x)`, `len(s)` and `s |= t` copied the whole set, so a 40,000-item BFS took 34 seconds; it now takes 0.09 s, as CPython does, and `-=`, `&=` and issuperset no longer slow down with the set's size.
- **Bug hunt, part 3** (exceptions, input, numbers). Python files with `except ZeroDivisionError`, `IndexError`, `KeyError`, `LookupError`, `ArithmeticError`, `AssertionError`, `EOFError`, `OverflowError` or `RecursionError` build over dynamic values; the typed build stopped at those errors instead of letting the except catch them. Silent wrong answers fixed: a dict or set changed in a loop over it went on (CPython raises RuntimeError; such files build over dynamic values); `2.0 ** 5000` printed inf (OverflowError), `0.0 ** -1` and `(-8) ** 0.5` printed inf/nan (ZeroDivisionError, and a refusal since Python gives a complex number); a huge int power wrapped its exponent. `input()` at the end of the input raises EOFError (it returned ""). `int("1_000")` and `float("1_0.5")` read as in Python. `copy.copy(rows)` shares the inner lists, as in Python (such files build over dynamic values; the typed build copied them). Dynamic builds: unbounded recursion raises RecursionError (it crashed with a stack overflow), and `sys.setrecursionlimit` still allows deep recursion.
- **Fast sets in typed builds.** Typed builds kept a Python set as a plain list, so every `x in s` scanned it: a 40,000-node BFS took several seconds and a 400x400 grid BFS with a walls set ran for over two minutes (CPython: 0.03 s and 0.4 s). A list or set that is searched with `in` and otherwise only added to, popped, counted, sorted or walked (a function's own `seen`, or a parameter used that way) is now kept with a hash index, so `in` takes constant time; the items stay in the same order, so output does not change. The grid BFS now takes 0.8 s and the 40,000-node BFS 0.2 s (what remains is `deque.popleft()`, still a list shift in typed builds). Set `&` and `-` sort one side once instead of scanning it for every item (15,000-item sets: 2.8 s to under 0.1 s), and `while q:` no longer copies the whole list on every test. tests/typed checks these against CPython with a time limit.
- **Fast deques and object sets in typed builds.** A `deque` (or a list taken from the front with `pop(0)`) that is otherwise only added to, popped, read by index or walked is kept as a ring buffer, so `popleft()` takes constant time instead of moving every item (40,000-node BFS: 0.20 s to 0.13 s; the 400x400 grid BFS takes 0.21 s with `--release`, CPython 0.43 s). Sets stored on objects (`self.seen = set()`, then `x in self.seen`) get the same hash index as local sets when every use of that field is a set use.
- **Big ints returned from functions.** A function whose result outgrows 64 bits returns a big integer, as in Python. This covers a loop that multiplies (`f *= i ... return f`), Fibonacci and Tribonacci sums, and recursion such as `return n * fact(n - 1)` or `fib(n - 1) + fib(n - 2)`. These calls stopped with an overflow error before. Where such a call meets other big values or is printed, it stays big. Where an int is needed (an index, a count, `//` or `%`), it becomes an int, and raises OverflowError only if it does not fit. A function's locals no longer have to stay small ints just because another function uses the same name.
- **Fast by default, and the Codon/Nuitka gaps closed.** `brevi run` and `brevi build` without `--release` now compile the program at opt-level 1 against a fully optimised runtime, so the default build beats CPython on all 12 speed programs in `bench/compare` (it was slower on 5): trees 9.8 s to 0.71 s (CPython 5.2 s), strings 5.6 s to 1.13 s (2.4 s), records 2.5 s to 0.38 s (0.71 s), nbody 1.4 s to 0.12 s; a cold build takes about 0.3 s longer. Typed programs get a small-object allocator (per-thread free lists for blocks up to 256 bytes, as CPython's pymalloc does), about 25% faster on string and dict work, plus quicker one-character `split` and `{i:06d}` formatting. List slice assignment (`xs[a:b] = ys`, `perm[:k+1] = perm[k::-1]`) builds typed, so fannkuch runs at 3.5x CPython instead of 0.4x. Ints that grow past 64 bits in Python (a factorial `f *= i`, a rolling hash `h = h * 31 + ord(c)`, Fibonacci `a, b = b, a + b`, big literals) become big ints, `3 ** 200 % m` is worked out exactly, and a literal power past 64 bits builds over dynamic values; these stopped with an overflow error before (a value returned from a function still does). Generators work in dynamic builds and run lazily, step by step with their caller as in CPython (endless generators, `send()`, `yield from` with a return value, `StopIteration.value`, generators in classes and closures); the typed build, which runs a generator to the end at its first use, keeps only generators where that cannot show (no prints, no reads of outside state, no while loops), and a yield inside try/finally or with is refused at build time. `csv.DictReader` is native (rows are dicts of str, so `d[r["dept"]]` no longer guesses int keys), `__file__` is the file's absolute path, and the `sys.path.insert(0, os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__)))` line needs no CPython. `csv.reader(f, delimiter=";")` is no longer read with commas. The chess huge-set program with generators and both `__file__` programs now build natively and match CPython.

- **Brevi vs Codon vs Nuitka** (`bench/compare/`): the same unchanged Python files built by Brevi, Codon 0.20.6 and Nuitka 4.3.4, checked against CPython's exact output and exit status, with build and run times. Of 591 saved agent solutions, Brevi builds 589 and matches CPython on 588, Nuitka 591 and 591, Codon 206 and 170 (16 of its builds print a different answer with no error). On typed code Brevi `--release` runs 2.7x to 47x faster than CPython on 9 of 12 speed programs, level with Codon; Nuitka stays near CPython. It found four silent wrong answers in Brevi, now fixed: `book = asks if buy else bids` followed by `book.remove(o)` changed a copy (such files now build over dynamic values); `m.group(3) is None` was False for a group that did not match (likewise); an f-string value starting with a number (`f"{1/3:.3f}"`, `f"{2*n}"`) printed as literal text; `raise SystemExit(3)` exited with 0 (dynamic build) or 1 (typed build) instead of 3. Also fixed two wrong refusals: `d.setdefault(k, set()).add(x)` stopped with KeyError, and `10 ** -2` stopped with an error instead of giving 0.01.

## v0.21.0 (2 Nov 2026)

Brevi passes its release test: over four runs each with Sonnet and Haiku (e31 to e34, each side's prompt cache kept apart), every task passed, plain Python built by Brevi cost 1.10x CPython with Sonnet (right at the 10% bar) and 0.95x with Haiku, and all 48 final programs built natively. Python files the typed build cannot handle now build natively over dynamic values instead of falling back to python3, and Python libraries (numpy, pandas, sqlite3 and any other installed module) run inside native builds through CPython loaded at run time.

- All 18 saved solutions of the huge set (chess, adventure and an interpreter, 200 to 750 lines over several files) now build natively and match CPython.
- Note: the dynamic build path compiles its runtime once into `~/.cache/brevi/dynrt`, so the first such build on a new machine takes about 30 seconds longer.
- **Release test passed** (runs e31 to e34, 2 Nov 2026): over four runs each with Sonnet and Haiku, every task passed (48 of 48), plain Python built by Brevi cost 1.10x CPython with Sonnet and 0.95x with Haiku (the bar is within 10%), and 48 of 48 final programs built natively (the bar is 90%).
- Python files that the typed build cannot handle now build natively over dynamic values instead of falling back to python3. Values whose type changes at run time (a list mixing numbers, text and None; an interpreter's tuple AST; JSON rows) work as in CPython, with CPython's printing, ordering (sets, dicts), int/float/big-int arithmetic, hashing, exceptions and tracebacks. `re`, `json`, `decimal`, `fractions`, `datetime`, `csv`, `statistics`, `html`, `collections`, `itertools`, `functools`, `heapq`, `bisect`, `dataclasses`, `copy`, `math`, `operator`, `string` and `threading` (the stack-size trick) work in these builds; anything else (`exec`, `open`, `os`, a module function Brevi lacks) is refused when the program is built, so the CPython fallback still runs it. Forcing every one of the 580 saved Python solutions through this path (`BREVI_DYN=1`), all 580 print exactly what CPython prints on every test. In the normal build (typed first), all 8 release-test fallbacks from e27 to e30 and all 18 saved huge-set solutions (chess, adventure, minilang) now build natively and match CPython on every test. Dynamic builds are faster than CPython but slower than typed builds (1.3x CPython's speed on a dict and recursion micro benchmark, 4.5x on the minilang tests counting process start), so the typed build is always tried first.
- Python: a state list mixing a list with plain values (`[board, 'w', 'KQkq', -1]`) is a record; the big-program chess solution that failed now passes (all 6 chess solutions build). `pat == (4, 1)` compares a list made by `tuple(...)` with a tuple literal. A mixed list stored in a list of dynamic values prints with `[ ]`, not `( )` (it printed as a tuple before).

- From the e27/e28 trials (every task passing, 19 of 24 native): a bound method passed as a callback (`orders.fill(sku, stock.take)`, then `take(sku, n)` inside); a parameter named like a module (`def fill(self, sku, stock)`, then `stock.take(...)`) is the parameter, not the module; a loop over `sorted(m.get(k, []), ...)` that changes its items no longer raises KeyError for a missing k; `min(d)` / `max(d)` on a dict give a key; a name set to tuples of different lengths in separate places (`c = (t, a, b)`, later `c = (n, path)`) is a new name each time when each setting is only read after it; `al = run.get(k)` takes the entry's type; `sc = g.scores[name]` then `sc[q] = v` types the field's inner dicts. All 5 e27/e28 fallbacks now build natively and pass every test.
- Python libraries in Python files: `import numpy as np`, `pandas`, `sqlite3`, `subprocess`, `pathlib`, `urllib.parse`, `hashlib` and any other installed module now run inside the native binary, through CPython loaded at run time (libpython). Their values, methods, operators (including `a @ b`), iteration, comprehensions and exceptions (`except sqlite3.OperationalError`) behave exactly as in Python. Brevi's own modules stay native; what they lack (`math.gcd`, `d.strftime(...)` on a date) is taken from CPython, learned from the build's errors. A list passed to a library sees its changes (`random.shuffle(xs)`), and lambdas work as callbacks. Before this, any such import made `brevi run` fall back to python3. 12 of 12 realistic library programs now build and match CPython (they used to fall back), and 25 of 32 short library snippets do. The other 7 are older native gaps, now with the roadmap.

- Dynamic values (a parameter or slot whose type could not be fixed) get the text and list methods Python code uses: slicing, `lower`, `upper`, `strip`, `split`, `startswith`, `endswith`, `replace`, `find`, `isdigit`, `isascii`, `sorted(..., key=)`, `reversed`, `sep.join(xs)` and more (e25/e26 fallbacks). Fixed silent wrong output: `s[i]` on dynamic text gave None (so a time check like `s[2] != ":"` was always true); it now gives the character, and an index past the end of dynamic text or a list raises IndexError.
- From the e24 trial: a function kept under another name at the top level (`_old_end_call = end_tx`, called inside a function defined above it); int arithmetic and comparisons with a value read from a mixed dict (`self.total(n) * 100 // self.max`, checked to be an int when it runs); a dict record whose list key only `setdefault` makes (`cur.setdefault("msgs", []).append(m)`, then `r.get("msgs", [])`). Before, that last one would have printed the messages as missing. Sonnet's books, quiz and poker attempts from e24 now build natively and pass every test.
- Huge set: `_, frm, d, to = c` with c a dynamic tuple gives dynamic values (they were unknown, so a field filled from them was never typed); a dict keyed by tuples with a dynamic slot is read, tested and set with plain keys (`self.exits.get((self.loc, d))`, also through a key kept in a name); a dynamic value appended to a list of str is checked to be a str; a dict keyed by dynamic values is read with a plain key. Haiku's h1 adventure (the saved solution) builds and matches CPython on all 9 tests; 12 of the 18 saved huge solutions now build.
- Fixed wrong output: `Fraction(1, 3) + Fraction(1, 6)` printed 0.499999999 (a Fraction was a 9-place decimal). A Fraction is now exact for every denominator that divides lcm(1..32)*10 (thirds, sevenths, 1/64, 1/1000 ...), prints as `1/2` (`Fraction(1, 2)` inside a list), floors with `//` as Python does, and stops with an error naming the value rather than round one it cannot hold (`1/41`).
- `@lru_cache` / `@cache` (also `functools.`) on a function memoises it, as in Python; before, the decorator was dropped, so a memoised recursion such as `fib(80)` ran in exponential time. Methods and `*args` functions still drop it.
- `json.dumps` of a literal with lists or dicts of mixed values (`json.dumps({'a': [1, 2, {'b': None}], 'c': True}, indent=2)`) builds; it was refused.
- From the e25/e26 trials: a name inside a comprehension no longer takes the type of the same name in a later loop (`[pt(x) for x in tk[3::2]]` next to `for i, (nm, x) in enumerate(st)` made pt's parameter dynamic); a list or dict literal with a None or empty slot (`heap = [(start, 0, (), frm, None)]`, `{(frm, t, None): []}`) takes that slot's type from what is put in later; a name set from a function that returns a dict record (`cur = new()`) gets the keys set on it later (`cur.setdefault("out", []).append(m)`). a dict record kept in a one-slot holder or under a key (`cur = [None]`, `cur[0] = {...}`; `state['cur'] = {'bad': True}` next to the full one) is one record, also through `tx = cur[0]` and `t = state['cur']`; a row pushed as `[name, {}]` is a fixed-slot row (a tuple), and `rows[i][1][q] = v` types its dict. With the dynamic-value methods from #111, all 8 e25/e26 fallbacks (trains, books, quiz, poker) now build natively and pass every test.
- Fixed a refusal added in #103: `b, turn, rights = state` followed by `b = list(b)` and changes to b was refused as a copy that cannot be written back (Sonnet's h2 chess); b is a new list there, so nothing needs writing back.

## v0.20.0 (2 Nov 2026)

Plain Python now always runs: `brevi run x.py` builds a native binary when it can and otherwise runs the file with python3, with a one-line note naming what stopped the build. Python objects, rows and records are shared by reference as in Python, values without one fixed type become dynamic values, error classes are real classes, files can be read and written with `open()`, and tkinter programs build (headless for tests, or a real window with `--window`). The parity probe of short Python programs shows no silent differences from CPython. On the release test, measured the fair way (runs e25 and e26, right after this release with #104 added, each side's prompt cache kept apart; every task passing), plain Python built by Brevi costs 0.99x CPython with Sonnet and 0.98x with Haiku. 16 of 24 final programs built natively (67%; it varies by run, 39 of 48 in e23 and e24; the bar is 90%), so the release test is not passed yet. Earlier runs (e15 to e24) let CPython reuse the prompt cache Brevi's runs had paid to write, which counted against Brevi (those runs showed 1.04x to 1.08x). brevi-lang.org leads with AI agents again: the fast, safe target for AI-written Python.

- brevi-lang.org, llms.txt and the README put AI agents first again: "The fast, safe target for AI-written Python." The agent writes plain Python; Brevi checks it, answers mistakes in one line and builds a native binary.
- Decimal `a // b` and `a % b` (also with an int) in Python files, with Python's Decimal signs (toward zero; `%` follows a). Fraction uses the same rule, which differs from Python for negative values.
- Benchmark: each e2e run tags its prompt, so `pyb` and `python` no longer share a prompt cache. Until now `pyb` ran first and paid the cache writes that `python` then read.
- From the e22/e23 trials: a name set to a list in one loop and to a tuple later (`c = [...]`, then `c = (a, k, l)`) is a new name each time, as in Python (Sonnet's trains); a method's arguments are typed where the call is (`name = t[0]` then `g.has(name)`, before a later `for name in g.names`), and calls on a value known to be a class count (Sonnet's quiz); `a, b = cur` fills the tuple's slots from how a and b are used later (Sonnet's poker); a `def` inside an `if` at the top level; `sc = {}`, `self.students[name] = sc`, then `sc[q] = v` in a loop (the storing is moved after the loop when nothing reads the dict in between). 8 of the 10 programs that fell back to CPython in e22 and e23 now build natively and pass every test.
- Fixed wrong output: `players, dealt = cur` followed by `players[k] = v` did not change `cur` (now written back, as with `p = cur[0]`); `cur is None` was true after `cur = None` and later `cur = ({}, set())`, since Brevi keeps None in a list or dict name as an empty one (such a name now carries a flag; where it cannot, as for a parameter tested again after `acc = []`, the program is refused); a list stored in a dict and then changed by a `for` loop whose body is one call (`for c in cs: row.append(c)`) lost the change (now refused).
- Huge set (minilang): a dict of functions set once and only called by key (`CMP = {'<': operator.lt, ...}`, `CMP[op](a, b)`, or lambdas) picks the function in place, and a missing key raises KeyError; `operator.lt`, `operator.add` and the like as values; a parameter that defaults to `print` and is never passed (`def __init__(self, funcs, out=print)` with `self.out(text)`) prints. A name inside a generator (`fmt(self.ev(e, loc)) for e in s[2]`) no longer takes the type of an `except RErr as e` elsewhere in the function (Sonnet's minilang).
- From the e22 trial (raising the share of programs Brevi builds natively): a list started as `[]` is never taken for a dict (`counters[i] = 0` after `counters.append(0)`, Haiku's toc); a sort key that can fail (`xs.sort(key=wline)` with `int(...)` inside) stops the run on a bad value; `set(d)` of a dict is the set of its keys (Haiku's trains); a list or dict read at a dynamic index (`CATS[key[0]]`, Haiku's poker); a method's parameter waits one round longer for its calls before it is taken as a dynamic value. A loop over a method's list of objects that is the last statement of an if branch (`else: for bo in orders.waiting(sku): bo.remaining -= got`) no longer gets a copy refusal: the objects are shared (Haiku's warehouse). Huge set: dicts with different keys pushed on one list (`stack.append({'kind': 'if', ...})`, `{'kind': 'while', ...}`) are one record; tuples of different lengths in one list are dynamic values; `_, frm, d, to, key = c` unpacks a dynamic tuple of 4 to 6 items (a wrong count stops the run); `dict(v)` copies a dynamic dict; a dict literal mixing a dynamic value with others is a dynamic dict; `w.capacity = c[1]` checks the value is an int.
- Python: `re.compile(...).match(v)` and `findall` work when `v` holds values of mixed kinds (a str checked with `isinstance`).
- `print(..., file=sys.stderr)` (with `sep=` and `end=`), `sys.stderr.write(s)`, `sys.stdout.write(s)` and `print(..., file=sys.stdout)` in Python files.
- More of minilang (huge set): a syntax tree grown as nested tuples (`l = ('add', l, self.term())`), token lists that mix `('int', 2)` and `('sym', '+')` (that slot holds dynamic values), `v in '+-'` and `v in ('==', '!=')` on such a value, a `while True:` loop that leaves only by `return` at the end of a function, error classes defined in another file of the program, and `x in (a, b, c, d, e, f)` with up to 8 items. A whole number past 2^53 in a dynamic value stops the run instead of losing digits.
- Python error classes with fields of their own (`class RErr(Exception): def __init__(self, msg, line=None)`) are real classes: `except RErr as e` gets the object raised, so `e.line`, `e.msg` and `e.line = n; raise` work, and `str(e)` is its message (a form Brevi cannot match stops the run). `threading.Thread(target=f, args=(..))` with `.start()` / `.join()` runs f in place; `threading.stack_size(n)` and `sys.stdout.flush()` are accepted. A method named like a built-in (`def args(self)`) works, and a dynamic value passed to an int or str parameter is checked when the call runs. Fixed: a class without constructor defaults took those of the class before it (`B(n)` was given `A`'s default `line=None`).
- Fixed wrong output: a Python `try` with several `except` clauses ran the first one for every error, so `except Brk: break` / `except Cont: continue` broke out of the loop on a continue. Each clause now catches only its classes (and their subclasses, `class NotFound(AppError)`), an error no clause names goes on up, and a bare `raise` in a handler raises the same error again. A failed built-in call is caught by any built-in error name.
- Python values without one fixed type are dynamic (JSON) values, as in the huge set's minilang: an untyped parameter called with different kinds, a function that returns different kinds by path, a name set to tuples of different shapes, `type(c) in (list, str)` and `type(a) is type(b)`, `int(s)` / `float(s)` on such a value (a bad string raises ValueError), and `w.rooms if c else w.items` used in `x in d`. A list typed from its first items that later gets mixed items is returned as a mixed list.
- Reading and writing files in Python files: `open(p).read()`, `open(p).readlines()`, `for line in open(p)`, `with open(p) as f:` (also several at once, with `encoding=`), `f = open(p)` ... `f.close()`, and writing with `open(p, "w")` or `"a"` through `f.write(s)` and `print(..., file=f)`. A missing file raises an error that `except FileNotFoundError:` catches. `s.splitlines(True)` keeps the line ends. A `.py` file always gets Python rules, even when nothing in it looks like Python.
- The last silent difference in the parity probe is gone: `[True, None]` printed `[True, False]`; such a list is now held as mixed values and prints as Python does. In Python files, the str + int error suggests an f-string (`f"...{x}"`), not Brevi's `"{x}"`.
- brevi-lang.org leads with speed and safety: write plain Python, get a native binary (1.3x to 10.8x faster than CPython on the speed set, measured 2 Nov 2026), with whole-program checks before running. The old "up to 21% fewer tokens" claim is gone (it came from the earlier code-and-errors count; end to end, plain Python built by Brevi costs about what CPython does). llms.txt points agents to the skill first and marks the guide as .bv-only.
- `brevi run x.py` runs the file with python3 when Brevi cannot build it, and says so in one line on stderr (`note: ran with python3; Brevi could not build line 5: unknown method .permutations()`). Plain Python always runs; when Brevi can build it, it runs at native speed. `--native` (or `BREVI_NATIVE=1`) keeps the strict behaviour, and `brevi build` and `brevi check` stay strict. `BREVI_PYTHON` picks the interpreter. The benchmark's `pyb` check does the same and logs whether each task built natively (`--native` for the old strict check).
- From the e20 trial: a stub that only returns None (`def parse_number_key(line): return None`); `q.update(type='choice', letter=x)` on a dict used as a record (one key set per argument); a record field that starts as `0` and has Decimal amounts added (`state['deb'] += a`) holds a Decimal; `dict(base, type='choice', answer=a)` with base a dict set once from plain names (each shape is its own record); `sorted(q['answer'])` on a mixed record's set or list value. From e21: a list built with `counters = counters[:d]`, `counters += [0] * n`, `counters[d - 1] += 1` is no longer taken for a dict; a top-level list of tuples with a slot that is only ever None (`players.append((name, sc, None))`), filled through `global players`. Type checking a function with many empty lists and dicts no longer takes time that doubles with each one (a 364-line solution took 90 s to check, now 5 s).
- Huge set: a function that returns tuples of different lengths by path (`('room', name, desc)`, `('capacity', n)` or None), with plain int, str, bool or None items, now works: callers read `c[0]`, `c[3]`, `len(c)`, print it as Python does, and add its whole-number items to an int (anything else stops the run). Tuples with a float item are still refused, since a float could print as an int.
- More of the huge set: `(p[1], p[3]) + tuple(p[5:])` joins a tuple with a list (the result prints as a tuple); `d = rooms if kind == 'room' else items` with two dicts of different value types, when d is only used in `x in d` / `x not in d`; `up = (lambda s: s.upper()) if by == 'w' else (lambda s: s)`; a function whose body is only `pass`. Fixed wrong output: printing the result of a Python function that gives no value now shows `None`, not `()`.
- `next(gen, None)` gives None itself when nothing matches, so `x is None` is true afterwards (it gave `""` or 0, and a later `is not None` test went the wrong way: the e20 Haiku poker attempt). `Decimal.scaleb(k)`, `int(s, base)` (with `0x`/`0o`/`0b` prefixes and base 0) and `sys.stdin.readline()`. "Did you mean" hints only name a near miss now: `sys.stdin.readline()` was told "did you mean .re_find()?" and `int(s, 16)` "did you mean input()?". The `brevi mcp` guide tool now says plain Python needs no guide first.
- Python results that silently differed now match CPython: `-7 % 3` is 2 and `divmod` / float `%` follow the sign of the divisor; `round(2.5)` is 2 (ties go to even); None in a float list prints `None`; a set prints as `{1, 2}` (or `set()`); `math.isqrt(n)` is the whole square root (the auto-fix used to swap it for `sqrt`). Reading a missing key of a plain dict raises KeyError, as in Python; a `defaultdict` or `Counter` still reads zero. `range(a, b, k)` in a loop is counted through directly, so a sieve runs about 9x faster than CPython instead of 3x slower.
- Huge set: types that are only partly known (`state = (board, 'w', None)` from one function, the None slot filled by another) now flow between functions, so the known parts type the parameters they reach. A class with its own `copy()` method gets that method instead of the list copy. A constructor parameter passed None, whose field is set to an int later, takes the field's type. `P, N, K = "PNK"` unpacks a string; `c in (R, Q)` tests a tuple of like items; a function that returns a value on some paths and can run off its end returns None there. Haiku's h1 chess builds and passes 10/10. `dict(v)` and `v.copy()` on a dict record give a new record with the same values.
- Silent wrong output from the parity probe is now refused with a fix: `b = a` on a plain list or dict followed by a change through one name and a read of the other (Brevi copies; Python shares), `[[0] * n] * m` (Python rows are one shared list), and a mutable default argument (`acc=[]`) that the function changes.
- `'%d: %s' % e` with the tuple in a name (or a field or item), as in Sonnet's h2 adventure world file.
- `json.dumps(x)` in Python files, with `indent=`, `separators=`, `sort_keys=` and `ensure_ascii=`, writes what Python's json module writes. `d.get(k, default)` and `k in d` work on JSON read with `json.loads`, also with a key that is itself a JSON value. From the saved-solutions sweep (16 solutions stopped at `json.dumps`).
- Also from e19: Python's one-item box (`cur = [None]`, read and set only as `cur[0]`, also inside nested defs) is a plain name the nested defs share; a dict record field that starts as `[None] * 9` gets its type from what is put in it later (the g2 tic-tac-toe). Fixed wrong output: `(gone if old else keep).append(l)` now appends to the list the condition picks instead of to a copy.
- The agent skill (`skills/brevi/SKILL.md`, also in the Claude Code plugin) now tells agents to write plain Python and run it with `brevi run main.py`, with no guide to read first; Brevi syntax and the guide are for `.bv` files only. Same-day runs on the xl3 set (r1): writing Brevi cost $0.270 (Sonnet) and $0.046 (Haiku), plain Python built by Brevi $0.190 and $0.028.
- `tools/parity_probe.py` runs short plain-Python probes (`tools/parity/probes.py`) with CPython and with `brevi run` and lists each one where Brevi prints something else (silent wrong answers) or refuses what CPython runs. First run: 17 silent differences and 24 refusals in 71 probes (missing dict keys, negative `%`, `round`, None in printed lists, list aliasing, mutable default arguments).
- Regex match positions: `m.start()`, `m.end(n)`, `m.span()`, and `re.finditer(p, s)` / `pat.finditer(s)`; a bracketed import over several lines (`from grade import (a, b,` then `c)`). From the e19 trial.
- From the e19 trial: a lambda given a name and only ever called (`bad = lambda n: out.append(...)` inside a function, or `sq = lambda x: x * x` at the top) is a nested or top-level `def`, so its parameters are typed from the calls and it can change the outer function's lists. A top-level name that starts as None and is later set to a tuple of empty lists and sets (`cur = ([], set(), set())`, filled through `cur[0].append(x)`, `cur[1].add(n)`, `cur[2].update(xs)`) takes its type from those uses, also when functions read it through `global cur`.
- A comprehension split over lines inside braces (`{k: f(q)` then `for q in qs}` on the next line, or an `if` on its own line) parses, as in Python. From Haiku's e18 quiz first attempt.
- `xs.count(x)` on a list (it used to need a closure) and `min(xs, default=d)` / `max(xs, default=d)`, also with `key=f`, from the e18 trial.
- `date(*map(int, s.split("-")))` and `datetime.date(*parts)`: a date from spread year, month and day.
- tkinter apps built around a class work: a method as a callback (`command=self.inc`, `canvas.bind("<Button-1>", self.tap)`, `root.after(100, self.tick, n - 1)`) runs on the object itself, now that Python objects are shared. Haiku's tic-tac-toe from the `gui` set passes every test.
- `itertools.groupby(xs, key=f)` (runs of neighbouring items with the same key) and `del xs[a:]` / `del xs[a:b]`, from the e17 trial.
- `repr(x)` (Python's text for a value, e.g. `"a'b"` or `[1, 2]`) and `iter(xs)` (so `next(iter(s))` takes the first item).
- From the huge set: a function whose tuple has a None slot (`return ([1, 2], 'w', None)`) takes that slot's type from the other function whose result goes in the same name (`s = new_state()`, then `s = step(s, k)`); a tuple that is still None prints `None`; a list set to `[]` in one branch and to a comprehension in the other takes the comprehension's type; and `type(v) is int` / `type(v) == bool` test a value's exact kind. Fixed wrong output: a list taken with `dq = lots.get(sku, deque())` and changed by a helper (`take(dq, q)` that pops lots) now changes the stored list. 409 of 580 saved Python solutions pass `brevi check` unchanged, 343 build, and 342 of those give the right output on every test.
- Python lists of mixed kinds used as rows (`backs.append([oid, sku, qty])`) and dicts made records are now shared too, like objects: a row changed through a filtered copy, a picked item (`max(rows, key=...)`) or another name changes everywhere. The "copy, not shared" errors now come only when the value really is a copy (a list of plain numbers or strings, a dict used as a dict); the Rust compiler checks the value's type at each such place. Haiku's GUI to-do app and the e17 warehouse first attempts that changed shared rows or objects now build. Fixed wrong output (pre-existing): `top = max(grid, key=...)` then `top[1] = 0` on a list of int lists is now refused instead of silently changing a copy.
- Python objects are now shared, as in Python: an object made from a class is a handle to data that lives for the whole run, so every name, list slot, dict value and field holding it sees the same object (`alias = st.lots[0]; alias.qty += 10`, `for l in st.for_sku("a"): l.qty -= 1`, `drain(st.last, 2)`). A None object is a null handle, and reading a field of one stops with Python's `'NoneType' object has no attribute` error. Lists and dicts are still copied when they are passed or stored. A method call that passes an object and reads its fields as arguments (`self.set_shown(self.shown + k)`) builds. A local named after a field (`t = self.tx`, then `t['postings'].append(p)` or `self.discard(t)`) now changes the field too, or is refused when later lines also set or use that field. Fixed wrong output: Haiku's z2 books solution, which built for the first time with this change. A class named like a Python built-in (`class Counter`) is the class, not `collections.Counter`. 410 of 580 saved Python solutions pass `brevi check` unchanged, 345 build, and 344 of those give the right output on every test.
- A Python list of mixed literal kinds that is looped over (`vals = [1, 'a', True, None, [2, 'b']]`, `for v in vals: show(v)`) is a list of JSON values, so a helper taking each item with `type(v) is int` / `isinstance(v, list)` checks builds (huge-set repro r2).
- From the GUI set's tkinter apps: a list of None filled in later (`board = [None] * 9`, then `board[c] = p` in a function); walking a tuple of one type like a list (`all(board[i] == p for i in line)`, `for i in line`, `i in line`, `sum(line)`); `d.update(k=v, ...)` and `d.update({...})` on a dict used as a record. Fixed wrong output: None inside a printed list of strings printed as `' None'`.
- Fixed wrong output: a Python list that mixes kinds (`[oid, sku, n]`) now prints as a list, not a tuple (unless the program also has real tuples of exactly those types). Changing an item of a list a helper returned as a filtered copy of another list (`vis = visible_tasks()`, then `vis[i][1] = ...`) is now refused rather than silently changing a copy; Python would change the shared item. 410 of 580 saved Python solutions pass `brevi check` unchanged, 344 build, and 343 of those give the right output on every test.
- More plain Python from the e17 trial's first attempts: a field filled as a map of maps (`self.scores[name][qid] = score`); a dict key set later to values of different kinds in different branches (`q['spec'] = t[2]`, `set(t[2])` or a list) holds any of them; dicts a function returns from different branches (`return {"Name": name, "N": n, "big": True}`) are one record, also when some values are parameters; and capitalised keys. Now refused instead of guessed: a dict name set both to None and to `{}` and then tested with `is None`, since Brevi can't tell those apart yet. Every e15, e16 and e17 first attempt that builds prints what CPython prints.
- More plain Python from the e16 trial's first attempts: `if obj:` on a class instance (true unless None, or by `__bool__`/`__len__`) and on a date; a dict that starts with a None value next to numbers (`state = {"accepted": 0, "last_date": None}`) or with only empty collections (`{"players": [], "names": set()}`) becomes a record whose keys get their own types; dict values from calls (`{'name': n, 'total': total(xs)}`) type as records; a class method named like a built-in (`stock.take(sku, n)`); `for (s, y) in d` over tuple keys; `all(xs[i] for i in ...)` by truth; and `out = print`. Fixed wrong output: a loop over a field that changes its items while also changing other fields of the object (`for b in self.backs: b[2] -= got; self.shipped += got`) now changes the stored items, a method that only changes the items of its object's list now changes the object, and a None object is told apart from one whose fields are all zero. Changing the items of a sorted or filtered local copy (`cand = sorted(l for l in self.lots if ...)`, then `for l in cand: l[1] -= n`) changes the originals, or is an error where Brevi can't do that yet. Built e16 first attempts now all print what CPython prints.
- Python dicts made records can get keys later in branches (`q = {"id": qid, "type": typ}`, then `q["answer"] = a` for one type and `q["value"] = Fraction(v)` for another); such a key prints only once set, and `'answer' in q` on one is refused rather than guessed. A helper that returns a filtered list is now written back also when called with an item (`for b in od.waiting(w[1]): b[2] -= got`). The e15 Haiku quiz first attempts (both runs) now build and print what Python prints, and Sonnet's e15a warehouse passes every test.
- `brevi build --window` (and `run --window`) builds a tkinter program with a real window, drawn by egui through the `eframe` crate (downloaded and compiled once, about a minute, then cached). Without a display, or with `BREVI_GUI=headless`, the same binary runs headless, so tests still work.
- Python tkinter programs now build. Brevi compiles the widgets, `StringVar`/`IntVar`/`BooleanVar`/`DoubleVar`, `pack`/`grid`/`place`, `command=` callbacks (function names, lambdas, `lambda ch=ch: ...` in a loop), `bind` with the event (`e.char`, `e.keysym`, `e.x`), `after`/`after_cancel` (also `after(ms, f, x)`), canvas items, listboxes and `messagebox` dialogs onto the `brevi_tk` runtime, which is built into the program (no crate to download). Built programs run headless for now: `mainloop` reads test actions from stdin and prints the window, exactly as the CPython stand-in does. Programs written with top-level functions work (Sonnet's to-do app in the `gui` set passes every test); programs built around a class (`self.on_click` as a callback) wait for Python object sharing. A lambda's default values (`lambda k=k: ...`) also work outside tkinter. Other GUI libraries (`pygame`, `PyQt`, ...) still give a clear "not supported" error.
- GUI benchmark set `gui` (`bench/ref/gen_gui.py`): a to-do app, a calculator and tic-tac-toe in tkinter, tested headlessly. `bench/tkshim` is a headless tkinter stand-in for CPython that prints the window the same way as Brevi's GUI runtime (`tools/gui_shim_check.sh`). Plain Python passes all three first try with Sonnet and Haiku; Brevi cannot build tkinter programs yet.
- String literals written next to each other are joined, as in Python, also across lines inside brackets: `("abc "\n "def")`, `f"{n} " f"items"`.
- New held-out benchmark set `huge` (`bench/ref/gen_huge.py`): an interpreter, a chess referee and a text adventure, 200 to 750 lines over 3 or 4 files. First run `h1`: plain Python passes 3/3 with Sonnet and Haiku; Brevi-built Python 2/3 with Sonnet (2.1x the cost) and 0/3 with Haiku. See [bench/README.md](bench/README.md).
- Python programs split over several `.py` files now build: `import stock` and `from shop.store import Store` find `stock.py` or `shop/store.py` (next to the importing file, then next to the main file, as Python does; folders with `__init__.py` are packages). A module's own `if __name__ == "__main__":` self-test is left out when another file imports it. Importing a GUI library (`tkinter`, `pygame`, `PyQt`, ...) now says plainly that Brevi has no GUI support yet, instead of a confusing type error.
- More plain Python from the e14 trial's first attempts: a lambda called at once (`(lambda s: (s[0], s[1]))(score(t))`); rows of a mixed list changed in a loop (`backorders.append([oid, sku, left])`, then `for b in backorders: b[2] -= got`); dicts built from a method's parameters (`self.lots.append({"seq": n, "sku": sku, "qty": qty})`) and dicts with a key that is a keyword (`'type'`) become records; and a loop that changes the dicts a helper returns after filtering and sorting them (`lots = [l for l in self.lots if ...]`, `lots.sort(key=...)`, `return lots`) changes the stored dicts, as in Python. The e14 Sonnet poker and warehouse and Haiku warehouse first attempts now pass every test, and Haiku's quiz builds and prints what Python prints.
- More plain Python from the e15 trial's first attempts: dicts built from local names and filled in key by key (`cur = {...}` then `cur["k"] = v`) become records, including keys that are keywords; a dict whose keys are set in a loop (`memo[k] = v`) is typed as a dict, not a list; `re.I` / `re.IGNORECASE` flags; `math.floor`/`ceil` on decimals; a lambda's nested tuple unpacking (`for (a, (b, c)) in ...`); and a helper that filters a local copy of a list and returns it writes changes back. Wrong output turned into errors: a loop at the end of a function that changes copies, removing items from a list while looping over its indexes, and a list that may be None or an empty list (`[] if ok else None` then `is None`), which Brevi can't yet tell apart. The e15 Sonnet poker and quiz, Haiku warehouse and Haiku trains first attempts now pass every test. Fixed wrong output: `h, a = teams[home], teams[away]` followed by `h['P'] += 1` now changes the stored dicts (Haiku's y2 league). 409 of 580 saved Python solutions pass `brevi check` unchanged, 342 build, and 341 of those give the right output on every test.

## v0.19.0 (1 Nov 2026)

Brevi now aims to build plain Python unchanged ("Python in, Rust speed out"). The release test is a plain-Python trial: models write ordinary Python, Brevi builds it, and the full API cost is compared with running the same models' Python on CPython. In run e14 Brevi costs 1.16x Python with Sonnet and 0.94x with Haiku, from 2.2x and 1.8x this morning. 331 of 580 saved Python solutions build and give the right output unchanged. The release test is not passed yet.

- From the e14 retries: `{d:+.2f}` keeps the plus sign on a Decimal, Python's `string` constants work (`string.digits`, `from string import ascii_lowercase`, and the same for uppercase, letters, hexdigits and octdigits), and JSON values can be dict keys and set items.
- The release test is now the plain-Python trial (`e2e.py --lang pyb` against `--lang python`, same day, Sonnet and Haiku, two runs). It passes when Brevi-built Python costs no more than CPython and passes as many tasks. See [bench/README.md](bench/README.md#release-test-plain-python-since-1-nov-2026).
- Plain-Python trial `e14` (model writes .py, Brevi builds it): Sonnet 1.16x the cost of plain Python (2.2x in e10 this morning), Haiku 0.94x. See [bench/README.md](bench/README.md).
- Python values whose type differs from row to row now work. This is the quiz pattern: a question's answer key is a letter, a set of letters, a `(value, tolerance)` pair of decimals or a list of words, depending on the question type. Such a value holds JSON, whether it is a slot of the returned tuples (also nested, `return qid, ('multi', pts, frozenset(s))`), a class field given different types by different calls (`Question(qid, kind, pts, data)`), or a name set to different types in the branches of an if (`spec = parts[3]` / `spec = frozenset(s)` / `spec = (Decimal(v), tol)`). Where such a value meets a decimal or a list it is read as one: `abs(v - spec[0]) <= spec[1]`, `s == spec`, `s & spec`, `ans in spec`, `value, tol = q.data`. Also `a <= b` / `a < b` on sets as a subset test, `all(words)` / `any(xs)` on strings and numbers (by truth), `\Z` and `\A` in regexes, fields named `type` (`q.type`), `Decimal(a) / Decimal(b)`, `Fraction(Decimal(s))`, `s.casefold()`, `order, questions = [], {}`, `a, b = s.split("=", 1)` inside a loop, and `best = None` with 5- and 6-tuples. Fixed wrong output: `dq = lots.get(sku, deque())` (or `[]`) followed by changes to dq now changes the stored list, as in Python. Every quiz first attempt from the e11, e12 and e13 trials now builds and prints what Python prints. 404 of 580 saved Python solutions pass `brevi check` unchanged, 334 build, and 331 of those give the right output on every test.

- Python import aliases: `from datetime import date as Date`, `import datetime as dt`, `from collections import Counter as C` (for modules Brevi has built in). `datetime.timedelta(...)` works like `timedelta(...)`, and a date that is still None prints `None` in Python files. Together these fix Haiku's failed books task in e13.
- Clearer build error when rustc can't work out a variable's type: it names the variable and says whether it starts as None or as an empty collection, instead of the general "type annotations needed". This rustc error is no longer shown when a more specific one is also reported. Run `e13` of the plain-Python trial is in [bench/README.md](bench/README.md).
- More Python classes from the e12 trial: `self.lots.setdefault(sku, []).append(lot)` types the field as a dict of lists, `for k in list(self.lots):` goes over the dict's keys, a loop that changes the items a small helper method returns (`for lot in self._ordered(sku):` with `return sorted(self.lots.get(sku, []), key=...)`) changes the stored items, `dealt.update(cards)` types a set from a list, and `st = stats[k]` / `dd = nxt.setdefault(k, {})` followed by `st[0] += q` or `dd[t] = x` types the entry, and a name set on some passes of a loop keeps its value into the next pass (`if key != prev: pos = i + 1` then `print(pos)`; this gave wrong ranks before), and `if best:` works on a tuple that started as `best = None`. The e12 warehouse and trains first attempts (and e11 sonnet trains) now pass every test. 403 of 580 saved Python solutions pass `brevi check` unchanged, 327 build, and 324 of those give the right output on every test.

- Python dicts used as records (`tx = {'n': n, 'date': d, 'posts': [], 'bad': False}`: fixed keys, values of different types) become record types, the way a class does. `tx['posts'].append(p)`, `tx['bad'] = True`, `tx.get('k', d)` and `'k' in tx` work; dicts put in the same name with fewer keys (`cur = {'silent': True}`) join the record; records print as the dict and go into JSON as an object. Also: a class's fields filled from outside the class (`shelf.items.append(x)` on a top-level object), a helper that returns an item of a dict (`st = stat(sku)` with `return stats[sku]`) changes the stored item, and `m = months.setdefault(date[:7], [0, 0])` with a computed key writes back. The books first attempts from the e11 and e12 trials now pass every test. 398 of 580 saved Python solutions pass `brevi check` unchanged, 318 build, and 315 of those give the right output on every test.
- Python loops that change the items of a sorted or copied list (`for lot in sorted(lots, key=...)`, `for b in list(backorders)` that may also `backorders.remove(b)`) change the list's own items, as in Python: the loop goes over indexes and each change is written back. Records written as lists of mixed types (`lots.append([expiry, seq, sku, qty])`) are tuples with settable slots (`lot[3] -= n`), Python's `xs.remove(x)` takes out the first item equal to x, and `c = d.setdefault(row[2], [0, 0])` then `c[0] += n` changes the stored list.
- Python lists that hold values of more than one type (a tokenizer's `tokens.append(int(s[i:j]))` next to `tokens.append(c)`) hold JSON values, and arithmetic, comparisons, `abs`, `//`, unary minus and `in` work on them as in Python; JSON values print as Python prints them (`[1, '+', 2]`, `None`, `True`). Also `f'{"#" * n}'` (a value starting with a quote inside a single-quoted f-string) and `\` at the end of a line to continue it. 309 of 580 saved Python solutions now build unchanged, and 305 of those give the right output on every test.
- Python code can set a name again to a value of another type in the block that made it (`w = int(w)` on a loop name, `st = int(st)` after `ip, st, path = m.groups()`): from there on the name holds the new type, as in Python. Also unpacking a list into up to 12 names (`ip, ts, meth, path, proto, st, size, ref, ua = m.groups()`), and `dist.get(v, INF)` with `INF = float('inf')` on a map of ints. 292 of 580 saved Python solutions now build unchanged (260 before).
- More Python: `frozenset(xs)`, `m.pop(k, default)`, `s - k` on sets made with `set(...)` in a function whose types are only known later, `x = None` for 4-tuples, and dates inside a mixed-type dict.
- Python's unlimited ints: in a Python-style program, a number read with `int(text)` that is only added, subtracted, multiplied, compared and printed becomes a big integer, so `a, b = int(p[0]), int(p[1])` / `print(a + b)` works for 60-digit numbers (all 24 saved `bigadd` Python solutions now give the right output; they hit a runtime error before). Any other use keeps it a fast int. Also `re.split` with groups gives the groups' text too, `x = m.setdefault(k, [0, 0])` shares the stored value as in Python, and Decimals inside lists and dicts print as `Decimal('1.50')`. With the parser work, 247 of 251 built Python solutions now give the right output on every test (`py_as_brevi.py --full --run`).
- A function that returns a JSON value on some paths and a number on others (`if isinstance(x, (int, float)): return x` ... `return 0`) now returns JSON, and `sum(f(v) for v in xs)` over such results works, so the usual recursive JSON-walking code builds unchanged.
- Python code that relies on shared objects no longer gives wrong output silently. `for a in m.values()`, `for k, a in m.items()` and `for i, x in enumerate(xs)` that change the item now change the original. Where a change would still be lost (changing items of `sorted(lots)` in a loop, changing a list after putting it in another, changing an item picked by `max(...)`), `brevi check` refuses the program and says how to write it. Also fixed wrong output from plain Python: `range(a, b, -1)` counted nothing, `if x:` was true for a None number or string, `(x,)` was taken as x, `m.setdefault(k, 0)` alone on a line did nothing, and `/` inside an f-string's `{...}` was whole-number division. `bench/py_as_brevi.py --full --run` now also runs each built solution on its task's tests: 224 of 248 built solutions give the right output on every test (188 of 247 before these fixes); most of the rest need Python's unbounded ints (`int("123456789012345678901234567890")`), which stop with an error rather than a wrong answer.
- More Python from the plain-Python trials: module-level values built by comprehensions or calls (`R = {c: i for i, c in enumerate(s)}`, `dict(pairs)`, `[(c, 1) for c in s]`) can be read inside functions; nested functions that call each other (`expr()` calling `peek()`) pass along what the callee reads from the outer function; `def __len__` is what `len(x)` calls and `def __str__` / `__repr__` is how an object prints; a parameter is typed from calls inside generators (`all(valid(c) for c in hand)`); a loop name that is still unknown no longer hides an earlier setting of the same name; `tuple(xs)` works (kept as a list); and `m.get(k)` on a map of maps or lists gives an empty value when k is missing.
- Python names share objects, and Python-style programs now follow that: `r = rows[k]` or `r = resting.get(rid)` followed by `r[0] -= t`, or `acct = accts[name]` followed by `acct.bal += 5` / `acct.deposit(5)`, changes what the container holds. Before, the change was silently lost, giving wrong output. Brevi-style programs keep their copies. Also nested unpacking (`(a, b), c = ...`, `for bid, (r, d) in m.items()` in comprehensions), Brevi keywords used as Python names (`def var(...)`, `match = ...`, `type = ...`), lists filled by `heapq.heappush` typed inside nested functions and changed there (`heappop` in a nested function changes the outer heap), `x.quantize(...)` typed as a decimal, and printing of 4- and 5-tuples.
- Python code that reads JSON works more often unchanged: `json.loads(s)` and `json.load(sys.stdin)`, `isinstance(v, int)` and `isinstance(v, (str, list))` (on a JSON value it checks what the value holds; on other values it follows the type, with bool counting as int as in Python), `v is None`, `v == 3` and `for x in v` / `sum(f(x) for x in v)` on a JSON value. 355 of 580 saved Python solutions now pass `brevi check --fast` unchanged (334 before). A function that returns a JSON value on some paths and a number on others is not supported yet. Also `x = None` that is later a Decimal (prints `None` until set), and `Fraction` as an annotation.
- More Python from the plain-Python trials: `for ... else:` and `while ... else:`, `lots[:] = [...]` (the caller's list changes), `not 1 <= mo <= 12`, `y, mo, d = (int(x) for x in s.split("-"))`, a docstring at the top of an imported file, quoted and loose annotations (`-> "int | None"`, `-> object`, `c: list = [0] * 3`), `pl = pd = None` with one name set later, tuple constants used as lists (`SIZES = ("S", "M")`, `for s in ("a", "b"):`), `()` as an empty list, lambdas that unpack rows (`key=lambda r: r[1]` on lists of lists), and Python printing for scripts with no `def`. A file defining the same function twice no longer makes `brevi check` hang.
- More Python from the guide trials: `nonlocal x` in a nested function changes the outer function's x; `m.group(3) or ""` and other `a or b` / `a and b` on values that are not bools give a value, as in Python; a parameter a function never reads (`stops: list` passed along) is dropped instead of failing to type; `for k in m:` over a dict reads its keys; and a method of a class from another file, or with a built-in name (`board.add(name, row)`, `stock.take(sku, n)`), is called once the value's class is known, with its changes kept. `p[1]` on pairs from a top-level list filled under `global` works inside functions.
- More Python: `d.to_integral_value()` on a Decimal (half to even), `m.setdefault(k, v)` as a statement with any default, `defaultdict(lambda: 0)` (and other empty defaults), `enumerate(xs, start=1)`, `bool(x)`, and `sys.setrecursionlimit(n)` (accepted; native code needs no limit). 334 of 580 saved Python solutions now pass `brevi check --fast` unchanged (321 before).
- More of Python's library: `s.update(xs)` on a set, `m.update(other)` on a dict and `c.update(xs)` on a Counter; `datetime.date(y, m, d)`, `datetime.strptime(s, "%Y-%m-%d")` (`.date()` gives the date), `timedelta(days=n, weeks=w)` added to a date, `d.isoformat()`, `d.toordinal()`, `d.weekday()` (0 = Monday in Python-style files) and `d.isoweekday()`; `calendar.monthrange(y, m)` and `calendar.isleap(y)`; `format(x, spec)`; `float(d)` on a Decimal; `s.isascii()`; and `Fraction(s)` / `Fraction(a, b)` as exact decimals (printed as decimals, not `3/10`). These were the library calls behind failed builds in the new `e2e.py --lang pyb` trial, where the model writes plain Python files and Brevi builds them.
- More Python: `s.ljust(n)`, `s.rjust(n, c)`, `s.split(sep, n)`, `s.rsplit(sep, n)`, `list(s)`, plus `set(s)` and `s.count(x)` on a string whose type only Rust knows, and `a - b` on sets.
- Python type annotations are accepted wherever Python allows them and used where they help: `self.names: list[str] = []` and `self.best: Optional[Lot] = None` on fields, `best: int` with no value, `list[tuple]` and other part-typed annotations (left to inference), `Decimal`, `Union[...]`, `int | str`, `Callable[...]`, `Iterator[...]`. Models write these all the time when they write Python, and they were the most common error left in the Python-first guide trial. Also `x in ("a", "b")`, `m.get(k)` (None when missing), `sum(xs, start)`, and None printing as `None`.
- Faster Python library calls in native builds: `for row in csv.reader(src)` reads rows one at a time with a faster parser (2M rows: 2.8 s and 514 MB before, 0.47 s and 36 MB now; CPython 0.87 s), `Counter(s.split())` counts without building the word list (9.6M words: 1.3 s to 0.32 s; CPython 1.4 s), and regexes match runs like `\S+` in a loop and `re.match` tries only the start (300k lines: 0.79 s to 0.45 s; CPython 0.37 s). `csv.reader` gives `[]` for a blank line, as Python does.
- More Python: `xs.clear()` and `m.clear()`, `sys.argv`, `sorted(m, key=f)` on a dict, and `date(y, m, d)`.
- Python programs split over several files work unchanged: `import stock` / `from stock import Stock, parse` reads `stock.bv` next to the file (`lib.stock` is `lib/stock.bv`), `stock.parse(x)` and `import stock as s` calls work, and a used file may hold `def`, `class`, decorators, imports and module-level constants. This was the main cost of the multi-file tasks in the Python-first guide trial.
- Generators: `yield x` and `yield from xs` (the function collects what it yields, so `for v in f()`, `list(f())` and `sum(f())` work; an endless generator is not supported). `f"{3.14159:.2f}"` and other format specs on a number written in place, `"-" * 40`, `None` for a date, and `try: return f(s)` / `except ...: return None` at a function's end.
- More Python classes and calls work unchanged: `@dataclass` classes (fields from `name: str` lines, with defaults and `field(default_factory=list)`), inheritance (`class Bird(Animal):` gets Animal's fields and methods, and `super().__init__(...)` runs Animal's), Python type annotations (`list[str]`, `dict[str, int]`, `Optional[T]`, `int | None`, `List`/`Dict` from typing), default and keyword arguments (`def scale(x, k=2)`, `scale(5, k=3)`), `*args`, and a class's own method named like a built-in one (`stock.add(4)` next to `seen.add(3)`). In Python-style programs `/` always gives a float (`7 / 2` is 3.5) and an int mixed with a float becomes a float, as in Python. Also `a, b = line.split()` inside a function, `dict(m)` and `Bird("x").describe()`.
- Python classes work unchanged: `class Account:` with `__init__` and methods becomes a type with the fields `self.x = ...` sets, typed from what is put there; `Account("ann", 10)` and `a.deposit(5)` call them. Error classes (`class BadInput(ValueError):`) may have a body. Also `"""docstrings"""` and other triple-quoted strings, `a ** b`, `0xFF`/`0o17`/`0b101`, `fn` as a name, and `st[-1], st[-2] = st[-2], st[-1]`.
- More plain Python is accepted unchanged: `try`/`except`/`else`/`finally` and `raise` (a failing call or `raise` inside `try` runs the handler, with `as e` giving the message), error classes such as `class BadInput(Exception): pass`, `pass`, nested `def` (lifted out, with the names it reads passed along), `;` between statements (also in `if c: a; b`), import lines anywhere, `def main()` with `if __name__ == "__main__":`, raw strings, `return a, b`, `a = b = 0`, `h, m = s.split(":")` from a list, `print(*xs)`, dict and set comprehensions, `{a, b}` sets with `|` and `&`, `<<`, `>>`, `^`, `|=` and the other compound operators, `x is y`, and comprehensions over a dict's keys. With the library batches (#32, #33, #34), 217 of 580 saved Python solutions pass `brevi check --fast` (9.7% before), and 134 (23.1%) build fully. With the class, import and generator batches below, 282 (48.6%) pass and 166 (28.6%) build.
- Types are optional everywhere. A parameter without a type now also takes the type its own function's uses show (`lots.push(Lot(...))`, `seen[id] = true`, `st[t]`, `p.qty`), and `date(s).days` and `weekday()` are typed. With every annotation removed from the 48 saved held-out XL solutions, 46 still build and pass (31 before). The guide now says so and its examples drop their types.
- A parameter changed in place (`xs.push(x)`, `xs[i] = v`, `p.n += 1`) edits the caller's value without `mut`, as in Python. It stays a copy when the function sets it whole or returns it. Leaving out `mut` used to give silently wrong output.
- `global x` and `nonlocal x` are accepted. A program that uses them follows Python's scope rule exactly; programs without them keep Brevi's rule.
- Python's `f"..."` and `f'...'` strings are accepted (Brevi strings already fill in `{name}`).
- A program's own type named `Result` no longer clashes with Rust's.
- A top-level map filled inside a function (`ts = {}`, then `ts[n] = Team()` in a function) is typed where other functions read it, so `fn key(n: str) = (-ts[n].w, n)` needs no return type. This was the most common first-check error in recent runs.
- `inner = m.get_or(k, {})` is typed by what is done with it, like a fresh `{}`, so nested maps built this way at the top level work inside functions.
- A function ending in `if c: m.remove(k) else: print(...)` gives no value, as in Python.
- Functions are values: `ops = [add, mul]`, pairs such as `("+", add)`, and calls straight from a list, tuple or field (`ops[0](3, 4)`).
- A third held-out set of six XL tasks (`bench/ref/gen_big5.py`, runs z1 and z2). Brevi is still behind Python: Sonnet 9,042 vs 7,990 tokens (13% more), Haiku 12,405 vs 11,394 (9% more), from 19% and 4% on the second set. Brevi passes first time more often with Haiku (10 of 12 vs 9 of 12), but its finished programs are no shorter than Python's at this size; type annotations are about 5% of Brevi's tokens.
- `s.index_of(sub)` and `s.index(sub)` on strings (-1 when missing).
- `None` for int, float, str and tuple values: `best = None`, `x is None`, `x is not None`, `return None`.
- Maps of functions: `ops = {"+": add, "-": lambda a, b: a - b}`, then `ops[op](a, b)` or `for k, f in ops.items()`.
- More Python habits: `set(xs)` and `s.add(x)`, `xs.extend(ys)`, `xs[::-1]`, `divmod`, `round(x)`/`round(x, n)`, `enumerate(xs, 1)`, `m.setdefault(k, [])`, `defaultdict(int)`, `"%d %s" % (a, b)`, `"{:>5}".format(x)`, `math.sqrt`, `sys.stdin`, `[0] * n`, `p.0.1`, and `import sys`/`from collections import ...` lines. These came from compile errors in earlier benchmark runs.
- Python 3.15 unpacking in comprehensions (`[*xs for xs in xss]`) reads as a comprehension followed by `flatten()` (#15). Rust 1.99 builds cleanly (#13).
- The core guide is trimmed from 5,035 to 3,490 tokens; rare details move to REFERENCE.md, served by `brevi guide WORD`, and CI fails when BREVI.md goes over 3,600 tokens (#18).
- Install channels prepared for launch, not yet published: Claude Code plugin and marketplace, MCP registry `server.json`, a PyPI wrapper, an npm wrapper (`brevi-lang`) and a dataset export of 49 tasks with 1,893 verified solutions (#20).
- Grammar for constrained decoding: `grammar/brevi.lark` and a llama.cpp copy, `grammar/brevi.gbnf`, which accept every test program and all 1,370 verified Brevi solutions. Examples for LangChain, the OpenAI Agents SDK, CrewAI and the Vercel AI SDK over `brevi mcp` (`integrations/`), and a Homebrew formula generator (#22).
- Safe to run: `allow read, write` (or `native`) at the top of a program declares what it touches, `brevi run --sandbox` refuses anything undeclared, and the MCP tools sandbox by default (`sandbox: false` turns it off).
- A second held-out set of six XL tasks (`bench/ref/gen_big4.py`). On it Brevi is behind Python: Sonnet 10,997 vs 9,222 tokens, Haiku 11,851 vs 11,426 (#19).
- Faster builds: the runtime prelude is compiled once per rustc version into a cached library instead of being rebuilt with every program (a build goes from about 0.73 s to 0.36 s, `brevi check` from 0.55 s to 0.21 s), and builds are cached by a hash of the generated code, flags and rustc version, so running the same program again takes about 0.1 s. Programs are built without debug info (5 MB to 1.1 MB). The compatibility check of 1,971 old solutions drops from about 6 to under 3 minutes. `BREVI_NO_CACHE=1` and `BREVI_NO_RTLIB=1` turn these off.
